And the level of pressure, like I say,

01:26:14 --> 01:26:17
like I've known every CEO of Twitter,

01:26:16 --> 01:26:19
they've all had the exact same experience,

01:26:18 --> 01:26:22
which when they were in the job, it was just daily beatings.

01:26:21 --> 01:26:23
Like it just getting punched

01:26:22 --> 01:26:25
in the face every single day, constantly.

01:26:24 --> 01:26:28
And, you know, Mark is very good

01:26:27 --> 01:26:30
at getting (laughs) physically punched in the face,

01:26:29 --> 01:26:31
and then- - Getting better and better.

01:26:30 --> 01:26:32
Yeah.

01:26:32 --> 01:26:35
- You know, and he's very good at, you know, taking a punch

01:26:34 --> 01:26:37
and he has taken many, many punches.

01:26:36 --> 01:26:39
So I would encourage people to have a level of sympathy

01:26:38 --> 01:26:40
for these are not kings,

01:26:39 --> 01:26:42
these are people who operate with like, I would say,

01:26:42 --> 01:26:45
extraordinary levels of external pressure.

01:26:44 --> 01:26:47
I think if I had been in his job for the last decade,

01:26:46 --> 01:26:50
I would be a little puddle on the floor.

01:26:49 --> 01:26:53
And so it says, I think a lot about him that he has,

01:26:52 --> 01:26:55
you know, risen to this occasion the way that he has.

01:26:54 --> 01:26:56
And by the way, I should also say, you know,

01:26:55 --> 01:26:57
the cynicism of course is immediately out

01:26:56 --> 01:27:00
and, you know, legitimate thing for people to say,

01:26:59 --> 01:27:01
but you know, it's like,

01:27:00 --> 01:27:02
"Oh, you're only doing this because of Trump,

01:27:01 --> 01:27:03
or, you know, whatever."

01:27:02 --> 01:27:06
And it's just like, no, like he has been thinking about

01:27:05 --> 01:27:07
and working on these things

01:27:06 --> 01:27:09
and trying to figure them out for a very long time.

01:27:08 --> 01:27:11
And so I think what you saw are legitimate,

01:27:10 --> 01:27:13
deeply held beliefs, not some, you know,

01:27:12 --> 01:27:14
sort of just in the moment thing

01:27:13 --> 01:27:16
that could change at any time.

01:27:15 --> 01:27:17
- So what do you think it's like to be him

01:27:16 --> 01:27:21
and other leaders of companies, to be you

01:27:20 --> 01:27:25
and withstand internal pressure and external pressure?

01:27:24 --> 01:27:26
What's that life like?

01:27:25 --> 01:27:28
Is it deeply lonely?

01:27:27 --> 01:27:29
- That's a great question.

01:27:28 --> 01:27:31
So leaders are lonely to start with.

01:27:30 --> 01:27:32
And this is one of those things

01:27:31 --> 01:27:34
where almost nobody has sympathy, right?

01:27:33 --> 01:27:36
Nobody feels sorry for a CEO, right?

01:27:35 --> 01:27:38
Like, it's not a thing, right?

01:27:37 --> 01:27:39
And again, legitimately so,

01:27:38 --> 01:27:41
like CEOs get paid a lot, like the whole thing.

01:27:40 --> 01:27:42
There's a lot of great things about it.

01:27:41 --> 01:27:44
So it's not like they should be out there asking

01:27:43 --> 01:27:44
for a lot of sympathy,

01:27:43 --> 01:27:47
but it is the case that they are human beings.

01:27:46 --> 01:27:48
And it is the case that it is a lonely job.

01:27:47 --> 01:27:50
And the reason it's a lonely job

01:27:49 --> 01:27:53
is because your words carry tremendous weight

01:27:53 --> 01:27:56
and you are dealing with extremely complicated issues,

01:27:55 --> 01:27:58
and you're under a tremendous amount of emotional, you know,

01:27:57 --> 01:28:00
personal, emotional stress.

01:27:59 --> 01:28:03
And, you know, you often end up not being able to sleep well

01:28:02 --> 01:28:04
and you end up not being able to like,

01:28:03 --> 01:28:06
keep up an exercise routine and all those things.

01:28:05 --> 01:28:08
And you know, you come under family stress

01:28:07 --> 01:28:09
'cause you're working all the time.

01:28:08 --> 01:28:11
Or my partner Ben, you know, he was CEO

01:28:10 --> 01:28:13
of our last company before we started the venture firm.

01:28:12 --> 01:28:15
He said, you know, the problem he had

01:28:14 --> 01:28:17
like with his family life was even when he was home

01:28:16 --> 01:28:19
at night, he wasn't home

01:28:18 --> 01:28:21
because he was in his head trying to solve

01:28:20 --> 01:28:22
all the business problems.

01:28:21 --> 01:28:23
And so he was like supposed to be like having dinner

01:28:22 --> 01:28:24
with his kids and he was physically there,

01:28:23 --> 01:28:25
but he wasn't mentally there.

01:28:24 --> 01:28:28
So, you know, you get that a lot.

01:28:27 --> 01:28:29
But the key thing is like, you can't talk to people, right?

01:28:28 --> 01:28:32
So I mean, you could talk to your spouse and your kids,

01:28:31 --> 01:28:33
but like, they don't understand

01:28:32 --> 01:28:34
that they're networking in your company.

01:28:33 --> 01:28:36
They don't understand, have the context to really help you.

01:28:36 --> 01:28:38
If you talk to your executives,

01:28:37 --> 01:28:40
they all have agendas, right?

01:28:41 --> 01:28:42
And they can't resist.

01:28:41 --> 01:28:44
Like it's just human nature.

01:28:43 --> 01:28:47
And so you can't necessarily rely on what they say.

01:28:46 --> 01:28:50
It's very hard in most companies to talk to your board

01:28:49 --> 01:28:51
because they can fire you.

01:28:52 --> 01:28:54
Now, Mark has the situation 'cause he has control,

01:28:53 --> 01:28:56
it actually turns out he can talk to his board.

01:28:55 --> 01:28:58
And Mark talks to us about many things that he does

01:28:57 --> 01:29:00
that most CEOs won't talk to their boards about

01:28:59 --> 01:29:03
because we literally, because we can't fire him.

01:29:02 --> 01:29:05
But a general, including all the CEOs of Twitter,

01:29:04 --> 01:29:06
that none of them had control

01:29:05 --> 01:29:08
and so they could all get fired.

01:29:07 --> 01:29:10
So you can't talk to the board members,

01:29:09 --> 01:29:12
they're gonna fire you, you can't talk to the shareholders

01:29:11 --> 01:29:15
'cause they'll just like dump your stock, right?

01:29:14 --> 01:29:18
Like, okay, so every once in a while,

01:29:17 --> 01:29:20
what you find is basically the best-case scenario they have

01:29:19 --> 01:29:21
is they can talk to other CEOs,

01:29:20 --> 01:29:23
and there's these little organizations

01:29:22 --> 01:29:24
where they kind of pair up and do that

01:29:23 --> 01:29:26
and so they maybe get a little bit outta that.

01:29:25 --> 01:29:27
But even that's fraught with peril

01:29:26 --> 01:29:30
'cause can you really talk about confidential information

01:29:29 --> 01:29:33
with another CEO, insider trading risk.

01:29:32 --> 01:29:35
And so it's just a very lonely isolating thing

01:29:34 --> 01:29:36
to start with.

01:29:37 --> 01:29:40
And then on top of that, you apply pressure, right?

01:29:39 --> 01:29:41
And that's where it gets painful.

01:29:40 --> 01:29:43
And then maybe I'll just spend a moment

01:29:42 --> 01:29:45
on this internal, external pressure thing.

01:29:45 --> 01:29:48
My general experience with companies

01:29:47 --> 01:29:52
is that they can withstand most forms of external pressure

01:29:51 --> 01:29:55
as long as they retain internal coherence.

01:29:54 --> 01:29:56
- Hmm. - Right.

01:29:55 --> 01:29:58
So as long as the internal team

01:29:57 --> 01:30:00
is really bonded together

01:29:59 --> 01:30:02
and supporting each other,

01:30:01 --> 01:30:05
most forms of external pressure you can withstand,

01:30:04 --> 01:30:07
and by that I mean investors dump your stock,

01:30:06 --> 01:30:10
you lose your biggest customers, you know,

01:30:09 --> 01:30:13
whatever negative article, you know, negative headline

01:30:12 --> 01:30:15
and you know, you can withstand all that.

01:30:14 --> 01:30:17
And basically, in fact, many of those forms of pressure

01:30:16 --> 01:30:20
can be bonding experiences for the team

01:30:19 --> 01:30:21
where they come out stronger.

01:30:20 --> 01:30:24
What you 100% cannot withstand is the internal crack.

01:30:24 --> 01:30:26
And what I always look for

01:30:25 --> 01:30:28
in high-pressure corporate situations now

01:30:27 --> 01:30:31
is the moment when the internal team cracks.

01:30:30 --> 01:30:33
Because I know the minute that happens,

01:30:33 --> 01:30:36
we're in a different regime. (laughs)

01:30:35 --> 01:30:37
Like, it's like the, you know,

01:30:36 --> 01:30:38
the solid has turned into a liquid.

01:30:37 --> 01:30:39
Like we're in a different regime

01:30:38 --> 01:30:41
and like the whole thing can unravel in the next week

01:30:40 --> 01:30:43
'cause then people turn, I mean this,

01:30:42 --> 01:30:46
I guess this is what's happening in Los Angeles right now.

01:30:45 --> 01:30:47
The mayor and the fire chief turned on each other,

01:30:46 --> 01:30:50
and that's it.

01:30:49 --> 01:30:51
That government is dysfunctional.

01:30:50 --> 01:30:53
It is never going to get put back together again.

01:30:52 --> 01:30:56
It is over, it is not going to work ever again.

01:30:55 --> 01:30:58
And that's what happens to inside companies.

01:30:57 --> 01:31:01
And so somebody like Mark

01:31:00 --> 01:31:04
is under like profound internal pressure

01:31:03 --> 01:31:05
and external pressure at the same time.

01:31:04 --> 01:31:07
Now he's been very good at maintaining the coherence

01:31:06 --> 01:31:09
of his executive team,

01:31:08 --> 01:31:12
but he has had over the years, a lot of activist employees

01:31:12 --> 01:31:14
as a lot of these companies have had

01:31:13 --> 01:31:16
and so that's been continuous pressure.

01:31:15 --> 01:31:17
And then the final thing I'd say, as I said,

01:31:16 --> 01:31:19
that companies can withstand most forms

01:31:18 --> 01:31:21
of external pressure, but not all.

01:31:20 --> 01:31:24
And especially, although not all one is government pressure.

01:31:23 --> 01:31:25
Is that when your government comes for you?

01:31:24 --> 01:31:28
Like yeah,

01:31:27 --> 01:31:30
any CEO who thinks that they're bigger

01:31:29 --> 01:31:31
than their government,

01:31:30 --> 01:31:33
has that notion beaten out of them in short order.

01:31:32 --> 01:31:35
- Can you just linger on that

01:31:34 --> 01:31:40
'cause it is maybe educating and deeply disturbing?

01:31:39 --> 01:31:41
You've spoken about it before,

01:31:40 --> 01:31:45
but we're speaking about again this government pressure.

01:31:44 --> 01:31:47
So you think they've crossed a line

01:31:46 --> 01:31:51
into essentially criminal levels of pressure?

01:31:50 --> 01:31:52
- Flagrant criminality,

01:31:52 --> 01:31:56
felonies, like obvious felonies.

01:31:55 --> 01:31:57
And I can actually cite the laws, (laughs)

01:31:56 --> 01:32:00
but yes, absolute criminality.

01:31:59 --> 01:32:04
- Can you explain how those possible to happen

01:32:03 --> 01:32:06
and maybe on a hopeful note,

01:32:05 --> 01:32:08
how we can avoid that happening again?

01:32:07 --> 01:32:10
- So I just start with is a lot of this now

01:32:09 --> 01:32:12
is in the public record,

01:32:11 --> 01:32:14
which is good 'cause it needs to be in the public record.

01:32:13 --> 01:32:15
And so there's three forms of things

01:32:14 --> 01:32:17
that are in the public record that people can look at.

01:32:16 --> 01:32:20
So when is the Twitter Files, right, which Elon put out

01:32:19 --> 01:32:22
with the set of journalists when he took over.

01:32:21 --> 01:32:23
And I will just tell you, the Twitter Files

01:32:22 --> 01:32:25
are 100% representative

01:32:24 --> 01:32:26
of what I've seen at every other one of these companies.

01:32:25 --> 01:32:29
And so you can just see what happened in Twitter

01:32:28 --> 01:32:30
and you can just assume that that happened

01:32:29 --> 01:32:33
in these other companies, you know, for the most part,

01:32:32 --> 01:32:35
certainly in terms of the kind of pressure that they got.

01:32:34 --> 01:32:35
So that's number one.

01:32:34 --> 01:32:37
That's stuff, you can just read it

01:32:36 --> 01:32:39
and you should if you haven't.

01:32:38 --> 01:32:43
The second is Mark referenced this in the Rogan podcast.

01:32:42 --> 01:32:44
There's a congressman Jim Jordan,

01:32:43 --> 01:32:46
who has a congressional committee

01:32:45 --> 01:32:47
called the Weaponization Committee.

01:32:46 --> 01:32:49
And they, in the last, you know, whatever three years

01:32:48 --> 01:32:51
have done a full-scale investigation of this.

01:32:50 --> 01:32:54
And Facebook produced a lot of documents

01:32:53 --> 01:32:55
into that investigation

01:32:54 --> 01:32:57
and many of those have now been made public

01:32:57 --> 01:32:59
and you can download those reports.

01:32:58 --> 01:33:01
And there's like 2,000 pages worth of material on that.

01:33:00 --> 01:33:03
And that's essentially the Facebook version

01:33:02 --> 01:33:04
of the Twitter Files just arrived

01:33:03 --> 01:33:06
at with a different mechanism.

01:33:05 --> 01:33:09
And then third is Mark himself talking about this on Rogan.

01:33:08 --> 01:33:11
So I'll, you know, just defer to his comments there.

01:33:10 --> 01:33:14
But yeah, basically what those three forms

01:33:13 --> 01:33:17
of information show you is basically the government,

01:33:16 --> 01:33:21
you know, over time and then culminating in 2020, 2021,

01:33:20 --> 01:33:22
you know, in the last four years,

01:33:21 --> 01:33:25
just decided that the First Amendment didn't apply to them.

01:33:24 --> 01:33:28
And they just decided that federal laws around free speech

01:33:27 --> 01:33:31
and around conspiracies to take away the rights

01:33:30 --> 01:33:32
of citizens just don't apply.

01:33:33 --> 01:33:34
And they just decided

01:33:33 --> 01:33:37
that they can just arbitrarily pressure.

01:33:36 --> 01:33:39
They just like literally arbitrarily call up companies

01:33:38 --> 01:33:41
and threaten and bully and yell and scream

01:33:40 --> 01:33:43
and, you know, threaten repercussions

01:33:42 --> 01:33:45
and force them to censor.

01:33:45 --> 01:33:48
And you know, there's this whole thing of like,

01:33:47 --> 01:33:49
well, the First Amendment only applies to, you know,

01:33:48 --> 01:33:51
the government, it doesn't apply to companies.

01:33:50 --> 01:33:52
It's like, well,

01:33:51 --> 01:33:54
there's actually a little bit of nuance to that.

01:33:53 --> 01:33:57
First of all, it definitely applies to the government.

01:33:56 --> 01:33:58
Like 100%,

01:33:57 --> 01:34:00
the First Amendment applies to the government.

01:33:59 --> 01:34:02
By the way, so does the Fourth Amendment

01:34:01 --> 01:34:02
and the Fifth Amendment,

01:34:01 --> 01:34:04
including the right to due process,

01:34:03 --> 01:34:05
also applies to the government.

01:34:04 --> 01:34:07
There was no due process at all

01:34:06 --> 01:34:09
to any of the censorship regime that was put in place.

01:34:08 --> 01:34:11
There was no due process put in place, by the way,

01:34:10 --> 01:34:12
for de-banking either.

01:34:11 --> 01:34:14
Those are just as serious violations

01:34:13 --> 01:34:16
as the free speech violations.

01:34:15 --> 01:34:18
And so this is just like flagrant, flagrant,

01:34:17 --> 01:34:19
unconstitutional behavior.

01:34:18 --> 01:34:21
And then there are specific federal statutes,

01:34:20 --> 01:34:24
it's 18 241 and 18 242.

01:34:23 --> 01:34:25
And one of them applies

01:34:24 --> 01:34:27
to federal employees, government employees,

01:34:26 --> 01:34:29
and the other one applies to private actors

01:34:29 --> 01:34:32
around what's called deprivation of rights

01:34:31 --> 01:34:34
and conspiracy to deprive rights.

01:34:33 --> 01:34:35
And it is not legal

01:34:34 --> 01:34:37
according to the United States Criminal Code

01:34:36 --> 01:34:38
for government employees

01:34:37 --> 01:34:40
or in a conspiracy private entities

01:34:39 --> 01:34:42
to take away constitutional rights.

01:34:41 --> 01:34:44
And interestingly,

01:34:43 --> 01:34:46
some of those constitutional rights are enumerated,

01:34:45 --> 01:34:48
for example, in the First Amendment, freedom of speech.

01:34:47 --> 01:34:49
And then some of those rights actually

01:34:48 --> 01:34:51
do not need to be enumerated.

01:34:51 --> 01:34:55
If the government takes away rights that you have,

01:34:54 --> 01:34:57
they don't need to be specifically enumerated rights

01:34:56 --> 01:34:59
in the Constitution order to still be a felony.

01:34:59 --> 01:35:02
The Constitution very specifically

01:35:01 --> 01:35:04
does not say you only have the rights that it gives you.

01:35:03 --> 01:35:06
It says you have all the rights

01:35:05 --> 01:35:07
that have not been previously

01:35:06 --> 01:35:09
defined as being taken away from you.

01:35:08 --> 01:35:12
Right, and so de-banking qualifies as a right,

01:35:11 --> 01:35:13
you know, right to access

01:35:12 --> 01:35:14
to the financial system, is every bit something

01:35:13 --> 01:35:17
that's subject to these laws as free speech.

01:35:16 --> 01:35:18
And so yeah, this has happened.

01:35:18 --> 01:35:20
And then I'll just add one final thing,

01:35:19 --> 01:35:21
which is we've talked about two parties so far.

01:35:20 --> 01:35:23
We talked about the government employees

01:35:22 --> 01:35:25
and then we've talked about the companies.

01:35:24 --> 01:35:28
The government employees for sure have misbehaved.

01:35:27 --> 01:35:30
The companies, there's a very interesting question there

01:35:29 --> 01:35:33
as to whether they are victims or perpetrators or both.

01:35:33 --> 01:35:36
You know, they will defend and they will argue,

01:35:35 --> 01:35:37
and I believe they have a good case,

01:35:36 --> 01:35:38
that they are victims, not perpetrators, right?

01:35:37 --> 01:35:41
They're the downstream subjects of pressure,

01:35:40 --> 01:35:43
you know, not the cause of pressure,

01:35:42 --> 01:35:46
but there's a big swath of people who are in the middle

01:35:45 --> 01:35:48
and specifically the ones that are funded by the government

01:35:47 --> 01:35:50
hat I think are in possibly pretty big trouble.

01:35:49 --> 01:35:54
And that's all of these third-party censorship bureaus.

01:35:53 --> 01:35:56
I mean the one that is sort of is most obvious

01:35:55 --> 01:35:58
is the so-called Stanford Internet Observatory

01:35:58 --> 01:36:01
that got booted up there over the last several years.

01:36:00 --> 01:36:04
And they basically were funded by the federal government

01:36:03 --> 01:36:06
o be third-party censorship operations.

01:36:05 --> 01:36:09
And they're private sector actors,

01:36:08 --> 01:36:11
but acting with federal funding.

01:36:10 --> 01:36:13
And so it puts them in this very interesting spot

01:36:12 --> 01:36:15
where there could be, you know,

01:36:14 --> 01:36:17
very obvious theory under which they're basically acting

01:36:16 --> 01:36:19
as agents of the government.

01:36:18 --> 01:36:21
And so I think they're also very exposed on this

01:36:20 --> 01:36:23
and have behaved in just flagrantly illegal ways.

01:36:22 --> 01:36:24
- So fundamentally,

01:36:23 --> 01:36:28
government should not do any kind of pressure,

01:36:27 --> 01:36:31
even soft pressure on companies to censor?

01:36:30 --> 01:36:31
- Can't.

01:36:31 --> 01:36:33
Not allowed.

01:36:32 --> 01:36:35
- It really is disturbing.

01:36:34 --> 01:36:38
I mean it probably started soft,

01:36:38 --> 01:36:41
lightly slowly,

01:36:40 --> 01:36:44
and then it escalates as the the old

01:36:43 --> 01:36:46
will to power will instruct them to do.

01:36:47 --> 01:36:49
I mean, yeah,

01:36:49 --> 01:36:52
that's why there's protection

01:36:51 --> 01:36:55
'cause you can't put a check on power for government, right?

01:36:54 --> 01:36:57
- There are so many ways that they can get you,

01:36:56 --> 01:36:59
like there are so many ways they can come at you and get you

01:36:58 --> 01:37:01
and, you know, the thing here to think about

01:37:00 --> 01:37:04
is a lot of times when people think about government action,

01:37:03 --> 01:37:05
they think about legislation, right?

01:37:04 --> 01:37:07
So when I was a kid,

01:37:06 --> 01:37:09
we got trained, how does government work?

01:37:08 --> 01:37:11
There was this famous animated short,

01:37:10 --> 01:37:13
the thing we got shown was just a cartoon

01:37:12 --> 01:37:14
of how a bill becomes a law.

01:37:13 --> 01:37:15
It's like this, you know,

01:37:14 --> 01:37:16
fancy little bill sneaked along and goes this-

01:37:15 --> 01:37:17
I'm just the bill.

01:37:16 --> 01:37:19
Yeah. - Exactly.

01:37:18 --> 01:37:20
It's like, all right, number one,

01:37:19 --> 01:37:21
that's not how it works at all.

01:37:20 --> 01:37:22
Like that doesn't actually happen.

01:37:21 --> 01:37:23
We could talk about that.

01:37:22 --> 01:37:25
But even beyond that, mostly what we're dealing with

01:37:24 --> 01:37:27
is not legislation.

01:37:26 --> 01:37:28
When we talk about government power these days,

01:37:27 --> 01:37:30
mostly it's not legislation.

01:37:29 --> 01:37:32
Mostly it's either regulation,

01:37:31 --> 01:37:33
which is basically the equivalent of legislation,

01:37:33 --> 01:37:36
but having not gone through the legislative process,

01:37:35 --> 01:37:37
which is a very big open legal issue.

01:37:36 --> 01:37:40
And one of the things that the DOGE is very focused on.

01:37:39 --> 01:37:42
Most government rules are not legislated.

01:37:41 --> 01:37:43
They're regulated

01:37:42 --> 01:37:45
and there's tons and tons of regulations

01:37:44 --> 01:37:46
that these companies are,

01:37:45 --> 01:37:48
this is another cliche you'll hear a lot, which is,

01:37:47 --> 01:37:49
"Oh, private companies can do whatever they want."

01:37:48 --> 01:37:51
It's like, "Oh, no, they can't."

01:37:50 --> 01:37:53
They're subject to tens of thousands of regulations

01:37:52 --> 01:37:55
that they have to comply with.

01:37:54 --> 01:37:57
And the hammer that comes down when you don't comply

01:37:56 --> 01:37:58
with regulations is profound.

01:37:57 --> 01:37:59
Like they can completely wreck your company

01:37:58 --> 01:38:02
with no ability for you to do anything about it.

01:38:01 --> 01:38:04
So regulation is a big part

01:38:03 --> 01:38:05
of the way the power gets exercised.

01:38:04 --> 01:38:06
And then there's what's called

01:38:05 --> 01:38:08
just flat out administrative power,

01:38:07 --> 01:38:09
the term that you'll hear

01:38:08 --> 01:38:11
and administrative power is just literally

01:38:10 --> 01:38:12
the government telling you,

01:38:11 --> 01:38:13
calling you and telling you what to do.

01:38:12 --> 01:38:14
Here's an example of how this works.

01:38:13 --> 01:38:16
So Facebook had this whole program a few years back

01:38:15 --> 01:38:20
to do a global cryptocurrency for payments called Libra.

01:38:19 --> 01:38:21
And they built the entire system

01:38:20 --> 01:38:22
and it was this high-scale, you know,

01:38:21 --> 01:38:23
sort of new cryptocurrency,

01:38:22 --> 01:38:24
and they were gonna build into every product,

01:38:23 --> 01:38:25
and they were gonna be 3 billion people

01:38:24 --> 01:38:27
who could transact with Libra.

01:38:26 --> 01:38:29
And they went to the government and they went to the,

01:38:28 --> 01:38:30
all these different try to figure out how to make it,

01:38:29 --> 01:38:32
so it's like fully compliant with anti-money laundering

01:38:31 --> 01:38:33
and all these, you know, controls and everything.

01:38:32 --> 01:38:35
And they had the whole thing ready to go.

01:38:34 --> 01:38:38
Two senators wrote letters to the big banks saying,

01:38:37 --> 01:38:41
"We're not telling you that you can't work

01:38:40 --> 01:38:43
with Facebook on this, but if you do,

01:38:42 --> 01:38:45
you should know that every aspect of your business

01:38:45 --> 01:38:48
is going to come under greatly increased level

01:38:47 --> 01:38:49
of regulatory scrutiny,"

01:38:48 --> 01:38:51
which is, of course, the exact equivalent

01:38:50 --> 01:38:54
of it sure is a nice corner restaurant you have here.

01:38:53 --> 01:38:55
It would be a shame if, you know,

01:38:54 --> 01:38:57
somebody tossed a Molotov cocktail through the window

01:38:56 --> 01:38:58
and burned it down tonight, right?

01:38:57 --> 01:39:01
And like, what is that letter?

01:39:00 --> 01:39:03
Like, it's not a law, it's not even a regulation,

01:39:02 --> 01:39:07
it's just like straight direct state power.

01:39:06 --> 01:39:09
And then it culminates in literally calls

01:39:08 --> 01:39:10
from the White House

01:39:09 --> 01:39:12
where they're just like flat out telling you what to do,

01:39:11 --> 01:39:14
which is, of course, what a king gets to do,

01:39:13 --> 01:39:15
but not what a president gets to do.

01:39:15 --> 01:39:19
And so anyway, so what these companies experienced

01:39:18 --> 01:39:21
was they experienced the full panoply of this,

01:39:20 --> 01:39:24
but the level of intensity was in that order,

01:39:23 --> 01:39:27
it was actually, legislation was the least important part.

01:39:26 --> 01:39:28
Regulation was more important,

01:39:27 --> 01:39:29
administrative power was more important

01:39:28 --> 01:39:31
and then just like flat out demands

01:39:30 --> 01:39:34
and flat out threats were ultimately the most important.

01:39:33 --> 01:39:35
How do you fix it?

01:39:34 --> 01:39:36
Well, first of all,

01:39:35 --> 01:39:39
like you have to elect people who don't do it, right?

01:39:38 --> 01:39:40
So like as with all these things,

01:39:39 --> 01:39:43
ultimately the fault lies with the voters.

01:39:42 --> 01:39:44
And so, you know, you have to decide.

01:39:43 --> 01:39:46
You don't wanna live in that regime. I have no idea

01:39:45 --> 01:39:47
what part of this recent election mapped

01:39:46 --> 01:39:48
to the censorship regime.

01:39:47 --> 01:39:50
I do know a lot of people on the right got very angry

01:39:49 --> 01:39:52
about the censorship, but I, you know,

01:39:51 --> 01:39:53
I think it probably at least helped

01:39:52 --> 01:39:54
with enthusiasm on that side.

01:39:54 --> 01:39:57
You know, maybe some people on the left

01:39:56 --> 01:39:58
will now not want their, you know,

01:39:57 --> 01:40:00
Democratic nominees to be so pro-censorship.

01:40:00 --> 01:40:03
So the voters definitely, you know,

01:40:02 --> 01:40:05
get a vote, number one.

01:40:04 --> 01:40:07
Number two, I think you need transparency.

01:40:06 --> 01:40:09
You need to know what happened.

01:40:08 --> 01:40:10
We know some of what happened.

01:40:09 --> 01:40:13
Peter Thiel has written in the FT just now saying,

01:40:12 --> 01:40:14
we just need like broad after what we've been through

01:40:13 --> 01:40:15
in the last decade,

01:40:14 --> 01:40:18
we need to broad base truth and reconciliation,

01:40:17 --> 01:40:21
you know, efforts to really get to the root of things.

01:40:20 --> 01:40:23
So maybe that's part of it.

01:40:22 --> 01:40:25
We need investigations for sure.

01:40:24 --> 01:40:27
Ultimately, we need prosecutions.

01:40:26 --> 01:40:29
Like ultimately, we need people to go to jail

01:40:28 --> 01:40:30
'cause we need to set object lessons that say

01:40:29 --> 01:40:33
that you don't get to do this.

01:40:32 --> 01:40:34
And on those last two, I would say

01:40:33 --> 01:40:36
that those are both up to the new administration,

01:40:35 --> 01:40:37
and I don't wanna speak for them

01:40:36 --> 01:40:39
and I don't wanna predict what they're gonna do,

01:40:38 --> 01:40:41
but they for sure have the ability to do both

01:40:40 --> 01:40:44
of those things and you know, we'll see where they take it.

01:40:43 --> 01:40:45
- Yeah, it's truly disturbing.

01:40:44 --> 01:40:47
I don't think anybody wants this kind of overreach of power

01:40:46 --> 01:40:49
for government,

01:40:48 --> 01:40:51
including perhaps people that are participating in it.

01:40:50 --> 01:40:56
It's like this dark momentum of power

01:40:55 --> 01:40:58
that you just get caught up in it.

01:40:57 --> 01:41:00
And that's the reason there's that kind of protection.

01:40:59 --> 01:41:01
Nobody wants that.

01:41:00 --> 01:41:02
- So I use the metaphor,

01:41:01 --> 01:41:03
the ring of power. - Yeah.

01:41:02 --> 01:41:05
- And for people who don't catch the reference,

01:41:04 --> 01:41:06
that's "Lord of the Rings."

01:41:05 --> 01:41:07
And the thing with the Ring of Power

01:41:06 --> 01:41:09
and "Lord of the Rings," it's the ring the Gollum

01:41:08 --> 01:41:11
has in the beginning and it turns you invisible.

01:41:10 --> 01:41:12
And it turns out it like unlocks

01:41:11 --> 01:41:13
all this like fearsome power.

01:41:12 --> 01:41:15
It's the most powerful thing in the world,

01:41:14 --> 01:41:16
is to key to everything.

01:41:15 --> 01:41:18
And basically, the moral lesson of "Lord of the Rings,"

01:41:17 --> 01:41:20
which was, you know, written by a guy

01:41:19 --> 01:41:22
who thought very deeply about these things is, yeah,

01:41:21 --> 01:41:24
the ring of power is inherently corrupting.

01:41:23 --> 01:41:25
The characters at one point, they're like,

01:41:24 --> 01:41:27
"Gandalf, just put on the ring and like fix this."

01:41:26 --> 01:41:30
Right, and he will not put the ring on

01:41:29 --> 01:41:33
even to like end the war

01:41:32 --> 01:41:35
because he knows that it will corrupt him.

01:41:34 --> 01:41:36
And then, you know, as it starts,

01:41:35 --> 01:41:39
the character of Gollum is the result of, you know,

01:41:38 --> 01:41:42
like a normal character who ultimately becomes,

01:41:41 --> 01:41:43
you know, this incredibly corrupt

01:41:42 --> 01:41:44
and deranged version of himself.

01:41:43 --> 01:41:45
And so, I mean,

01:41:44 --> 01:41:47
I think you said something actually quite profound there,

01:41:46 --> 01:41:50
which is the ring of power is infinitely tempting.

01:41:49 --> 01:41:52
You know, the censorship machine is infinitely tempting.

01:41:51 --> 01:41:56
If you have it, like you are going to use it.

01:41:55 --> 01:41:58
It's overwhelmingly tempting 'cause it's so powerful,

01:41:57 --> 01:42:01
and that it will corrupt you.

01:42:00 --> 01:42:02
And yeah, I don't know

01:42:01 --> 01:42:05
hether any of these people feel any of this today.

01:42:04 --> 01:42:06
They should.

01:42:05 --> 01:42:07
I don't know if they do.

01:42:06 --> 01:42:08
But yeah.

01:42:07 --> 01:42:10
You go out five or 10 years later, you know, you would hope

01:42:09 --> 01:42:12
that you would realize that your soul has been corroded

01:42:11 --> 01:42:14
and you probably started out thinking

01:42:13 --> 01:42:15
that you were a patriot

01:42:14 --> 01:42:16
and you were trying to defend democracy,

01:42:15 --> 01:42:18
and you ended up being, you know,

01:42:17 --> 01:42:19
extremely authoritarian and anti-democratic

01:42:18 --> 01:42:21
and anti-Western.

01:42:20 --> 01:42:24
- Can I ask you a tough question here?

01:42:24 --> 01:42:27
Staying on the ring of power,

01:42:26 --> 01:42:31
Elon is quickly becoming the most powerful human on Earth.

01:42:34 --> 01:42:36
- I'm not sure about that.

01:42:35 --> 01:42:37
- You don't think he is?

01:42:36 --> 01:42:40
- Well, he doesn't have the nukes so.

01:42:39 --> 01:42:41
- Nukes.

01:42:40 --> 01:42:43
(Marc laughing)

01:42:42 --> 01:42:44
Yeah, there's different definitions

01:42:43 --> 01:42:46
and perspectives on power, right?

01:42:45 --> 01:42:47
- Yeah.

01:42:46 --> 01:42:49
- How can he and/or Donald Trump

01:42:49 --> 01:42:54
avoid the corrupting aspects of this power?

01:42:53 --> 01:42:55
- I mean, I think the danger is there with power.

01:42:54 --> 01:42:57
It's flat out there.

01:42:56 --> 01:42:59
I would say with Elon, we'll see.

01:42:58 --> 01:43:01
I would say with Elon, and I would say by the way,

01:43:00 --> 01:43:02
overwhelmingly, I would say so far so good.

01:43:01 --> 01:43:04
I'm extremely, extremely thrilled by

01:43:03 --> 01:43:07
what he's done on almost every front for like,

01:43:06 --> 01:43:08
you know, the last 30 years.

01:43:07 --> 01:43:09
(Lex laughing)

01:43:08 --> 01:43:10
But including all this stuff recently,

01:43:09 --> 01:43:12
like I think he's been a real hero on a lot of topics

01:43:11 --> 01:43:13
where we needed to see heroism.

01:43:12 --> 01:43:16
But look, I would say, I guess the sort of case

01:43:15 --> 01:43:18
that he has this level of power is that some combination

01:43:17 --> 01:43:19
of the money

01:43:18 --> 01:43:20
and the proximity to the president.

01:43:19 --> 01:43:22
And obviously both of those are, you know,

01:43:21 --> 01:43:23
are instruments of power.

01:43:22 --> 01:43:25
The counter argument to that is,

01:43:24 --> 01:43:28
I do think a lot of how Elon is causing change

01:43:27 --> 01:43:30
in the world right now.

01:43:29 --> 01:43:31
I mean, there's the companies he's running directly

01:43:30 --> 01:43:32
where I think he's doing very well,

01:43:31 --> 01:43:36
and we're investors in multiple of them and doing very well.

01:43:36 --> 01:43:38
But I think like a lot of the stuff

01:43:37 --> 01:43:40
that gets people mad at him is like,

01:43:39 --> 01:43:42
it's the social and political stuff

01:43:41 --> 01:43:43
and it's, you know, it's his statements,

01:43:42 --> 01:43:45
and then it's the downstream effects of his statements.

01:43:44 --> 01:43:46
So like for example, it's, you know,

01:43:45 --> 01:43:49
for the last couple weeks, it's been him, you know,

01:43:48 --> 01:43:51
kind of weighing in on this rape gang scandal, you know,

01:43:50 --> 01:43:53
this rape organized child rape thing in the UK.

01:43:53 --> 01:43:58
And you know, it's a preface cascade.

01:43:57 --> 01:43:58
It's one of these things

01:43:57 --> 01:44:00
where people knew there was a problem,

01:43:59 --> 01:44:01
they weren't willing to talk about it,

01:44:00 --> 01:44:03
it kind of got suppressed.

01:44:02 --> 01:44:05
And then Elon brought it up,

01:44:04 --> 01:44:07
and then all of a sudden there's now in the UK

01:44:06 --> 01:44:09
this like massive explosion of basically open conversation

01:44:08 --> 01:44:10
about it for the first time.

01:44:09 --> 01:44:12
And you know, it's like this catalyzing,

01:44:11 --> 01:44:13
all of a sudden everybody's kind of woken up

01:44:12 --> 01:44:16
and being like, "Oh, my God, you know, this is really bad."

01:44:15 --> 01:44:18
And there will be now, you know,

01:44:17 --> 01:44:20
pretty clearly big changes as a result.

01:44:19 --> 01:44:22
So, and Elon was, you know, he played the role of the boy

01:44:21 --> 01:44:24
who said, the emperor has no clothes, right?

01:44:23 --> 01:44:26
But here's the thing, here's my point.

01:44:25 --> 01:44:29
Like he said it about something that was true, right?

01:44:28 --> 01:44:31
And so had he said it about something that was false,

01:44:30 --> 01:44:33
you know, he would get no credit for it.

01:44:32 --> 01:44:35
He wouldn't deserve any credit for it.

01:44:34 --> 01:44:37
But he said something that was true.

01:44:36 --> 01:44:38
And by the way, everybody over there instantly,

01:44:37 --> 01:44:41
they were like, "Oh, yeah, he's right," right? (laughs)

01:44:40 --> 01:44:43
Like nobody seriously said they're just arguing

01:44:42 --> 01:44:44
the details now.

01:44:43 --> 01:44:46
So number one, it's like, okay, he says true things.

01:44:45 --> 01:44:48
And so it's like, okay, how far...

01:44:47 --> 01:44:49
Put it this way.

01:44:48 --> 01:44:52
Like, how worried are we about somebody becoming corrupt

01:44:51 --> 01:44:53
by virtue of their power being

01:44:52 --> 01:44:55
that they get to speak the truth?

01:44:54 --> 01:44:57
And I guess I would say, especially in the last decade

01:44:56 --> 01:44:58
of what we've been through

01:44:57 --> 01:44:59
where everybody's been lying all the time about everything,

01:44:58 --> 01:45:01
I'd say, I think we should run this experiment

01:45:00 --> 01:45:02
as hard as we can

01:45:01 --> 01:45:03
to get people to tell the truth.

01:45:02 --> 01:45:06
And so I don't feel that bad about that.

01:45:05 --> 01:45:08
And then the money side, you know,

01:45:07 --> 01:45:11
this rapidly gets into the money and politics question.

01:45:10 --> 01:45:12
And the money and politics question

01:45:11 --> 01:45:13
is this very interesting question

01:45:12 --> 01:45:17
because it seems like it, there's a clear-cut case

01:45:16 --> 01:45:19
that the more money in politics, the worse things are

01:45:18 --> 01:45:21
and the more corrupted the system is.

01:45:20 --> 01:45:24
That was a very popular topic of public conversation up

01:45:23 --> 01:45:28
until 2016 when Hillary outspent Trump 3 to 1 and lost.

01:45:27 --> 01:45:31
You'll notice that money and politics has all most vanished

01:45:30 --> 01:45:33
as a topic in the last eight years.

01:45:32 --> 01:45:36
And once again, Trump spent far, you know,

01:45:35 --> 01:45:38
Kamala raised and spent 1.5 billion

01:45:37 --> 01:45:39
top of what Biden had spent.

01:45:38 --> 01:45:41
So they were at, I don't know,

01:45:40 --> 01:45:42
something like 3 billion total in Trump,

01:45:41 --> 01:45:45
I think spent again like a third or a fourth of that.

01:45:45 --> 01:45:48
And so the money in politics kind of topic

01:45:47 --> 01:45:50
has kind of vanished from a popular conversation

01:45:49 --> 01:45:51
in the last eight years.

01:45:50 --> 01:45:54
It has come back a little bit now that Elon is spending.

01:45:56 --> 01:45:58
You know, but again, it's like,

01:45:57 --> 01:46:00
okay, he's spending, but the data would seem

01:45:59 --> 01:46:02
to indicate in the last, at least in the last eight years,

01:46:01 --> 01:46:03
that money doesn't win the political battles.

01:46:02 --> 01:46:06
It's actually like the voters actually have a voice

01:46:05 --> 01:46:07
and they actually exercise it,

01:46:06 --> 01:46:08
and they don't just listen to ads.

01:46:07 --> 01:46:09
And so again, there I would say like, yeah,

01:46:08 --> 01:46:10
clearly there's some power there,

01:46:09 --> 01:46:14
but I don't know if it's some weapon

01:46:13 --> 01:46:16
that he can just like turn on

01:46:15 --> 01:46:18
and use in a definitive way.

01:46:17 --> 01:46:20
- And I don't know if those parallels there,

01:46:19 --> 01:46:23
but I could also say just on a human level,

01:46:22 --> 01:46:25
he has a good heart and I interact

01:46:24 --> 01:46:27
with a lot of powerful people,

01:46:26 --> 01:46:29
and that's not always the case.

01:46:28 --> 01:46:31
So that's a good thing there. - Yeah.

01:46:30 --> 01:46:33
- If we can draw parallels to the Hobbit

01:46:32 --> 01:46:35
or whatever. (laughs)

01:46:34 --> 01:46:36
Who gets to put on the ring?

01:46:35 --> 01:46:37
- [Marc] Frodo.

01:46:36 --> 01:46:39
- Frodo, yeah. - Yeah.

01:46:38 --> 01:46:40
Maybe one of the lessons of "Lord of the Rings" right,

01:46:39 --> 01:46:42
is even Frodo would've been, you know,

01:46:41 --> 01:46:43
even Frodo would've been corrupted, right?

01:46:42 --> 01:46:46
But, you know, nevertheless, you had somebody who could do

01:46:45 --> 01:46:48
what it took at at the time.

01:46:47 --> 01:46:49
The thing that I find just so amazing

01:46:48 --> 01:46:50
about the Elon phenomenon

01:46:49 --> 01:46:52
and all the critiques is, you know,

01:46:51 --> 01:46:53
the one thing that everybody

01:46:52 --> 01:46:54
in our societies universally agrees on

01:46:53 --> 01:46:59
because it's sort of our post-Christian egalitarian.

01:46:58 --> 01:47:00
So, you know, we live in sort

01:46:59 --> 01:47:03
of this post-secularized Christian context in the West now,

01:47:02 --> 01:47:06
and it's, you know, we consider Christianity kind of,

01:47:05 --> 01:47:07
you know, backwards,

01:47:06 --> 01:47:09
but we still believe essentially all the same things.

01:47:08 --> 01:47:12
We just dress them up in sort of fake science.

01:47:11 --> 01:47:13
(Lex laughing)

01:47:12 --> 01:47:15
So the one thing that we're all told,

01:47:14 --> 01:47:16
we're all taught from early

01:47:15 --> 01:47:17
is that the best people in the world are the people

01:47:16 --> 01:47:19
who care about all of humanity, right?

01:47:18 --> 01:47:22
And we venerate, you know, all of our figures are people

01:47:21 --> 01:47:23
who care about all of, you know...

01:47:22 --> 01:47:24
Jesus cared about all of humanity.

01:47:23 --> 01:47:26
Gandhi cared about all of humanity.

01:47:25 --> 01:47:27
Martin Luther King cared about all of humanity.

01:47:26 --> 01:47:28
Like it's this universe,

01:47:27 --> 01:47:31
the person who cares the most about everybody.

01:47:31 --> 01:47:35
And with Elon, you have a guy who literally, like, is,

01:47:34 --> 01:47:36
he talks about this constantly,

01:47:35 --> 01:47:37
and he talks about exactly the same in private.

01:47:36 --> 01:47:39
He is literally, he is operating on behalf

01:47:38 --> 01:47:41
of all of humanity to try to get us to,

01:47:40 --> 01:47:42
you know, he goes through

01:47:41 --> 01:47:44
to get us through multi-planetary civilization,

01:47:43 --> 01:47:45
so that we can survive a strike on any one planet,

01:47:44 --> 01:47:47
so that we can extend the light of human consciousness

01:47:46 --> 01:47:49
into the world and, you know, into the universe

01:47:48 --> 01:47:50
and have it persist,

01:47:49 --> 01:47:51
you know, in the good of the whole thing.

01:47:50 --> 01:47:53
And like literally the critique is, yeah,

01:47:52 --> 01:47:54
we want you to care about all of humanity,

01:47:54 --> 01:47:56
but not like that.

01:47:55 --> 01:47:57
(both laughing)

01:47:56 --> 01:47:58
- Yeah.

01:47:57 --> 01:47:59
All the critics.

01:47:59 --> 01:48:02
All the surface turmoil,

01:48:01 --> 01:48:04
the critics will be forgotten.

01:48:03 --> 01:48:06
- Yeah, I think, yeah, that's clear.

01:48:05 --> 01:48:09
- You said that we always end up being ruled

01:48:08 --> 01:48:11
by the elite of some kind.

01:48:10 --> 01:48:14
Can you explain this law, this idea?

01:48:13 --> 01:48:17
- So this comes from a Italian political philosopher

01:48:16 --> 01:48:19
from about 100 years ago named Robert...

01:48:18 --> 01:48:21
I'm gonna mangle it from,

01:48:20 --> 01:48:22
let you pronounce the Italian.

01:48:21 --> 01:48:26
Michels or Michels.

01:48:25 --> 01:48:29
And then I learned about it through a famous book

01:48:28 --> 01:48:30
on politics, probably the best book on politics written

01:48:29 --> 01:48:31
in the 20th century

01:48:30 --> 01:48:35
called "The Machiavellians" by this guy James Burnham,

01:48:34 --> 01:48:36
who has had a big impact on me.

01:48:35 --> 01:48:38
But in "The Machiavellians,"

01:48:37 --> 01:48:39
he resurrects what he calls

01:48:38 --> 01:48:40
is this sort of Italian realist school

01:48:39 --> 01:48:41
of political philosophy

01:48:40 --> 01:48:44
from the '10s and '20s. And these were people, to be clear,

01:48:43 --> 01:48:45
this was not like a Mussolini thing.

01:48:44 --> 01:48:47
These were people who were trying to understand

01:48:46 --> 01:48:50
the actual mechanics of how politics actually works.

01:48:49 --> 01:48:54
So to get to the actual sort of mechanical substance of like

01:48:53 --> 01:48:56
how the political machine operates.

01:48:55 --> 01:48:59
And this guy, Michels had this concept ended up

01:48:58 --> 01:49:02
with called the iron law of oligarchy.

01:49:01 --> 01:49:04
And so what the iron law of oligarchy,

01:49:03 --> 01:49:06
and I mean take a step back

01:49:05 --> 01:49:07
to say what he meant by oligarchy

01:49:06 --> 01:49:08
'cause it has multiple meanings.

01:49:07 --> 01:49:10
So basically, in classic political theory,

01:49:09 --> 01:49:12
there's basically three forms of government at core.

01:49:11 --> 01:49:15
There's democracy, which is rule of many.

01:49:14 --> 01:49:17
There's oligarchy, which is rule of the few.

01:49:16 --> 01:49:19
And there's monarchy, which is rule of the one.

01:49:18 --> 01:49:21
And you can just use that as a general framework

01:49:20 --> 01:49:22
of any government you're gonna be under

01:49:21 --> 01:49:25
is gonna be one of those, just mechanical observation

01:49:24 --> 01:49:26
without even saying which one's good or bad,

01:49:25 --> 01:49:28
just a structural observation.

01:49:28 --> 01:49:30
And so the question that Michels asked was like,

01:49:29 --> 01:49:32
is there such a thing as democracy?

01:49:31 --> 01:49:34
Like is there actually such a thing as democracy?

01:49:33 --> 01:49:37
Is there ever actually like direct government?

01:49:36 --> 01:49:38
And what he did was

01:49:37 --> 01:49:40
he mounted this sort of incredible historical exploration

01:49:39 --> 01:49:43
of whether democracies had ever existed in the world.

01:49:42 --> 01:49:45
And the answer basically is almost never.

01:49:44 --> 01:49:46
And we could talk about that.

01:49:45 --> 01:49:47
But the other thing he did was

01:49:46 --> 01:49:51
he sought out the most democratic private organization

01:49:50 --> 01:49:53
in the world that he could find at that point,

01:49:52 --> 01:49:53
which he concluded

01:49:52 --> 01:49:56
was some basically communist German autoworkers union

01:49:55 --> 01:49:58
that was like wholly devoted to the workers

01:49:57 --> 01:49:59
of the world uniting, you know,

01:49:58 --> 01:50:01
back when that was like the hot thing.

01:50:00 --> 01:50:02
And he went in there and he is like, okay,

01:50:01 --> 01:50:04
this is the organization out of all organizations

01:50:03 --> 01:50:06
on planet Earth that must be operating

01:50:05 --> 01:50:07
as a direct democracy.

01:50:06 --> 01:50:09
And he went in there and he is like, "Oh, nope."

01:50:08 --> 01:50:10
There's a leadership class.

01:50:09 --> 01:50:11
You know, there's like six guys at the top

01:50:10 --> 01:50:13
and they control everything (laughs)

01:50:12 --> 01:50:15
and then they lead the rest of the membership along,

01:50:14 --> 01:50:15
you know, by the nose,

01:50:14 --> 01:50:18
which is, of course, the story of every union.

01:50:17 --> 01:50:21
The story of every union is always the story of, you know,

01:50:20 --> 01:50:22
there's a Jimmy Hoffa in there, you know,

01:50:21 --> 01:50:23
kind of running the thing.

01:50:22 --> 01:50:24
You know, we just saw

01:50:23 --> 01:50:25
that with the dock workers' union, right?

01:50:24 --> 01:50:29
Like, you know, there's a guy and he's in charge.

01:50:28 --> 01:50:31
And by the way, the number two is his son, right?

01:50:30 --> 01:50:35
Like, that's not like a, you know, an accident, right?

01:50:34 --> 01:50:37
So the iron law oligarchy basically says democracy is fake.

01:50:38 --> 01:50:41
There's always a ruling elite structurally.

01:50:40 --> 01:50:42
And he said,

01:50:41 --> 01:50:42
"The reason for that

01:50:41 --> 01:50:45
is because the masses can't organize," right?

01:50:44 --> 01:50:46
What's the fundamental problem?

01:50:45 --> 01:50:48
Whether the mass is 25,000 people in a union

01:50:47 --> 01:50:49
or 250 million people in a country,

01:50:48 --> 01:50:52
the masses can't organize, the majority cannot organize,

01:50:51 --> 01:50:54
only a minority can organize.

01:50:53 --> 01:50:57
And to be effective in politics, you must organize.

01:50:56 --> 01:50:59
And therefore, every political structure

01:50:58 --> 01:51:00
in human history

01:50:59 --> 01:51:03
has been some form of a small organized elite ruling

01:51:03 --> 01:51:05
a large and dispersed majority.

01:51:04 --> 01:51:06
Every single one.

01:51:07 --> 01:51:10
The Greeks and the Florentines

01:51:09 --> 01:51:12
had brief experiments in direct democracy,

01:51:11 --> 01:51:15
and they were total disasters.

01:51:14 --> 01:51:16
In Florence... I forget the name of it.

01:51:15 --> 01:51:18
It was called like The Workers' Revolt

01:51:17 --> 01:51:19
or something like that.

01:51:18 --> 01:51:21
There was like a two-year period

01:51:20 --> 01:51:23
where they basically experimented with direct democracy

01:51:22 --> 01:51:24
during the Renaissance,

01:51:23 --> 01:51:26
and it was a complete disaster

01:51:25 --> 01:51:28
and they never tried it again.

01:51:27 --> 01:51:29
In the state of California,

01:51:28 --> 01:51:31
we have our own experiment on this,

01:51:30 --> 01:51:34
which is the proposition system, which is an overlay

01:51:33 --> 01:51:35
on top of the legislature.

01:51:34 --> 01:51:37
You know, anybody who looks at it

01:51:36 --> 01:51:39
for two seconds concludes it's been a complete disaster.

01:51:38 --> 01:51:40
It's just a catastrophe,

01:51:39 --> 01:51:42
and it's caused enormous damage to the state.

01:51:41 --> 01:51:45
And so basically, the presumption

01:51:44 --> 01:51:46
that we are in a democracy

01:51:45 --> 01:51:47
is just sort of by definition fake.

01:51:46 --> 01:51:49
Now, good news for the US,

01:51:48 --> 01:51:50
it turns out the founders understood this.

01:51:49 --> 01:51:52
And so of course, they didn't give us a direct democracy,

01:51:51 --> 01:51:54
they gave us a representative democracy, right?

01:51:53 --> 01:51:57
And so they built the oligarchy into the system

01:51:56 --> 01:51:58
in the form of Congress

01:51:57 --> 01:52:00
and the executive branch and the judicial branch.

01:52:00 --> 01:52:02
So anyway, so as a consequence,

01:52:01 --> 01:52:04
democracy is always everywhere fake.

01:52:03 --> 01:52:06
There is always a ruling elite.

01:52:06 --> 01:52:09
And basically, the lesson of "The Machiavellians"

01:52:08 --> 01:52:11
is you can deny that if you want,

01:52:10 --> 01:52:12
but you're fooling yourself.

01:52:11 --> 01:52:14
The way to actually think about how to make a system work

01:52:13 --> 01:52:17
and maintain any sort of shred of freedom

01:52:16 --> 01:52:19
is to actually understand that is actually what's happening.

01:52:18 --> 01:52:21
- And lucky for us,

01:52:20 --> 01:52:25
the founders saw this and figured out a way to,

01:52:24 --> 01:52:28
given that there's going to be a ruling elite,

01:52:27 --> 01:52:30
how to create a balance of power

01:52:30 --> 01:52:32
among that elite- - Yes.

01:52:31 --> 01:52:33
- So it doesn't get out of hand.

01:52:32 --> 01:52:35
- And it was very clever. Right?

01:52:34 --> 01:52:37
And you know, some of this was based on earlier experiments.

01:52:36 --> 01:52:38
By the way, you know,

01:52:37 --> 01:52:40
these were very, very smart people, right?

01:52:39 --> 01:52:41
And so they knew tremendous amounts

01:52:40 --> 01:52:42
of like Greek and Roman history.

01:52:41 --> 01:52:44
They knew the Renaissance history, you know,

01:52:43 --> 01:52:46
"The Federalist Papers," they argue this a great length.

01:52:45 --> 01:52:48
You can read it all.

01:52:47 --> 01:52:50
You know, they ran like a one of the best seminars

01:52:49 --> 01:52:52
in world history trying to figure this out.

01:52:51 --> 01:52:54
And they went through all this and yeah.

01:52:53 --> 01:52:55
And so they thought through it very carefully,

01:52:54 --> 01:52:57
but just, I'll give you an example,

01:52:56 --> 01:52:58
which continues to be a hot topic.

01:52:57 --> 01:53:00
So, you know, one way they did it

01:52:59 --> 01:53:02
just through the three branches of government, right?

01:53:01 --> 01:53:03
Executive, legislative and judicial,

01:53:03 --> 01:53:06
sort of balance of powers.

01:53:05 --> 01:53:07
But the other way,

01:53:06 --> 01:53:08
they did it was they sort of echoing

01:53:07 --> 01:53:09
what had been done earlier.

01:53:08 --> 01:53:11
I think in the UK Parliament,

01:53:10 --> 01:53:13
they created the two different bodies

01:53:12 --> 01:53:14
of the legislature, right?

01:53:13 --> 01:53:16
And so the, you know, the House and the Senate,

01:53:15 --> 01:53:17
and as you know, the house is a portion

01:53:16 --> 01:53:20
the basis of population, and the Senate is not, right?

01:53:19 --> 01:53:22
The small states have just as many senators

01:53:21 --> 01:53:23
as the big states.

01:53:22 --> 01:53:25
And then they made the deliberate decision

01:53:24 --> 01:53:27
to have the house get reelected every two years

01:53:26 --> 01:53:29
to make it very responsive to the will of the people.

01:53:28 --> 01:53:30
And they made the decision to have the Senate

01:53:29 --> 01:53:34
get reelected every six years so that it had more buffer

01:53:33 --> 01:53:36
from the passions of the moment.

01:53:35 --> 01:53:36
But what's interesting

01:53:35 --> 01:53:38
is they didn't choose one or the other, right?

01:53:37 --> 01:53:40
They did them both.

01:53:39 --> 01:53:41
And then to get legislation passed,

01:53:40 --> 01:53:42
you have to get through both of them.

01:53:41 --> 01:53:44
And so they built in like a second layer

01:53:43 --> 01:53:46
of checks and balances.

01:53:45 --> 01:53:48
And then there's a thousand observations

01:53:47 --> 01:53:49
we could make about like

01:53:48 --> 01:53:50
how well the system is working today

01:53:49 --> 01:53:53
and like how much does it live up to the ideal

01:53:52 --> 01:53:54
and how much are we actually complying

01:53:53 --> 01:53:55
with the constitution?

01:53:54 --> 01:53:56
And you know,

01:53:55 --> 01:53:59
there's lots of open questions there, but you know,

01:53:58 --> 01:54:02
this system has survived for coming on 250 years

01:54:01 --> 01:54:04
with a country that has been spectacularly successful.

01:54:03 --> 01:54:06
But I don't think, at least, you know, I don't think any

01:54:05 --> 01:54:08
of us would trade the system for any other one.

01:54:07 --> 01:54:10
And so it's, yeah, one of the great all-time achievements.

01:54:09 --> 01:54:11
- Yeah, it's incredible.

01:54:10 --> 01:54:14
And we should say they were all pretty young relative

01:54:13 --> 01:54:16
to our current set of leaders. - They were.

01:54:15 --> 01:54:18
Many in their 20s at the time, and like super geniuses.

01:54:17 --> 01:54:20
This is one of those things where it's just like, all right,

01:54:19 --> 01:54:23
something happened where there was a group of people where,

01:54:22 --> 01:54:24
you know, nobody ever tested their IQs,

01:54:23 --> 01:54:26
but like these are Einsteins of politics.

01:54:25 --> 01:54:27
Yeah. An amazing thing.

01:54:26 --> 01:54:29
But anyway, I go through all that,

01:54:28 --> 01:54:30
which is they were very keen students

01:54:29 --> 01:54:34
of the actual mechanical practice of democracy,

01:54:33 --> 01:54:36
not fixated on what was desirable.

01:54:35 --> 01:54:39
They were incredibly focused on what would actually work,

01:54:38 --> 01:54:40
which is, you know, I think the way to think

01:54:39 --> 01:54:41
about these things.

01:54:40 --> 01:54:43
- There were engineers of sort,

01:54:42 --> 01:54:46
not the fuzzy humanity students of sort.

01:54:45 --> 01:54:49
- They were shape rotators, not word cells. (laughs)

01:54:48 --> 01:54:50
- I remember that.

01:54:49 --> 01:54:53
Wow. That meme came and went.

01:54:52 --> 01:54:54
I think you were center to them,

01:54:53 --> 01:54:55
you're center to a lot of memes.

01:54:54 --> 01:54:56
- [Marc] I was.

01:54:55 --> 01:54:59
- You're the meme dealer and the meme popularizer.

01:54:58 --> 01:55:00
- That's true.

01:54:59 --> 01:55:01
That meme I get some credit for

01:55:00 --> 01:55:02
and then the current thing is,

01:55:01 --> 01:55:03
the other one I get some credit for.

01:55:02 --> 01:55:04
I don't know that I invented either one,

01:55:03 --> 01:55:06
but I popularized them.

01:55:05 --> 01:55:07
- Take credit and run with it.

01:55:06 --> 01:55:08
- [Marc] Yep.

01:55:09 --> 01:55:12
- If we can just linger on the Machiavellians.

01:55:13 --> 01:55:16
It's a study of power and power dynamics,

01:55:15 --> 01:55:20
like you mentioned looking at the actual reality

01:55:19 --> 01:55:22
of the machinery of power.

01:55:21 --> 01:55:24
From everything you've seen now in government,

01:55:23 --> 01:55:26
but also in companies,

01:55:25 --> 01:55:29
what are some interesting things you can sort of continue

01:55:28 --> 01:55:31
to say about the dynamics of power,

01:55:30 --> 01:55:32
the jostling for power

01:55:31 --> 01:55:35
that happens inside these institutions?

01:55:34 --> 01:55:37
- Yeah, so a lot of it, you know,

01:55:36 --> 01:55:38
we already talked about this a bit with the universities,

01:55:37 --> 01:55:41
which is you can apply a Machiavellian-style lens to the...

01:55:40 --> 01:55:43
It's why I posed the question to you that I did,

01:55:42 --> 01:55:47
which is, okay, who runs the university, the trustees,

01:55:46 --> 01:55:49
the administration, the students or the faculty?

01:55:48 --> 01:55:51
And you know, the true answer is some combination

01:55:50 --> 01:55:54
of the three or of the four, plus the donors,

01:55:53 --> 01:55:55
by the way, plus the government,

01:55:54 --> 01:55:57
plus the press, et cetera, right?

01:55:56 --> 01:55:58
And so you know,

01:55:57 --> 01:56:00
there's a mechanical interpretation of that.

01:55:59 --> 01:56:02
I mean, companies operate under the exact same,

01:56:01 --> 01:56:03
you know, set of questions.

01:56:02 --> 01:56:04
Who runs a company?

01:56:03 --> 01:56:06
You know, the CEO,

01:56:05 --> 01:56:08
but like the CEO runs the company basically up to the day

01:56:07 --> 01:56:11
that either the shareholders or the management team revolt.

01:56:10 --> 01:56:13
If the shareholders revolt, it's very hard for the CEO

01:56:12 --> 01:56:14
to stay in the seat.

01:56:13 --> 01:56:15
If the management team revolts,

01:56:14 --> 01:56:17
it's very hard for the CEO to stay in the seat.

01:56:16 --> 01:56:18
By the way, if the employees revolt,

01:56:17 --> 01:56:20
it's also hard to stay in the seat.

01:56:19 --> 01:56:21
By the way, if The New York Times comes at you,

01:56:20 --> 01:56:23
it's also very hard to stay in the seat.

01:56:22 --> 01:56:24
If the Senate comes at you,

01:56:23 --> 01:56:26
it's very hard to stay in the seat.

01:56:25 --> 01:56:29
So, you know, like a reductionist version

01:56:28 --> 01:56:32
of this that is a good shorthand is who can get who fired?

01:56:33 --> 01:56:35
You know, so who has more power?

01:56:34 --> 01:56:37
You know, the newspaper columnist who makes, you know,

01:56:36 --> 01:56:38
$200,000 a year,

01:56:37 --> 01:56:41
or the CEO who makes, you know, $200 million a year.

01:56:40 --> 01:56:43
And it's like, well, I know for sure

01:56:42 --> 01:56:44
that the columnist can get the CEO fired.

01:56:43 --> 01:56:47
I've seen that happen before I have yet to see a CEO

01:56:46 --> 01:56:48
get a columnist fired.

01:56:47 --> 01:56:53
- Did anyone ever get fired from the Bill Ackman assault

01:56:53 --> 01:56:56
on journalism? (laughs)

01:56:55 --> 01:56:58
So Bill like really showed the bullshit

01:56:57 --> 01:57:00
hat happens in journalism.

01:56:59 --> 01:57:02
- No, 'cause what happens is they wear it with a...

01:57:01 --> 01:57:04
And yeah, I would say to their credit,

01:57:03 --> 01:57:05
they wear it as a badge of honor

01:57:04 --> 01:57:06
and then to their shame,

01:57:05 --> 01:57:07
they wear it as a badge of honor, right?

01:57:06 --> 01:57:10
Which is, you know, if they're doing the right thing,

01:57:09 --> 01:57:12
then they are justifiably priding themselves

01:57:11 --> 01:57:14
for standing up under pressure.

01:57:13 --> 01:57:16
But it also means that they can't respond

01:57:15 --> 01:57:17
to legitimate criticism

01:57:16 --> 01:57:19
and, you know, they're obviously terrible at that now.

01:57:19 --> 01:57:23
As I recall, he went straight to the CEO of Axel Springer

01:57:22 --> 01:57:25
that owns Insider.

01:57:24 --> 01:57:27
You know, and I happen to know the CEO,

01:57:26 --> 01:57:28
and I think he's quite a good CEO,

01:57:27 --> 01:57:29
but I like, well, this is a good example.

01:57:28 --> 01:57:33
Does the CEO Axel Springer run his own company, right?

01:57:32 --> 01:57:35
Like, well, there's a fascinating, okay,

01:57:34 --> 01:57:37
so there's a fascinating playing out right now.

01:57:36 --> 01:57:39
Not to dwell on these fires,

01:57:38 --> 01:57:42
but you see the pressure reveals things, right?

01:57:41 --> 01:57:43
And so if you've been watching what's happened

01:57:42 --> 01:57:46
with the LA Times recently, so this guy,

01:57:45 --> 01:57:47
biotech entrepreneur buys the LA Times,

01:57:46 --> 01:57:49
like whatever, eight years ago.

01:57:48 --> 01:57:52
It is just like the most radical social revolutionary thing

01:57:51 --> 01:57:53
you can possibly imagine.

01:57:52 --> 01:57:56
It's endorses every crazy left-wing radical you can imagine.

01:57:55 --> 01:57:57
It endorses Karen Bass,

01:57:56 --> 01:57:58
it endorses Gavin Newsom.

01:57:57 --> 01:58:01
It's just like a litany of all the people who, you know,

01:58:00 --> 01:58:03
are currently burning the city to the ground.

01:58:02 --> 01:58:05
It's just like endorsed every single bad person

01:58:04 --> 01:58:06
every step of the way.

01:58:05 --> 01:58:08
He's owned it the entire time.

01:58:07 --> 01:58:10
You know, for the first time,

01:58:09 --> 01:58:12
I think put his foot down right before the November election

01:58:11 --> 01:58:14
and said, we're getting...

01:58:13 --> 01:58:15
He said, "We're gonna get outta this thing

01:58:14 --> 01:58:16
where we just always endorse the Democrat."

01:58:15 --> 01:58:18
And we said, we're not endorsing, I think he said,

01:58:17 --> 01:58:19
"We're not endorsing for the presidency."

01:58:18 --> 01:58:21
And like the paper flipped out, right?

01:58:20 --> 01:58:24
It's like our billionaire backer who's...

01:58:23 --> 01:58:24
And I don't know what he spends, but like,

01:58:23 --> 01:58:27
he must be burning 50 or 100 million dollars a year out

01:58:26 --> 01:58:29
of his pocket to keep this thing running.

01:58:28 --> 01:58:33
He paid 500 million for it, which is amazing.

01:58:32 --> 01:58:36
Back when people still thought these things were businesses.

01:58:35 --> 01:58:40
And then he's probably burned another 500 million

01:58:39 --> 01:58:41
over the last decade keeping it running.

01:58:40 --> 01:58:43
And he burns probably another 50, 100 million a year

01:58:42 --> 01:58:44
to do this.

01:58:43 --> 01:58:46
And the journalists at the LA Times hate him

01:58:45 --> 01:58:48
with the fury of 1,000 suns.

01:58:47 --> 01:58:50
Like they just like absolutely freaking despise him.

01:58:49 --> 01:58:52
And they have been like attacking him

01:58:51 --> 01:58:54
and you know, the ones that can get jobs elsewhere quit

01:58:53 --> 01:58:55
and do it, and the rest just stay

01:58:54 --> 01:58:57
and say the worst, you know, most horrible things about him.

01:58:56 --> 01:58:59
And they wanna constantly run these stories attacking him.

01:58:59 --> 01:59:02
And so he has had this reaction

01:59:01 --> 01:59:04
that a lot of people in LA are having right now

01:59:03 --> 01:59:05
to this fire,

01:59:04 --> 01:59:07
and to this just like incredibly vivid collapse

01:59:06 --> 01:59:08
of leadership.

01:59:07 --> 01:59:09
And all these people

01:59:08 --> 01:59:11
that his paper head endorsed are just disasters.

01:59:11 --> 01:59:15
And he's on this tour, he's basically just,

01:59:15 --> 01:59:17
he's decided to be the boy

01:59:16 --> 01:59:19
who says the emperor has no clothes,

01:59:18 --> 01:59:21
but he's doing it to his own newspaper. (laughs)

01:59:20 --> 01:59:22
Very smart guy.

01:59:21 --> 01:59:24
He is not a press tour and he is basically saying, yeah,

01:59:23 --> 01:59:26
yes, we did all that and we endorsed all these people

01:59:25 --> 01:59:28
and it was a huge mistake and we're gonna completely change.

01:59:27 --> 01:59:31
And his paper is, you know, in a complete internal revolt.

01:59:30 --> 01:59:33
But I go through it, which is okay,

01:59:32 --> 01:59:34
now we have a very interesting question,

01:59:33 --> 01:59:36
which is who runs the LA Times?

01:59:35 --> 01:59:38
Because for the last eight years,

01:59:37 --> 01:59:41
it hasn't been him. (laughs)

01:59:40 --> 01:59:42
It's been the reporters.

01:59:42 --> 01:59:45
Now for the first time, the owner is showing up saying,

01:59:44 --> 01:59:47
"Oh, no, I'm actually in charge."

01:59:46 --> 01:59:49
And the reporters are saying, "No, you're not."

01:59:48 --> 01:59:52
And like it is freaking on.

01:59:51 --> 01:59:54
And so again, if the, the Machiavellian's mindset on this

01:59:53 --> 01:59:57
like, okay, how is power actually exercised here?

01:59:56 --> 01:59:59
Can a guy who's like even super rich

01:59:58 --> 02:00:01
and super powerful who even owns his own newspaper,

02:00:00 --> 02:00:03
can he stand up to a full-scale assault,

02:00:02 --> 02:00:04
not only by his own reporters,

02:00:03 --> 02:00:06
but by every other journalism outlet

02:00:05 --> 02:00:09
who also now thinks he's the Antichrist?

02:00:08 --> 02:00:11
- And he is trying to exercise power

02:00:10 --> 02:00:12
by speaking out publicly

02:00:11 --> 02:00:14
and so that's the game of power there.

02:00:13 --> 02:00:16
- And firing people. - Firing people. Yeah.

02:00:15 --> 02:00:18
- You know, he has removed people and he has set new rules.

02:00:17 --> 02:00:20
I mean, he is now, I think at long,

02:00:19 --> 02:00:20
I think he's saying

02:00:19 --> 02:00:22
he's now at long last actually exercising prerogatives

02:00:21 --> 02:00:24
of an owner of a business,

02:00:23 --> 02:00:25
which is decide on the policies

02:00:24 --> 02:00:26
and staffing of the business.

02:00:25 --> 02:00:28
There are certain other owners of these publications

02:00:27 --> 02:00:31
that are doing similar things right now.

02:00:30 --> 02:00:33
He's the one I don't know, so he's the one I can talk about.

02:00:32 --> 02:00:34
But there are others that are going

02:00:33 --> 02:00:37
through the same thing right now.

02:00:36 --> 02:00:39
And I think it's a really interesting open question.

02:00:38 --> 02:00:41
Like, you know, in a fight between the employees

02:00:40 --> 02:00:43
and the employer, like it's not crystal clear

02:00:42 --> 02:00:44
that the employer wins that one.

02:00:43 --> 02:00:46
- And just to stay on journalism for a second,

02:00:45 --> 02:00:47
we mentioned Bill Ackman.

02:00:46 --> 02:00:51
I just wanna say put him in the category we mentioned

02:00:50 --> 02:00:53
before of a really courageous person.

02:00:52 --> 02:00:57
I don't think I've ever seen anybody so fearless

02:00:57 --> 02:01:00
in going after, you know,

02:00:59 --> 02:01:03
in following what he believes in publicly.

02:01:02 --> 02:01:05
That's courage.

02:01:05 --> 02:01:08
That several things he's done publicly

02:01:07 --> 02:01:10
has been really inspiring.

02:01:09 --> 02:01:11
Just being courageous.

02:01:10 --> 02:01:12
- What do you think is like the most impressive example?

02:01:11 --> 02:01:15
- Where he went after journalists

02:01:14 --> 02:01:18
whose whole incentive is to, like,

02:01:17 --> 02:01:23
I mean, it's like kicking the beehive or whatever,

02:01:22 --> 02:01:27
you know, what's gonna follow and do that.

02:01:26 --> 02:01:29
I mean that's why it's difficult

02:01:28 --> 02:01:32
o challenge journalistic organizations

02:01:31 --> 02:01:33
because they're going to, you know,

02:01:32 --> 02:01:35
there's just so many mechanisms they use,

02:01:34 --> 02:01:37
including like writing articles

02:01:36 --> 02:01:39
and get cited by Wikipedia and then drive the narrative

02:01:38 --> 02:01:41
and then they can get you fired,

02:01:40 --> 02:01:42
all this kind of stuff.

02:01:41 --> 02:01:47
Bill Ackman, like a bad MFer just tweets these essays

02:01:48 --> 02:01:51
and just goes after them legally

02:01:50 --> 02:01:53
and also in the public eye.

02:01:52 --> 02:01:54
And just, I don't know.

02:01:53 --> 02:01:56
That was truly inspiring.

02:01:55 --> 02:02:00
There's not many people like that out in public

02:01:59 --> 02:02:02
and hopefully that inspires not just me,

02:02:01 --> 02:02:06
but many others to be courageous themselves.

02:02:05 --> 02:02:07
- Did you know of him

02:02:06 --> 02:02:09
before he started doing this in public?

02:02:08 --> 02:02:11
- I knew of Neri, his wife,

02:02:10 --> 02:02:12
who's a brilliant researcher and scientist.

02:02:11 --> 02:02:14
And so I admire her.

02:02:13 --> 02:02:16
Looked up to her and think she's amazing.

02:02:15 --> 02:02:17
- Well, the reason I ask if you knew about Bill

02:02:16 --> 02:02:20
is because a lot of people had not heard of him before,

02:02:19 --> 02:02:21
especially like before October 7th

02:02:20 --> 02:02:23
and before some of the campaigns he's been running

02:02:22 --> 02:02:25
since in public, and with Harvard and so forth.

02:02:24 --> 02:02:27
But he was very well known

02:02:26 --> 02:02:29
in the investment world before that.

02:02:28 --> 02:02:31
So he was a famous,

02:02:30 --> 02:02:33
he was so-called activist investor for, you know,

02:02:32 --> 02:02:34
very, very successful

02:02:33 --> 02:02:37
and very widely respected for probably 30 years

02:02:37 --> 02:02:39
before now.

02:02:38 --> 02:02:41
And I bring that up because it turns out,

02:02:40 --> 02:02:42
they weren't for the most part battles

02:02:41 --> 02:02:44
that happened in kind of full public view.

02:02:43 --> 02:02:45
They weren't national stories, but in the business

02:02:44 --> 02:02:49
and investing world, the activist investor is a very,

02:02:48 --> 02:02:50
it's like in the movie "Taken."

02:02:49 --> 02:02:51
It's a very specific set of skills.

02:02:50 --> 02:02:53
(both laughing)

02:02:52 --> 02:02:54
- Yeah.

02:02:53 --> 02:02:56
- On how to like really take control

02:02:55 --> 02:02:57
of situations. - Yeah.

02:02:56 --> 02:03:00
- And how to wreck the people who you're going up against.

02:02:59 --> 02:03:04
And there's been controversy over the years

02:03:03 --> 02:03:06
on this topic, and there's too much detail to go into.

02:03:05 --> 02:03:08
But the defense of activist investing,

02:03:07 --> 02:03:10
which I think is valid is, you know, these are the guys

02:03:09 --> 02:03:12
who basically go in

02:03:11 --> 02:03:13
and take stakes in companies

02:03:12 --> 02:03:16
that are being poorly managed or under-optimized.

02:03:15 --> 02:03:17
And then generally what that means is,

02:03:16 --> 02:03:19
at least the theory is that means the existing management

02:03:18 --> 02:03:23
is become entrenched and lazy, mediocre, you know,

02:03:22 --> 02:03:24
whatever.

02:03:23 --> 02:03:26
Not, you're responding to the needs of the shareholders.

02:03:25 --> 02:03:28
Often not responding to the customers.

02:03:27 --> 02:03:31
And the activists basically go in with a minority position

02:03:30 --> 02:03:34
and then they rally support among other investors

02:03:33 --> 02:03:35
who are not activists.

02:03:34 --> 02:03:38
And then they basically show up and they force change.

02:03:37 --> 02:03:40
But they are the aggressive version of this.

02:03:39 --> 02:03:42
And I've been involved in companies

02:03:41 --> 02:03:43
that have been on the receiving end

02:03:42 --> 02:03:44
of these- - Oh-oh.

02:03:43 --> 02:03:46
- Where it is amazing how much somebody like

02:03:45 --> 02:03:48
that can exert pressure on situations

02:03:47 --> 02:03:50
even when they don't have formal control.

02:03:49 --> 02:03:54
So it would be another chess piece on the mechanical board

02:03:53 --> 02:03:55
of kind of how power gets exercised.

02:03:54 --> 02:03:57
And basically what happens is the effective analysts,

02:03:56 --> 02:03:58
a large amount of time,

02:03:57 --> 02:04:00
they end up taking over control of companies

02:03:59 --> 02:04:01
even though they never own more

02:04:00 --> 02:04:03
than like 5% of the stock.

02:04:02 --> 02:04:04
And so anyway, so it turns out with Bill's,

02:04:03 --> 02:04:05
it's such a fascinating case.

02:04:04 --> 02:04:08
'Cause he has that like complete skillset.

02:04:07 --> 02:04:09
- (laughs) Yeah.

02:04:08 --> 02:04:11
- And he has now decided to bring it to bear

02:04:10 --> 02:04:14
in areas that are not just companies.

02:04:13 --> 02:04:15
And two interesting things for that.

02:04:14 --> 02:04:17
One is, you know, some of these places, you know,

02:04:16 --> 02:04:19
and some of these battles are still ongoing,

02:04:18 --> 02:04:22
but number one, like a lot of people who run universities

02:04:21 --> 02:04:23
or newspapers are not used

02:04:22 --> 02:04:25
to being up against somebody like this.

02:04:24 --> 02:04:27
And by the way, also now with infinitely deep pockets

02:04:26 --> 02:04:29
and lots of experience in courtrooms

02:04:28 --> 02:04:30
and all the things that kind of go with that.

02:04:29 --> 02:04:32
But the other is through example,

02:04:31 --> 02:04:35
he is teaching a lot of the rest of us

02:04:34 --> 02:04:37
the activists playbook, like in real time.

02:04:36 --> 02:04:41
And so Liam Neeson skillset is getting more broadly diffused

02:04:40 --> 02:04:43
just by being able to watch and learn from him.

02:04:42 --> 02:04:45
So I think he's having a, you know,

02:04:44 --> 02:04:46
I would put him up there with Elon

02:04:45 --> 02:04:48
in terms of somebody who's really affecting

02:04:47 --> 02:04:49
how all this is playing out.

02:04:48 --> 02:04:51
- But even skillset aside just courage and-

02:04:50 --> 02:04:52
Yes, including by the way,

02:04:51 --> 02:04:54
courage to go outside of his own zone.

02:04:53 --> 02:04:56
- Yeah. - Right.

02:04:55 --> 02:04:57
You know, 'cause like he hasn't been,

02:04:56 --> 02:04:58
I'll give you an example.

02:04:57 --> 02:05:00
Like my firm venture capital firm, we have LPs.

02:04:59 --> 02:05:02
There are things that I feel like I can't do or say

02:05:01 --> 02:05:04
'cause I feel like I would be bringing, you know,

02:05:03 --> 02:05:05
I would be bringing embarrassment

02:05:04 --> 02:05:08
or other consequences to our LPs.

02:05:07 --> 02:05:11
He has investors also where he worries about that.

02:05:10 --> 02:05:12
And so a couple things.

02:05:11 --> 02:05:14
One, it's his willingness to go out a bit

02:05:13 --> 02:05:16
and risk his relationship with his own investors.

02:05:15 --> 02:05:17
But I will tell you the other thing,

02:05:16 --> 02:05:19
which is his investors... I know this for a fact.

02:05:18 --> 02:05:21
His investors have been remarkably supportive

02:05:20 --> 02:05:23
of him doing that.

02:05:22 --> 02:05:24
'Cause as it turns out,

02:05:23 --> 02:05:26
a lot of them actually agree with him.

02:05:25 --> 02:05:28
And so it is the same thing he does

02:05:27 --> 02:05:30
in the his activism campaigns.

02:05:29 --> 02:05:31
He is able to be the tip of the spear on something

02:05:30 --> 02:05:34
that actually a lot more people agree with.

02:05:33 --> 02:05:34
- Yeah.

02:05:33 --> 02:05:36
It turns out if you have truth behind you,

02:05:35 --> 02:05:38
it helps.

02:05:37 --> 02:05:39
- And just again, our, you know,

02:05:38 --> 02:05:42
how I started is a lot of people are just fed up.

02:05:41 --> 02:05:44
- You've been spending a bunch of time in Mar-a-Lago,

02:05:43 --> 02:05:47
in Palm Beach helping the new administration in many ways,

02:05:46 --> 02:05:50
including interviewing people who might join.

02:05:49 --> 02:05:54
So what's your general sense about the talent,

02:05:53 --> 02:05:55
about the people who are coming in

02:05:54 --> 02:05:57
to the new administration?

02:05:56 --> 02:05:58
- So I should start by saying

02:05:57 --> 02:06:00
I'm not a member of the new administration.

02:06:01 --> 02:06:04
I'm not like in the room when a lot of these people

02:06:03 --> 02:06:04
are being selected.

02:06:03 --> 02:06:06
- I believe you said unpaid intern.

02:06:05 --> 02:06:07
- I'm an unpaid intern.

02:06:06 --> 02:06:10
So I'm a volunteer and I, you know, when helpful,

02:06:09 --> 02:06:13
but I'm not making the decisions, nor am I in a position to,

02:06:12 --> 02:06:14
you know, speak for the administration.

02:06:13 --> 02:06:15
So I don't wanna say anything

02:06:14 --> 02:06:16
that would cause people to think I'm doing that.

02:06:15 --> 02:06:18
It's a very unusual situation, right?

02:06:17 --> 02:06:19
Where you had an incumbent president

02:06:18 --> 02:06:20
and then you had a four-year gap

02:06:19 --> 02:06:21
where he is outta office,

02:06:20 --> 02:06:23
and then you have him coming back, right?

02:06:22 --> 02:06:25
And as you'll recall, there was a fair amount

02:06:24 --> 02:06:28
of controversy over the end of the first term.

02:06:27 --> 02:06:29
- Oh, yeah.

02:06:28 --> 02:06:32
- The specific concern was, you know,

02:06:31 --> 02:06:33
the first Trump administration,

02:06:32 --> 02:06:34
you know, they will all say this is like,

02:06:33 --> 02:06:36
they didn't come in with a team, right?

02:06:35 --> 02:06:37
So you know, they didn't come in with a team

02:06:36 --> 02:06:39
and most of the sort of institutional base

02:06:38 --> 02:06:41
of the Republican Party were Bush Republicans.

02:06:41 --> 02:06:43
And many of them had become never Trumpers.

02:06:42 --> 02:06:46
And so they had a hard time putting the team together.

02:06:45 --> 02:06:46
And then by the way,

02:06:45 --> 02:06:48
they had a hard time getting people confirmed.

02:06:47 --> 02:06:49
And so if you talk to the people

02:06:48 --> 02:06:50
who were there in the first term,

02:06:49 --> 02:06:52
it took them 2 to 3 years to kind of even get

02:06:51 --> 02:06:54
he government in place.

02:06:53 --> 02:06:57
And then they basically only had the government in place,

02:06:56 --> 02:06:59
you know, for basically like 18 months, and then COVID hit.

02:06:58 --> 02:07:01
You know, and then sort of the aftermath and everything,

02:07:00 --> 02:07:03
and all the drama and headlines and everything.

02:07:02 --> 02:07:05
And so the concern, you know,

02:07:04 --> 02:07:06
including from some various smart people

02:07:05 --> 02:07:07
in the last two years has been, boy,

02:07:06 --> 02:07:09
if Trump gets a second term,

02:07:08 --> 02:07:11
is he gonna be able to get a team that

02:07:10 --> 02:07:12
is as good as the team he had last time

02:07:11 --> 02:07:14
or a team that is actually not as good?

02:07:13 --> 02:07:15
'Cause maybe people got burned out.

02:07:14 --> 02:07:17
Maybe they're more cynical now.

02:07:16 --> 02:07:19
Maybe they're not willing to go through the drama.

02:07:18 --> 02:07:21
By the way, a lot of people in the first term came under,

02:07:20 --> 02:07:23
like, you know, with their own withering legal assaults,

02:07:22 --> 02:07:24
and, you know, some of them went to prison

02:07:23 --> 02:07:27
and like, you know, a lot of stuff happened,

02:07:26 --> 02:07:29
lots of investigations, lots of legal fees,

02:07:28 --> 02:07:32
lots of bad press, lots of debanking, by the way.

02:07:31 --> 02:07:35
A lot of the officials in the first Trump term got debanked,

02:07:36 --> 02:07:40
including the president's wife and son.

02:07:39 --> 02:07:41
- Yeah, I heard you tell that story.

02:07:40 --> 02:07:42
