That's insane.

02:07:41 --> 02:07:43
That's just insane.

02:07:42 --> 02:07:44
- In the wake of the first term, yes.

02:07:43 --> 02:07:47
We now take out spouses and children with our ring of power.

02:07:48 --> 02:07:50
And so there's like this legitimate question

02:07:49 --> 02:07:51
as to like, whether, okay,

02:07:50 --> 02:07:53
what will the team for the second term look like?

02:07:52 --> 02:07:55
And at least what I've seen

02:07:54 --> 02:07:56
and what you're seeing with the appointments

02:07:55 --> 02:07:58
is it looks much, much better.

02:07:57 --> 02:08:00
First of all, it just looks better than the first term

02:07:59 --> 02:08:01
and not 'cause the people in the first term

02:08:00 --> 02:08:02
were not necessarily good,

02:08:01 --> 02:08:04
but just you just have this like,

02:08:03 --> 02:08:06
influx of like incredibly capable people that have shown up

02:08:05 --> 02:08:08
that wanna be part of this,

02:08:07 --> 02:08:10
and you just didn't have that the first time.

02:08:09 --> 02:08:12
And so they're just drawing on a much deeper,

02:08:11 --> 02:08:14
richer talent pool than they had the first time.

02:08:13 --> 02:08:16
And they're drawing on people who know what the game is.

02:08:15 --> 02:08:18
Like they're drawing on people now who know

02:08:17 --> 02:08:20
hat is gonna happen and they're still willing to do it.

02:08:19 --> 02:08:22
And so they're gonna get, I think, you know,

02:08:21 --> 02:08:24
some of the best people from the first term,

02:08:23 --> 02:08:25
but they're bringing in a lot of people

02:08:24 --> 02:08:28
who they couldn't get the first time around.

02:08:28 --> 02:08:30
And then second is there's a bunch of people,

02:08:29 --> 02:08:31
including people in the first term

02:08:30 --> 02:08:33
where they're just 10 years older.

02:08:32 --> 02:08:35
And so they went through the first term

02:08:34 --> 02:08:37
and they just learned how everything works.

02:08:36 --> 02:08:38
Or they're young people

02:08:37 --> 02:08:39
who just had a different point of view

02:08:38 --> 02:08:40
and now they're 10 years older

02:08:39 --> 02:08:43
and they're ready to go serving government.

02:08:42 --> 02:08:45
And so there's a generational shift happening.

02:08:44 --> 02:08:47
And actually one of the interesting things about the team

02:08:46 --> 02:08:49
that's forming up is it's remarkably young.

02:08:48 --> 02:08:51
Some of the cabinet members, and then many of the second

02:08:50 --> 02:08:54
and third level people are like in their 30s and 40s,

02:08:53 --> 02:08:57
you know, which is a big change from the gerontocracy that,

02:08:56 --> 02:08:59
you know, we've been under for the last 30 years.

02:08:58 --> 02:09:01
And so I think the caliber has been outstanding, you know,

02:09:00 --> 02:09:04
and we could sit here and list tons and tons of people,

02:09:03 --> 02:09:06
but like, you know, the people who are running, you know,

02:09:05 --> 02:09:06
it's everything from the people

02:09:05 --> 02:09:09
who are running all the different departments at HHS.

02:09:08 --> 02:09:10
It's the people running, you know,

02:09:09 --> 02:09:12
the number two at the Pentagon is Steve Feinberg,

02:09:11 --> 02:09:14
who's just like an incredible legend

02:09:13 --> 02:09:17
of private equity, incredible capable guy.

02:09:16 --> 02:09:19
We've got, actually two of my partners are going in

02:09:18 --> 02:09:21
who I both think are amazing.

02:09:20 --> 02:09:23
Yeah, like many, many parts of the government,

02:09:22 --> 02:09:26
the people are like, really impressive.

02:09:25 --> 02:09:28
- Well, I think one of the concerns

02:09:27 --> 02:09:33
is actually that given the human being of Donald Trump,

02:09:33 --> 02:09:37
that there would be more tendency towards,

02:09:36 --> 02:09:41
let's say favoritism versus meritocracy.

02:09:40 --> 02:09:45
That there's kind of circles of sycophancy that form.

02:09:44 --> 02:09:50
And if you're be able to be loyal

02:09:49 --> 02:09:51
and never oppose

02:09:50 --> 02:09:55
and just be basically suck up to the president,

02:09:54 --> 02:09:56
that you'll get a position.

02:09:55 --> 02:09:57
So that's one of the concerns.

02:09:56 --> 02:10:00
And I think you're in a good position to speak to the degree

02:09:59 --> 02:10:05
that's happening versus hiring based on merit

02:10:04 --> 02:10:07
and just getting great teams.

02:10:06 --> 02:10:09
- Yeah, so look, I just start by saying any leader

02:10:08 --> 02:10:12
at that level, by the way, any CEO,

02:10:11 --> 02:10:13
there's always some risk of that, right?

02:10:12 --> 02:10:14
So there's always some, you know,

02:10:13 --> 02:10:16
it's like a natural reality warps

02:10:15 --> 02:10:17
around powerful leaders.

02:10:16 --> 02:10:19
And so there's always some risk to that.

02:10:18 --> 02:10:20
Of course, the good and powerful leaders

02:10:19 --> 02:10:21
are, you know, very aware of that.

02:10:20 --> 02:10:23
And Trump, at this point in his life,

02:10:22 --> 02:10:24
I think is highly aware of that,

02:10:23 --> 02:10:25
at least in my interactions with him.

02:10:24 --> 02:10:27
Like he definitely seems very aware of that.

02:10:28 --> 02:10:30
So that's one thing.

02:10:29 --> 02:10:31
I would just say that the, I think the way to look at that,

02:10:30 --> 02:10:32
I mean, and look, like I said,

02:10:31 --> 02:10:34
I don't wanna predict what's gonna happen

02:10:33 --> 02:10:35
once this whole thing starts unfolding,

02:10:34 --> 02:10:37
but I would just say that it's again,

02:10:36 --> 02:10:38
the caliber of the people

02:10:37 --> 02:10:39
who are showing up and getting the jobs,

02:10:38 --> 02:10:40
and then the fact that these are

02:10:39 --> 02:10:43
some of the most accomplished people in the business world

02:10:43 --> 02:10:46
and in the medical field.

02:10:45 --> 02:10:46
I just, you know,

02:10:45 --> 02:10:49
Jay Bhattacharya coming in to run NIH,

02:10:48 --> 02:10:51
so I was actually, I was part of the interview team

02:10:50 --> 02:10:53
for a lot of the HHS folks.

02:10:52 --> 02:10:54
- Nice.

02:10:53 --> 02:10:55
Jay's amazing.

02:10:54 --> 02:10:56
Oh, I was so happy to see that.

02:10:55 --> 02:10:57
- So I literally got, this is a story.

02:10:56 --> 02:10:59
I got to the transition office for one of the days

02:10:58 --> 02:11:00
of the HHS interviews,

02:10:59 --> 02:11:02
and I was on one of the interview interviewing teams,

02:11:01 --> 02:11:03
and they gave us, I didn't know who the candidates were,

02:11:02 --> 02:11:05
and they gave us the sheet in the beginning,

02:11:04 --> 02:11:07
and I go down the sheet and I saw Jay's name, and I, like,

02:11:06 --> 02:11:09
I almost physically fell outta my chair.

02:11:08 --> 02:11:10
- Yeah.

02:11:09 --> 02:11:11
- And I was just like...

02:11:10 --> 02:11:14
(laughs) You know, and I happen to know Jay,

02:11:14 --> 02:11:16
and I respect him enormously.

02:11:15 --> 02:11:17
And then he proved himself under this,

02:11:16 --> 02:11:19
like, talk about a guy who proved himself

02:11:18 --> 02:11:20
under extraordinary pressure-

02:11:19 --> 02:11:21
Yeah. - Over the last five years.

02:11:20 --> 02:11:25
- And then go radical under the pressure.

02:11:24 --> 02:11:28
He maintained balance and thoughtfulness and depth.

02:11:27 --> 02:11:29
I mean, incredibly-

02:11:28 --> 02:11:32
Very serious, very analytical, very applied.

02:11:32 --> 02:11:34
And yes, 100%.

02:11:33 --> 02:11:35
Tested under pressure came out.

02:11:34 --> 02:11:38
Like the more people look back at what he said and did,

02:11:37 --> 02:11:40
and you know, he's not, you know, none of us are perfect,

02:11:39 --> 02:11:44
but like overwhelmingly insightful

02:11:43 --> 02:11:45
throughout that whole period.

02:11:44 --> 02:11:47
And, you know, we would all be much better off today

02:11:46 --> 02:11:50
had he been in charge of the response.

02:11:49 --> 02:11:52
And so just like an incredibly capable guy.

02:11:51 --> 02:11:53
And look, and then he learned from all that, right?

02:11:52 --> 02:11:55
He learned a lot in the last five years.

02:11:54 --> 02:11:58
And so the idea that somebody like that could be head of NIH

02:11:57 --> 02:11:59
as compared to the people

02:11:58 --> 02:12:01
we've had is just like breathtakingly.

02:12:00 --> 02:12:03
It's just a gigantic upgrade.

02:12:02 --> 02:12:05
You know, and then Marty Makary coming in to run FDA,

02:12:04 --> 02:12:07
exact same thing.

02:12:06 --> 02:12:09
The guy coming to run a CDC, exact same thing.

02:12:10 --> 02:12:13
I mean, I've been spending time with Dr. Oz,

02:12:12 --> 02:12:15
so, you know, and I'm not, like, again,

02:12:14 --> 02:12:16
I'm not on these teams.

02:12:15 --> 02:12:17
I'm not in the room.

02:12:16 --> 02:12:18
But like, I've been spending enough time trying

02:12:17 --> 02:12:21
to help that, like his level of insight

02:12:20 --> 02:12:24
into the healthcare system, it's like astounding.

02:12:23 --> 02:12:26
And it comes from being a guy who's been like in the middle

02:12:25 --> 02:12:27
of the whole thing and been talking

02:12:26 --> 02:12:28
to people about this stuff and working on it

02:12:27 --> 02:12:30
and serving as a doctor himself

02:12:29 --> 02:12:32
and in medical systems for, you know, his entire life.

02:12:31 --> 02:12:33
And it's just like, you know,

02:12:32 --> 02:12:36
he's like a walking encyclopedia on these things.

02:12:35 --> 02:12:37
And you know, very dynamic, you know,

02:12:36 --> 02:12:40
very charismatic, very smart, organized, effective.

02:12:40 --> 02:12:43
So, you know, to have somebody like that in there.

02:12:42 --> 02:12:45
And so anyway, they're just, I have like 30

02:12:44 --> 02:12:49
of these stories now across all these different positions.

02:12:49 --> 02:12:51
And then to be quite honest,

02:12:50 --> 02:12:53
you do the compare and contrast to the last four years.

02:12:52 --> 02:12:56
And it, not even, these people are not in the same ballpark.

02:12:55 --> 02:12:58
They're just like wildly better.

02:12:59 --> 02:13:01
And so it, you know,

02:13:00 --> 02:13:03
the pound for pound is maybe be the best team

02:13:02 --> 02:13:05
in the White House since, you know,

02:13:04 --> 02:13:07
I don't even know, maybe the 90s,

02:13:08 --> 02:13:12
maybe the 30s, maybe the 50s, you know,

02:13:11 --> 02:13:14
maybe Eisenhower had a team like this or something.

02:13:13 --> 02:13:17
But there's a lot of really good people in there now.

02:13:16 --> 02:13:18
- Yeah, the potential for change

02:13:17 --> 02:13:20
is certainly extremely high.

02:13:19 --> 02:13:21
Well, can you speak to DOGE?

02:13:21 --> 02:13:27
What's the most wildly successful next two years for DOGE,

02:13:27 --> 02:13:29
can you imagine?

02:13:28 --> 02:13:32
Maybe also, can you think about the trajectory

02:13:31 --> 02:13:34
that's the most likely

02:13:33 --> 02:13:37
and what kind of challenges would it be facing?

02:13:36 --> 02:13:39
- Yeah, so start by saying, again, I'm not...

02:13:38 --> 02:13:41
Disclaimer, I have to disclaimer,

02:13:40 --> 02:13:44
I'm not on DOGE, (laughs) I'm not a member of DOGE.

02:13:43 --> 02:13:46
- We should say there's about 10 lawyers in the room,

02:13:45 --> 02:13:47
they're staring.

02:13:46 --> 02:13:49
No, I'm just kidding.

02:13:48 --> 02:13:51
- Both the angels and the devils on my shoulder

02:13:50 --> 02:13:52
are- - Okay. All right, cool.

02:13:51 --> 02:13:54
- So yeah, so I'm not speaking for DOGE.

02:13:53 --> 02:13:56
I'm not in charge of DOGE. - Yeah.

02:13:55 --> 02:13:57
- Those guys are doing it, I'm not doing it.

02:13:56 --> 02:13:59
But you know, again, I'm volunteering to help

02:13:58 --> 02:14:02
as much as I can and I'm 100% supportive.

02:14:02 --> 02:14:05
Yeah, so look, I think the way to think,

02:14:04 --> 02:14:07
I mean, the basic outlines are in public, right?

02:14:06 --> 02:14:10
Which it's a time limited, you know, basically commission.

02:14:09 --> 02:14:12
It's not a formal government agency.

02:14:11 --> 02:14:14
It's a, you know, time limited, 18 month.

02:14:15 --> 02:14:17
In terms of implementation,

02:14:16 --> 02:14:19
it will advise the executive branch, right?

02:14:18 --> 02:14:21
And so the implementation will happen through

02:14:20 --> 02:14:23
the White House.

02:14:22 --> 02:14:24
And the president has total attitude

02:14:23 --> 02:14:26
on what he wants to implement.

02:14:26 --> 02:14:29
And then basically what I think about it

02:14:28 --> 02:14:32
is the three kind of streams, you know, kind of target sets,

02:14:31 --> 02:14:33
and they're related, but different.

02:14:32 --> 02:14:36
So money, people and regulations.

02:14:36 --> 02:14:39
And so, you know, the headline number, they, you know,

02:14:38 --> 02:14:40
put us the $2 trillion number

02:14:39 --> 02:14:41
and there's already, you know,

02:14:40 --> 02:14:42
disputes over that and whatever,

02:14:41 --> 02:14:44
and there's whole question there.

02:14:43 --> 02:14:45
But then there's the people thing.

02:14:44 --> 02:14:47
And the people thing is interesting 'cause you get

02:14:46 --> 02:14:51
into these very kind of fascinating questions.

02:14:50 --> 02:14:53
And I've been doing this, I won't do this for you

02:14:52 --> 02:14:55
as a pop quiz, but I do this for people in government

02:14:54 --> 02:14:58
as a pop quiz, and I can stump them every time, which is, A,

02:14:57 --> 02:14:59
how many federal agencies are there?

02:14:59 --> 02:15:03
And the answer is somewhere between 450 and 520.

02:15:02 --> 02:15:05
And nobody's quite sure.

02:15:04 --> 02:15:06
And then the other is

02:15:05 --> 02:15:08
how many people work for the federal government?

02:15:07 --> 02:15:10
And the answer is, you know, something on the order,

02:15:09 --> 02:15:12
I forget, but like 4 million full-time employees

02:15:11 --> 02:15:14
and maybe up to 20 million contractors,

02:15:13 --> 02:15:15
and nobody's quite sure.

02:15:15 --> 02:15:19
And so there's a large people component to this.

02:15:18 --> 02:15:21
And then by the way, there's a related component to that,

02:15:20 --> 02:15:23
which is how many of them are actually in the office?

02:15:22 --> 02:15:25
And the answer is not many,

02:15:24 --> 02:15:27
most of the federal buildings are still empty, right?

02:15:26 --> 02:15:29
And then there's questions of like, are people,

02:15:28 --> 02:15:30
you know, working from home?

02:15:29 --> 02:15:32
Or are we actually working from home?

02:15:31 --> 02:15:33
So there's the people dimension,

02:15:32 --> 02:15:35
and of course, the money and the people are connected.

02:15:34 --> 02:15:36
And then there's the third,

02:15:35 --> 02:15:37
which is the regulation thing, right?

02:15:36 --> 02:15:40
And I described earlier how basically our system

02:15:39 --> 02:15:42
of government is much more now based on regulations

02:15:41 --> 02:15:43
than legislation, right?

02:15:42 --> 02:15:46
Most of the rules that we all live under

02:15:45 --> 02:15:48
are not from a bill that went through Congress.

02:15:47 --> 02:15:50
They're from an agency that, that created a regulation.

02:15:49 --> 02:15:52
That turns out to be very, very important.

02:15:51 --> 02:15:54
So one is, Elon have already described,

02:15:53 --> 02:15:55
we wanna do the DOGE

02:15:54 --> 02:15:57
wants to do broad-based regulatory relief,

02:15:56 --> 02:15:58
and Trump has talked about this,

02:15:57 --> 02:16:00
and basically get the government off people's backs

02:15:59 --> 02:16:01
and liberate the American people

02:16:00 --> 02:16:03
to be able to do things again.

02:16:02 --> 02:16:04
So that's part of it.

02:16:03 --> 02:16:05
But there's also something else that's happened,

02:16:04 --> 02:16:06
which is very interesting,

02:16:05 --> 02:16:08
which was there were a set of Supreme Court decisions

02:16:07 --> 02:16:12
about two years ago that went directly after the idea

02:16:11 --> 02:16:16
that the executive branch can create regulatory agencies

02:16:15 --> 02:16:17
and issue regulations

02:16:16 --> 02:16:18
and enforce those regulations

02:16:17 --> 02:16:20
without corresponding congressional legislation.

02:16:20 --> 02:16:25
And most of the federal government that exists today,

02:16:24 --> 02:16:28
including most of the departments and most of the rules,

02:16:27 --> 02:16:30
and most of the money, and most of the people,

02:16:29 --> 02:16:33
most of it is not enforcing laws that Congress passed.

02:16:32 --> 02:16:35
Most of it is regulation.

02:16:34 --> 02:16:36
And the Supreme Court basically said,

02:16:35 --> 02:16:39
"Large parts, you know,

02:16:38 --> 02:16:41
large to maybe all of that regulation

02:16:40 --> 02:16:43
that did not directly result from a bill

02:16:42 --> 02:16:45
that went through Congress, the way that the cartoon said

02:16:44 --> 02:16:49
that it should, that may not actually be legal.

02:16:48 --> 02:16:52
Now, the previous White House, of course,

02:16:51 --> 02:16:54
was super in favor of big government.

02:16:53 --> 02:16:56
They had no desire to, they did nothing based on this.

02:16:55 --> 02:16:57
They didn't, you know, pull anything back in.

02:16:56 --> 02:17:01
But the new regime, if they choose to, could say, "Look,

02:17:00 --> 02:17:03
the thing that we're doing here is not,

02:17:02 --> 02:17:05
you know, challenging the laws,

02:17:04 --> 02:17:07
we're actually complying with the Supreme Court decision

02:17:06 --> 02:17:10
that basically says we have to unwind a lot of this,

02:17:09 --> 02:17:12
and we have to unwind the regulations,

02:17:11 --> 02:17:13
which are no longer legal, constitutional,

02:17:12 --> 02:17:15
we have to unwind the spend

02:17:14 --> 02:17:16
and we have to unwind the people."

02:17:16 --> 02:17:19
And that's how you get from,

02:17:18 --> 02:17:20
basically you connect the thread from the regulation part

02:17:19 --> 02:17:23
back to the money part, back to the people part.

02:17:22 --> 02:17:25
They have work going on all three of these threads.

02:17:24 --> 02:17:27
They have, I would say, incredibly creative ideas

02:17:26 --> 02:17:28
on how to deal with this.

02:17:29 --> 02:17:31
I know lots of former government people

02:17:30 --> 02:17:33
who 100% of them are super cynical on this topic.

02:17:32 --> 02:17:35
And they're like, "This is impossible.

02:17:34 --> 02:17:36
This could never possibly work."

02:17:35 --> 02:17:38
And I'm like, "Well, I can't tell you

02:17:37 --> 02:17:42
what the secret plans are, but like blow my mind."

02:17:41 --> 02:17:44
And all three of those, like, they have ideas

02:17:43 --> 02:17:47
that are like really quite amazing as you'd expect from,

02:17:46 --> 02:17:48
you know, from the people involved.

02:17:47 --> 02:17:51
And so over the course of the next few months, you know,

02:17:50 --> 02:17:52
that'll start to become visible.

02:17:51 --> 02:17:54
And then the final thing I would say is,

02:17:54 --> 02:17:57
this is going to be very different than attempts,

02:17:56 --> 02:18:01
like there have been other programs like this in the past.

02:18:00 --> 02:18:03
The Clinton-Gore administration had one,

02:18:02 --> 02:18:06
and then there were others before that, Reagan had one.

02:18:05 --> 02:18:09
The difference is this time, their social media.

02:18:09 --> 02:18:12
And so there has never been like,

02:18:13 --> 02:18:16
it's interesting, one of the reasons people in Washington

02:18:15 --> 02:18:18
are so cynical is because they know all the bullshit.

02:18:17 --> 02:18:22
Like they know all the bad spending and all the bad rules

02:18:21 --> 02:18:24
and all the, like, you know, I mean, look,

02:18:23 --> 02:18:25
we're adding a trillion dollars to the national debt

02:18:24 --> 02:18:27
every 100 days right now.

02:18:27 --> 02:18:30
And that's compounding, and it's now passing the size

02:18:29 --> 02:18:32
of the defense department budget, and it's compounding,

02:18:31 --> 02:18:34
and it's pretty soon it's gonna be adding a trillion dollars

02:18:33 --> 02:18:35
every 90 days,

02:18:34 --> 02:18:36
and then it's gonna be adding a trillion dollars

02:18:35 --> 02:18:37
every 80 days,

02:18:36 --> 02:18:39
and then it's gonna be a trillion dollars every 70 days.

02:18:38 --> 02:18:40
And then if this doesn't get fixed,

02:18:39 --> 02:18:42
at some point we enter a hyper-inflationary spiral

02:18:41 --> 02:18:47
and we become Argentina or Brazil and (indistinct), right?

02:18:46 --> 02:18:49
And so, like everybody in DC knows that something

02:18:48 --> 02:18:52
has to be done, and then everybody in DC knows for a fact

02:18:51 --> 02:18:55
hat it's impossible to do anything, right?

02:18:54 --> 02:18:55
They know all the problems

02:18:54 --> 02:18:57
and they also know the sheer impossibility of fixing it.

02:18:56 --> 02:19:00
But I think what they're not taking into account,

02:18:59 --> 02:19:01
that what the critics

02:19:00 --> 02:19:04
are not taking into account is these guys can do this

02:19:03 --> 02:19:06
in the full light of day and they can do it on social media.

02:19:05 --> 02:19:08
They can completely bypass the press,

02:19:07 --> 02:19:10
they can completely bypass the cynicism,

02:19:09 --> 02:19:13
they can expose any element of, you know, unconstitutional

02:19:12 --> 02:19:14
or, you know, silly government spending.

02:19:13 --> 02:19:16
They can run victory lapse every single day

02:19:15 --> 02:19:17
on what they're doing.

02:19:16 --> 02:19:19
They can bring the people into the process.

02:19:18 --> 02:19:21
And again, if you think about it,

02:19:20 --> 02:19:23
this goes back to our Machiavellian structure,

02:19:22 --> 02:19:25
which is if you think about,

02:19:24 --> 02:19:28
again, you've got democracy, oligarchy, monarchy,

02:19:27 --> 02:19:31
rule of the many, rule of the few, rule of the one.

02:19:30 --> 02:19:32
You could think about what's happening here as a little bit

02:19:31 --> 02:19:33
of a sandwich, right?

02:19:32 --> 02:19:36
Which is, we don't have a monarch,

02:19:35 --> 02:19:38
but we have a president, rule of the one with some power.

02:19:37 --> 02:19:41
And then we have the people who can't organize,

02:19:40 --> 02:19:43
but they can be informed and they can be aware

02:19:42 --> 02:19:44
and they can express themselves

02:19:43 --> 02:19:46
through voting and polling, right?

02:19:45 --> 02:19:48
And so there's a sandwich happening right now,

02:19:47 --> 02:19:49
is a way to think about it,

02:19:48 --> 02:19:52
which is you've got basically (indistinct)

02:19:51 --> 02:19:53
if you got rule of one combining

02:19:52 --> 02:19:54
with the rule of many, right?

02:19:53 --> 02:19:56
And rule of many is they do get to vote, right?

02:19:55 --> 02:19:58
The people do get to vote basically.

02:19:57 --> 02:20:01
And then essentially Congress as in this sort

02:20:00 --> 02:20:02
of permanent bureaucratic class in Washington

02:20:01 --> 02:20:04
as the oligarchy in the middle.

02:20:03 --> 02:20:07
And so the White House plus the people,

02:20:06 --> 02:20:09
I think have the power to do all kinds of things here,

02:20:08 --> 02:20:12
and I think that would be the way I would wash it.

02:20:11 --> 02:20:14
- The transparency.

02:20:13 --> 02:20:19
I mean, Elon, just by who he is incentivized

02:20:19 --> 02:20:22
to be transparent and show the bullshit in the system

02:20:21 --> 02:20:24
and to celebrate the victories.

02:20:23 --> 02:20:26
So it's gonna be so exciting.

02:20:25 --> 02:20:29
I mean, honestly, it just makes government more exciting,

02:20:28 --> 02:20:32
which is a win for everybody.

02:20:31 --> 02:20:34
- These people are spending our money.

02:20:33 --> 02:20:35
- [Lex] Yeah.

02:20:34 --> 02:20:39
- These people have enormous contempt for the taxpayer.

02:20:38 --> 02:20:40
Okay, here's the thing you hear in Washington,

02:20:39 --> 02:20:41
here's one of the things.

02:20:40 --> 02:20:42
So the first thing you hear is, "This is impossible,

02:20:41 --> 02:20:43
they'll be able to do nothing."

02:20:42 --> 02:20:45
And then, yeah, I walk them through this and they're like,

02:20:44 --> 02:20:47
it starts to dawn on them that this is a new kind of thing.

02:20:46 --> 02:20:49
And then they're like, "Well, it doesn't matter

02:20:48 --> 02:20:51
because all the money is in entitlements and the debt

02:20:50 --> 02:20:52
and the military."

02:20:51 --> 02:20:54
And so like, yeah, you've got like this silly,

02:20:53 --> 02:20:58
fake whatever, you know, NPR funding or whatever,

02:20:57 --> 02:21:00
and like, it's a rounding error and it doesn't matter.

02:20:59 --> 02:21:01
And you look it up in the budget

02:21:00 --> 02:21:04
and it's like, whatever, $500 million or $5 billion

02:21:04 --> 02:21:08
or it's the charging stations that don't exist.

02:21:07 --> 02:21:09
It's the $40 billion of charging stations

02:21:08 --> 02:21:10
and they bill eight charging stations,

02:21:09 --> 02:21:13
or it's the broadband internet plan

02:21:12 --> 02:21:15
that delivered broadband to nobody, right?

02:21:15 --> 02:21:17
And costs you $30 billion.

02:21:16 --> 02:21:18
Like, so these boondoggles

02:21:17 --> 02:21:20
and what everybody in Washington says

02:21:19 --> 02:21:21
is that $30 billion is a rounding error

02:21:20 --> 02:21:23
on the federal budget, it doesn't matter.

02:21:22 --> 02:21:24
Who cares if they make it go away?

02:21:23 --> 02:21:27
And of course, any taxpayer is like, "What the fuck?"

02:21:26 --> 02:21:28
(Lex laughing)

02:21:27 --> 02:21:31
- What do you mean? - It's $30 billion.

02:21:30 --> 02:21:32
- Yeah. - Right.

02:21:31 --> 02:21:34
And then the experts are like,

02:21:33 --> 02:21:35
and the press is in on this too.

02:21:34 --> 02:21:37
Then the experts are like, "Well, it doesn't matter

like the one thing we know

and like we know that all this crazy stuff

02:22:55 --> 02:22:57
And like, you know,

do you remember Elon's story of what got the Amish

02:23:02 --> 02:23:05
Oh, okay.

02:23:04 --> 02:23:06

great history.

- 10,000.

02:23:54 --> 02:23:55

$10,000 per taxpayer per year.

in a positive sense at scale.

It's super exciting.

As long as there's no bureaucracy that slows you down

02:24:48 --> 02:24:52
or not huge amounts of bureaucracy

02:24:51 --> 02:24:54
that slows you down significantly.

02:24:53 --> 02:24:55
- Yeah.

02:24:54 --> 02:24:56
So here's the trick, this blew my mind

02:24:55 --> 02:24:57
'cause I was, you know,

02:24:58 --> 02:25:00
once you open the hellmouth

02:24:59 --> 02:25:01
of looking into the federal budget,

02:25:00 --> 02:25:03
you learn all kinds of things.

02:25:03 --> 02:25:07
So there is a term of art in government called impoundment.

02:25:06 --> 02:25:09
And so if you're like me,

02:25:09 --> 02:25:10
you've learned this the hard way

02:25:09 --> 02:25:12
when your car has been impounded.

02:25:11 --> 02:25:13
The government meaning of impoundment,

02:25:12 --> 02:25:16
the federal budget meaning is a different meaning.

02:25:15 --> 02:25:18
Impoundment is as follows.

02:25:17 --> 02:25:21
The constitution requires Congress to authorize money

02:25:20 --> 02:25:22
to be spent by the executive branch, right?

02:25:21 --> 02:25:24
So the executive branch goes to Congress says,

02:25:23 --> 02:25:26
"We need money X."

02:25:25 --> 02:25:27
Congress does their thing.

02:25:26 --> 02:25:29
They come back and they say, "You can have money Y."

02:25:28 --> 02:25:30
The money's appropriated from Congress,

02:25:29 --> 02:25:32
the executive branch spends it on the military

02:25:31 --> 02:25:34
or whatever they spend it on, or on roads to nowhere

02:25:33 --> 02:25:36
or charging stations to nowhere or whatever.

02:25:37 --> 02:25:39
And what's in the constitution

02:25:38 --> 02:25:41
is the Congress appropriates the money.

02:25:41 --> 02:25:45
Over the last 60 years,

02:25:44 --> 02:25:47
there has been an additional interpretation

02:25:46 --> 02:25:49
of appropriations applied by the courts

02:25:48 --> 02:25:51
and by the system, which is the executive branch

02:25:50 --> 02:25:55
not only needs Congress to appropriate X amount of money,

02:25:54 --> 02:25:57
the executive branch is not allowed to underspend.

02:25:56 --> 02:26:01
- Yeah, I'm aware of this. I'm aware of this.

02:26:00 --> 02:26:03
- And so there's this thing that happens in Washington

02:26:02 --> 02:26:05
at the end of every fiscal year, which is September 30th,

02:26:04 --> 02:26:07
and it's the great budget flush.

02:26:06 --> 02:26:09
And any remaining money that's in the system

02:26:08 --> 02:26:11
that they don't know how to productively spend,

02:26:10 --> 02:26:13
they deliberately spend it unproductively.

02:26:12 --> 02:26:14
- Yep.

02:26:13 --> 02:26:16
- To the tune of hundreds and hundreds

02:26:15 --> 02:26:17
of billions of dollars.

02:26:16 --> 02:26:18
A president

02:26:17 --> 02:26:21
hat doesn't wanna spend the money can't not spend it.

02:26:20 --> 02:26:21
- Yeah.

02:26:20 --> 02:26:25
- Like, okay, A, that's not what's in the constitution.

02:26:24 --> 02:26:26
And there's actually quite a good Wikipedia page

02:26:25 --> 02:26:27
that goes through the great debate

02:26:27 --> 02:26:29
on this that's played out in the legal world

02:26:28 --> 02:26:30
over the last 60 years.

02:26:29 --> 02:26:32
And like, basically, if you look at this

02:26:31 --> 02:26:34
with anything resembling, I think an open mind, you're like,

02:26:33 --> 02:26:36
"All right, this is not what the founders meant."

02:26:36 --> 02:26:38
And then number two, again, we go back to this thing

02:26:37 --> 02:26:43
of contempt, like, can you imagine showing up

02:26:42 --> 02:26:45
and running the government like that

02:26:44 --> 02:26:46
and thinking that you're doing the right thing

02:26:45 --> 02:26:47
and not going home at night

02:26:46 --> 02:26:48
and thinking that you've sold your soul, right?

02:26:48 --> 02:26:51
Like it's just like, I actually think you sort of had it

02:26:50 --> 02:26:53
in a really good point, which is it's even unfair

02:26:52 --> 02:26:55
to the people who have to execute this.

02:26:54 --> 02:26:56
- Yeah. - Right.

02:26:55 --> 02:26:57
'Cause it makes them bad people.

02:26:56 --> 02:27:00
And they didn't start out wanting to be bad people.

02:26:59 --> 02:27:01
And so there is stuff like this like-

02:27:00 --> 02:27:04
Yeah, everywhere. - Everywhere.

02:27:03 --> 02:27:06

I am extremely encouraged what I've seen so far.

02:27:09 --> 02:27:11
but yeah- - For sure.

02:27:10 --> 02:27:12

I'm not in this in the new administration or the government.

02:27:42 --> 02:27:46
So I would say I would describe as a complex

02:27:48 --> 02:27:49
that's maybe a little bit different

02:27:48 --> 02:27:51
han what a lot of my peers have.

02:27:53 --> 02:27:55
And I kind of thought about this, you know,

02:27:54 --> 02:27:56
I didn't say anything about it all the way through

02:27:55 --> 02:27:57
the big kind of debate over Christmas,

And so, oh, let's start with this,

where I ended up, in Silicon Valley so

02:28:19 --> 02:28:22
In past lives, I've been in DC many times arguing

02:28:24 --> 02:28:27

and very solid and very, you know,

02:28:33 --> 02:28:36
has paid off for the US in many, many ways.

02:28:35 --> 02:28:39
And we can go through it, but I think it's the argument

02:28:38 --> 02:28:39
everybody already knows, right?

02:28:38 --> 02:28:41
It's like the stock.

02:28:40 --> 02:28:42
You take any Silicon Valley person,

02:28:41 --> 02:28:43
you press the button and they tell you

why we need to brain drain the world

02:28:43 --> 02:28:45
to get more H-1B's, right?

02:28:44 --> 02:28:47
So everybody kind of gets that argument.

02:28:46 --> 02:28:49
- So it's basically, just to summarize,

02:28:48 --> 02:28:52
it's a mechanism by which you can get super smart people

02:28:51 --> 02:28:53
from the rest of the world,

02:28:52 --> 02:28:56
import them in, keep them here

02:28:55 --> 02:28:59
to increase the productivity of the US companies.

02:28:58 --> 02:29:00
- Yeah,

02:28:59 --> 02:29:02
and then it's not just good for the them

02:29:01 --> 02:29:04
and it's not just good for Silicon Valley

02:29:03 --> 02:29:05
or the tech industry, it's good for the country

02:29:04 --> 02:29:07
because they then create new companies

02:29:06 --> 02:29:08

02:29:07 --> 02:29:11

02:29:12 --> 02:29:14
than would've previously existed.

02:29:13 --> 02:29:15
And so you've got a, it's a positive,

02:29:14 --> 02:29:18
some flywheel thing where everybody wins.

02:29:17 --> 02:29:20
Like everybody wins, there are no trade-offs.

02:29:19 --> 02:29:23
It's all absolutely glorious in all directions.

02:29:24 --> 02:29:27
There cannot possibly be a moral right argument

02:29:26 --> 02:29:28
against it under any circumstances.

02:29:27 --> 02:29:30
Anybody who argues against it is obviously doing

02:29:29 --> 02:29:32
so from a position of racism,

02:29:31 --> 02:29:34
is probably a fascist and a Nazi, right?

02:29:33 --> 02:29:35
Right, I mean that- - Right.

02:29:34 --> 02:29:36
- That's the thing.

02:29:35 --> 02:29:38
And like I said, I've made that argument many times.

02:29:37 --> 02:29:39
I'm very comfortable with that argument.

02:29:38 --> 02:29:40
And then I'd also say, look, I would say number one,

02:29:39 --> 02:29:43
I believe a lot of it, I'll talk about the parts

02:29:42 --> 02:29:44
I don't believe, but I believe a lot of it.

02:29:48 --> 02:29:52
in the United Nations, like my own firm and our founders

02:29:51 --> 02:29:55

02:29:54 --> 02:29:59
and my friends, you know, are just this like amazing,

And you know, I've worked, I dunno at this point

where people from, it's gotta be,

02:30:06 --> 02:30:09
I don't know, 80 countries or something.

02:30:08 --> 02:30:10
And hopefully, over time,

it'll be, you know, the rest as well.

And then, you know, there's just the practical version

these people get educated anyway, right?

02:30:25 --> 02:30:27

02:30:26 --> 02:30:27
And so, you know,

this is the old kind of Mitt Romney, staple a green card

to every, you know, at least, you know,

Maybe the sociologist we could quibble about,

but you know, the roboticist for sure, for sure.

02:30:39 --> 02:30:41
For sure, we can all agree that-

At least I won you over on something today.

- Well, no, I'm exaggerating for effect, so-

Oh.

02:30:57 --> 02:31:00

And again, like there's a lot of merit to that, obviously.

in robotics.

What step one to being the world leader

Like, you know, very, unlike the underpants.

No, it's like a very straightforward formula, right?

- Yeah.

02:31:14 --> 02:31:16

02:31:17 --> 02:31:21

hat's sort of right underneath

at you also hear from, you know, these same people,

02:31:54 --> 02:31:57
So there's like a weird, there's little cracks in the matrix

that you can kind of stick your fingernail

into and kind of wonder about,

But like, at least, yes, our education system has its flaws.

And then underneath that is the argument that, you know,

02:32:06 --> 02:32:09
Vivek made, which is, you know,

02:32:08 --> 02:32:11
we have cultural rot in the country

and, you know, native-born people in the country,

you know, don't work hard enough,

and spend too much time watching TV and TikTok

and don't spend enough time studying differential,

02:32:19 --> 02:32:21
And again, it's like, all right, like, you know,

yeah, there's a fair amount to that.

02:32:22 --> 02:32:26
Like there's a lot of American culture that is, you know,

02:32:25 --> 02:32:29
there's a lot of frivolity, there's a lot of, you know,

02:32:28 --> 02:32:30
like, I mean, we have well-documented social issues

02:32:29 --> 02:32:31
in many fronts,

02:32:30 --> 02:32:33
many things that cut against having a culture

02:32:32 --> 02:32:35
of just like straightforward high achievement

02:32:34 --> 02:32:37
and effort and striving.

02:32:37 --> 02:32:40
Anyway, like, you know, those are the basic arguments.

02:32:39 --> 02:32:42
But then I have this kind of other side of my, you know,

02:32:41 --> 02:32:44
kind of personality and thought process, which is, well,

02:32:43 --> 02:32:46
I grew up in a small farming town

02:32:45 --> 02:32:47
of rural Wisconsin, the rural Midwest.

02:32:46 --> 02:32:50
And you know, it's interesting, there's not a lot of people

02:32:49 --> 02:32:53
who make it from rural Wisconsin to, you know, high tech.

02:32:54 --> 02:32:57
And so it's like, all right, why is that exactly, right?

02:32:56 --> 02:32:58
And then I know this, I'm an aberration.

02:32:57 --> 02:33:00
Like I was the only one from anybody I ever knew

02:32:59 --> 02:33:02
ho ever did this, right?

02:33:01 --> 02:33:03
I know what an aberration I am,

02:33:02 --> 02:33:04
and I know exactly how that aberration happened,

02:33:03 --> 02:33:06
and it's a very unusual, you know, set of steps,

02:33:06 --> 02:33:10
including, you know, many that were just luck.

02:33:09 --> 02:33:13
But like, there is in no sense a talent flow

02:33:12 --> 02:33:15
from rural Wisconsin into high tech,

02:33:14 --> 02:33:18
like, not at all.

02:33:18 --> 02:33:22
There is also like, in no sense a talent flow from the rest

02:33:21 --> 02:33:23
of the Midwest into high tech.

02:33:22 --> 02:33:25
There is no talent flow from the South into high tech.

02:33:24 --> 02:33:27
There is no flow from the Sunbelt into high tech.

02:33:26 --> 02:33:29
There's no flow from, you know,

02:33:28 --> 02:33:31
the Deep South into high tech.

02:33:30 --> 02:33:33
Just like, literally it's like the blank.

02:33:32 --> 02:33:36
There's this whole section of the country

02:33:35 --> 02:33:38
that just where the people just like for some reason

02:33:37 --> 02:33:39
don't end up in tech.

02:33:38 --> 02:33:41
Now, that's a little bit strange,

02:33:40 --> 02:33:44
'cause these are the people who put a man on the moon.

02:33:43 --> 02:33:47
These are the people who built the World War II War Machine.

02:33:46 --> 02:33:48
These are the people,

02:33:47 --> 02:33:50
at least their ancestors are the people

02:33:49 --> 02:33:52
who built the Second Industrial Revolution,

02:33:51 --> 02:33:53
and built the railroads

02:33:52 --> 02:33:55
and built the telephone network,

02:33:54 --> 02:33:57
and built, you know, logistics and transportation

02:33:56 --> 02:33:59
in the auto in, I mean, the auto industry was built

02:33:58 --> 02:34:00
in Cleveland and Detroit.

02:33:59 --> 02:34:03
And so at least these people's parents and grandparents

02:34:02 --> 02:34:05
and great grandparents somehow had the wherewithal

02:34:04 --> 02:34:07
to like build all of this like amazing things,

02:34:06 --> 02:34:08
invent all these things.

02:34:07 --> 02:34:10
And then there's many, many, many, many stories

02:34:09 --> 02:34:11
in the history of American invention

02:34:10 --> 02:34:14
and innovation and capitalism where you had people

02:34:13 --> 02:34:16
who grew up in the middle of nowhere, Philo Farnsworth,

02:34:15 --> 02:34:18
who invented the television, and just like, you know,

02:34:17 --> 02:34:21
tons and tons of others, endless stories like this.

02:34:20 --> 02:34:23
Now you have a, like a puzzle, right?

02:34:22 --> 02:34:24
And the conundrum, which is like, okay,

02:34:23 --> 02:34:27
like what is happening on the blank spot of the map?

02:34:26 --> 02:34:29
And then of course, you also can't help noticing

02:34:28 --> 02:34:31
that the blank spot on the map, the Midwest, the South,

02:34:30 --> 02:34:33
you've also just defined Trump country,

02:34:34 --> 02:34:36
the Trump voter base, right?

02:34:35 --> 02:34:37
And it's like, "Oh, well, that's interesting.

02:34:36 --> 02:34:39
Like how did that happen?" Right?

02:34:38 --> 02:34:41
And so either you really, really, really have

02:34:40 --> 02:34:43
to believe the very, very strong version

02:34:42 --> 02:34:44
of like the Vivek thesis

02:34:43 --> 02:34:45
or something where you have to believe that like,

02:34:44 --> 02:34:49
that basically culture, the whole sort of civilization

02:34:48 --> 02:34:50
in the middle of the country

02:34:49 --> 02:34:52
and the south of the country is so like, deeply flawed,

02:34:51 --> 02:34:54
either inherently flawed or culturally flawed,

02:34:53 --> 02:34:56
such that for whatever reason,

02:34:55 --> 02:34:58
they are not able to do the things that their, you know,

02:34:57 --> 02:35:00
parents and grandparents were able to do,

02:34:59 --> 02:35:01
and that their peers are able to do.

02:35:00 --> 02:35:03
Or something else is happening.

02:35:02 --> 02:35:04
Would you care to guess on what else is happening?

02:35:03 --> 02:35:06
- You mean what, affirmative action?

02:35:05 --> 02:35:07
- Affirmative action.

02:35:08 --> 02:35:11
Okay. (laughs) This is very, think about this,

02:35:10 --> 02:35:12
this is very entertaining, right?

02:35:11 --> 02:35:13
What are the three things that we know

02:35:12 --> 02:35:15
about affirmative action?

02:35:14 --> 02:35:17
It is absolutely 100% necessary.

02:35:18 --> 02:35:21
However, it cannot explain the success

02:35:20 --> 02:35:22
of any one individual.

02:35:22 --> 02:35:24
- Right.

02:35:23 --> 02:35:26
- Nor does it have any victims at all.

02:35:25 --> 02:35:28
- That could explain maybe disproportionate,

02:35:27 --> 02:35:30
but like, surely it doesn't explain

02:35:29 --> 02:35:33
why you're probably the only person

02:35:32 --> 02:35:35
in Silicon Valley from Wisconsin.

02:35:34 --> 02:35:37
- What educational institution in the last 60 years

02:35:36 --> 02:35:39
has wanted farm boys from Wisconsin?

02:35:38 --> 02:35:42
- But what institution rejected farm boys from Wisconsin?

02:35:41 --> 02:35:44
- All of them. - All of them.

02:35:43 --> 02:35:45
- Of course.

02:35:44 --> 02:35:46
Okay, so we know this, we know this.

02:35:45 --> 02:35:47
The reason we know this is because of the Harvard

02:35:46 --> 02:35:50
and UNC Supreme Court cases.

02:35:49 --> 02:35:52
So this was like three years ago,

02:35:51 --> 02:35:53
these were big court cases.

02:35:52 --> 02:35:55
And you know, 'cause the idea of affirmative action

02:35:54 --> 02:35:56
has been litigated for many, many, many years

02:35:55 --> 02:35:58
and through many court cases.

02:35:57 --> 02:36:00
And the Supreme Court repeatedly in the past had upheld

02:35:59 --> 02:36:03
that it was a completely legitimate thing to do.

02:36:02 --> 02:36:04
And there's basically two categories

02:36:03 --> 02:36:06
of affirmative action that like really matter, right?

02:36:05 --> 02:36:09
One is the admissions into educational institutions,

02:36:08 --> 02:36:11
and then the other is jobs, right?

02:36:10 --> 02:36:12
Getting hired.

02:36:11 --> 02:36:13
Like, those are the two biggest areas.

02:36:12 --> 02:36:15
The education one is like super potent,

02:36:14 --> 02:36:16
has been a super potent political issue

02:36:15 --> 02:36:17
for a very long time,

02:36:16 --> 02:36:19
for all, you know, people have written

02:36:18 --> 02:36:20
and talked about this for many decades.

02:36:19 --> 02:36:21
I don't need to go through it.

02:36:20 --> 02:36:23
There's many arguments for why it's important,

02:36:22 --> 02:36:25
there's many arguments as to how it could backfire.

02:36:24 --> 02:36:26
It's been this thing.

02:36:25 --> 02:36:28
But the Supreme Court upheld it for a very long time.

02:36:28 --> 02:36:30
The most recent ruling,

02:36:29 --> 02:36:32
I'm not a lawyer, I don't have the exact reference

02:36:31 --> 02:36:37
in my head, but there was a case in 2003 that said that

02:36:36 --> 02:36:40
Sandra Day O'Connor famously wrote that, you know,

02:36:39 --> 02:36:41
although it had been 30 years of affirmative action,

02:36:40 --> 02:36:44
and although it was not working remotely

02:36:43 --> 02:36:47
as it had been intended, she said that, you know,

02:36:46 --> 02:36:49
well, basically we need to try it for another 25 years.

02:36:48 --> 02:36:51
But she said basically as a message

02:36:50 --> 02:36:52
to future Supreme Court justices,

02:36:51 --> 02:36:55
if it hasn't resolved basically the issues it's intended

02:36:54 --> 02:36:56
to resolve within 25 years,

02:36:55 --> 02:36:58
then we should probably call it off.

02:36:57 --> 02:37:01
By the way, we're coming up on the 25 years, right?

02:37:00 --> 02:37:03
It's a couple years away.

02:37:02 --> 02:37:05
The Supreme Court just had these cases is,

02:37:04 --> 02:37:07
it's a Harvard case

02:37:06 --> 02:37:08
and I think a University of North Carolina case.

02:37:07 --> 02:37:10
And what's interesting about those cases

02:37:09 --> 02:37:12
is the lawyers in those cases put a tremendous amount

02:37:11 --> 02:37:14
of evidence into the record

02:37:13 --> 02:37:18
of how the admissions decisions actually happen at Harvard

02:37:17 --> 02:37:19
and happen at UNC.

02:37:18 --> 02:37:22
And it is like every bit as cartoonishly garish

02:37:21 --> 02:37:25
and racist as you could possibly imagine,

02:37:24 --> 02:37:28
because it's a ring of power.

02:37:27 --> 02:37:30
And if you're an admissions officer at a private university

02:37:29 --> 02:37:31
or an administrator,

02:37:30 --> 02:37:33
you have unlimited power to do what you want,

02:37:32 --> 02:37:36
and you can justify any of it under any of these rules

02:37:35 --> 02:37:38
or systems.

02:37:37 --> 02:37:40
And up until these cases, it had been a black box

02:37:39 --> 02:37:42
where you didn't have to explain yourself

02:37:41 --> 02:37:42
and show your work.

02:37:41 --> 02:37:44
And what the Harvard and UNC cases did

02:37:43 --> 02:37:47
is they basically required showing the work.

02:37:46 --> 02:37:49
And there was like all kinds of like phenomenal detail.

02:37:48 --> 02:37:51
I mean, number one is there were text messages in there

02:37:50 --> 02:37:53
that will just curl your hair of people,

02:37:52 --> 02:37:54
of students being spoken of

02:37:53 --> 02:37:56
and just like crude racial stereotypes

02:37:55 --> 02:37:58
that would just make you want to jump out the window.

02:37:57 --> 02:37:58
It's horrible stuff.

02:37:57 --> 02:38:02
But also, there was statistical information.

02:38:01 --> 02:38:03
And of course, the big statistical kicker

02:38:02 --> 02:38:05
to the whole thing is that at top institutions,

02:38:04 --> 02:38:07
it's common for different ethnic groups

02:38:06 --> 02:38:11
to have different cutoffs for SAT that are as wide

02:38:10 --> 02:38:13
as 400 points, right?

02:38:12 --> 02:38:14
So different groups.

02:38:15 --> 02:38:17
So a specifically Asians need

02:38:16 --> 02:38:21
to perform at 400 SAT points higher than other ethnicities

02:38:20 --> 02:38:23
in order to actually get admitted into these.

02:38:22 --> 02:38:24
I mean, this is not even about, I mean,

02:38:23 --> 02:38:24
white people are a part of this,

02:38:24 --> 02:38:26
but like Asians are like a very big part of this.

02:38:25 --> 02:38:28
And actually the Harvard case is actually brought

02:38:27 --> 02:38:29
by an activist on behalf

02:38:28 --> 02:38:32
of actually the Asian students who were being turned away.

02:38:31 --> 02:38:34
And it's basically, I mean it's the cliche now

02:38:33 --> 02:38:36
in the valley and in the medical community,

02:38:35 --> 02:38:38
which is like if you want a super genius,

02:38:37 --> 02:38:39
you hire an Asian from Harvard

02:38:38 --> 02:38:42
'cause they are guaranteed to be freaking Einstein.

02:38:41 --> 02:38:43
'Cause if they weren't,

02:38:42 --> 02:38:44
they were never getting admitted, right?

02:38:43 --> 02:38:47
Almost all the qualified Asians get turned away.

02:38:47 --> 02:38:51
So they've been running this, it's a very, very explicit,

02:38:50 --> 02:38:53
very, very clear program.

02:38:52 --> 02:38:54
This, of course, has been a third rail of things

02:38:53 --> 02:38:56
that people are not supposed to discuss

02:38:55 --> 02:38:58
under any circumstances.

02:38:57 --> 02:39:00
The thing that has really changed the tenor on this

02:38:59 --> 02:39:01
I think two things.

02:39:00 --> 02:39:02
Number one, those Supreme Court cases,

02:39:01 --> 02:39:06
the Supreme Court ruled that they can no longer do that.

02:39:05 --> 02:39:07
I will tell you,

02:39:06 --> 02:39:09
I don't believe there's a single education institution

02:39:08 --> 02:39:12
in America that is conforming with the Supreme Court ruling.

02:39:11 --> 02:39:14
I think they're all flagrantly ignoring it.

02:39:13 --> 02:39:15
And we could talk about that.

02:39:14 --> 02:39:17
- Mostly 'cause of momentum probably, or what?

02:39:16 --> 02:39:19
- They are trying to make the world a better place.

02:39:18 --> 02:39:21
They're trying to solve all these social problems.

02:39:20 --> 02:39:23
They are trying to have diverse student populations.

02:39:22 --> 02:39:25
They are trying to live up

02:39:24 --> 02:39:26
to the expectations of their donors.

02:39:25 --> 02:39:28
They're trying to make their faculty happy.

02:39:27 --> 02:39:30
They are trying to have their friends

02:39:29 --> 02:39:32
and family think that they're good people.

02:39:31 --> 02:39:33
- [Lex] Right.

02:39:32 --> 02:39:35
- They're trying to have the press write nice things

02:39:34 --> 02:39:36
about them.

02:39:36 --> 02:39:39
Like it's nearly impossible for them.

02:39:38 --> 02:39:41
And you know, to be clear, like nobody has been fired

02:39:40 --> 02:39:44
from an admissions office for, you know, 25 years of prior,

02:39:43 --> 02:39:45
what we now,

02:39:44 --> 02:39:47
the Supreme Court now is ruled to be illegality.

02:39:46 --> 02:39:49
And so they're all the same people

02:39:48 --> 02:39:50
under the exact same pressures.

02:39:49 --> 02:39:52
And so like I, you know,

02:39:51 --> 02:39:54
the numbers are moving a little bit,

02:39:53 --> 02:39:55
but like,

02:39:54 --> 02:39:56
I don't know anybody in the system

02:39:55 --> 02:39:58
who thinks that they're compliant with the Supreme Court.

02:39:57 --> 02:39:59
Like who's in charge,

02:39:58 --> 02:40:01
in the rank ordering of who rules who,

02:40:00 --> 02:40:03
the university's rule of the Supreme Court way more than

02:40:02 --> 02:40:06
the Supreme Court rules the universities, right?

02:40:05 --> 02:40:07
Well, another example of that is,

02:40:06 --> 02:40:08
I think that every sitting member

02:40:07 --> 02:40:10
of the Supreme Court right now went to either Harvard

02:40:09 --> 02:40:12
or Yale, right?

02:40:11 --> 02:40:14
Like the level of incestuousness here is...

02:40:13 --> 02:40:16
Anyway, so there's that.

02:40:15 --> 02:40:18
And so this has been running for a very long time.

02:40:17 --> 02:40:20
So one is the Harvard and USC cases kind of gave up

02:40:19 --> 02:40:21
the game, number one,

02:40:20 --> 02:40:23
or at least showed what the mechanism was.

02:40:22 --> 02:40:25
And then number two, the other thing is obviously

02:40:24 --> 02:40:27
the aftermath of October 7th, right?

02:40:26 --> 02:40:30
And what we discovered was happening with Jewish applicants

02:40:29 --> 02:40:32
and what was happening at all the top institutions

02:40:31 --> 02:40:34
for Jewish applicants was they were being managed down,

02:40:33 --> 02:40:37
either being actively managed down as a percentage

02:40:37 --> 02:40:40
of the base.

02:40:39 --> 02:40:42
And let's say I've heard reports

02:40:41 --> 02:40:45
of like extremely explicit, basically plans

02:40:45 --> 02:40:47
to manage the Jewish admissions down

02:40:46 --> 02:40:50
to their representative percentage of the US population,

02:40:49 --> 02:40:51
which is 2%.

02:40:51 --> 02:40:53
And you know, there's a whole backstory here,

02:40:52 --> 02:40:54
which is 100 years ago, Jews were not admitted

02:40:53 --> 02:40:56
into a lot of these institutions,

02:40:55 --> 02:40:58
and then there was a big campaign to get them in.

02:40:57 --> 02:40:58
Once they could get in,

02:40:57 --> 02:41:01
they immediately became 30% of these institutions

02:41:00 --> 02:41:03
'cause there's so many smart, talented Jews.

02:41:02 --> 02:41:05
So it went from 0% to 30%.

02:41:04 --> 02:41:06
And then the most recent generation

02:41:05 --> 02:41:08
of leadership has been trying to get it done to 2%.

02:41:07 --> 02:41:10
And a lot of Jewish people,

02:41:09 --> 02:41:12
at least a lot of Jewish people I know sort of,

02:41:12 --> 02:41:14
they kind of knew this was happening,

02:41:13 --> 02:41:16
but they discovered it the hard way

02:41:15 --> 02:41:16
after October 7th, right?

02:41:15 --> 02:41:19
And so all of a sudden, so basically,

02:41:18 --> 02:41:21
the Supreme Court case meant

02:41:20 --> 02:41:24
hat you could address this in terms of the Asian victims.

02:41:23 --> 02:41:25
The October 7th meant

02:41:24 --> 02:41:27
hat you could address it in terms of the Jewish victims.

02:41:26 --> 02:41:29
And for sure, both of those groups

02:41:28 --> 02:41:31
are being systematically excluded, right?

02:41:30 --> 02:41:32
And then, of course,

02:41:31 --> 02:41:34
there's the thing that you basically can't talk about,

02:41:33 --> 02:41:37
which is all the white people are being excluded.

02:41:36 --> 02:41:40
And then it turns out it's also happening to Black people,

02:41:39 --> 02:41:42
and this is the thing

02:41:41 --> 02:41:45
that like blew my freaking mind when I found out about it.

02:41:44 --> 02:41:48
So I just assumed that like, this was great news

02:41:47 --> 02:41:49
for like American Blacks,

02:41:48 --> 02:41:52
because like, you know, obviously if you know Whites, Asians

02:41:51 --> 02:41:55
and Jews are being excluded, then, you know, the whole point

02:41:54 --> 02:41:57
of this in the beginning was to get the Black population up.

02:41:56 --> 02:41:59
And so this must be great for American Blacks.

02:41:58 --> 02:42:03
So then I discovered this New York Times article from 2004

02:42:02 --> 02:42:05
called Blacks are Being Admitted

02:42:04 --> 02:42:07
into Top Schools at Greater Numbers,

02:42:06 --> 02:42:09
but which ones? (laughs)

02:42:08 --> 02:42:10
- [Lex] Uh-oh.

02:42:09 --> 02:42:12
- And again, and by the way, this is in The New York Times,

02:42:11 --> 02:42:13
this is not in like, you know,

02:42:12 --> 02:42:17
whatever, National Review, this is New York Times, 2004.

02:42:16 --> 02:42:19
And the two authorities that were quoted in the story

02:42:18 --> 02:42:21
are Henry Louis Gates,

02:42:20 --> 02:42:23
who's the dean of the African-American Studies, you know,

02:42:22 --> 02:42:24
community in the United States,

02:42:23 --> 02:42:25
super brilliant guy.

02:42:24 --> 02:42:26
And then Lani Guinier,

02:42:28 --> 02:42:31
she was a potential Supreme Court appointee under,

02:42:30 --> 02:42:33
I think she was a close friend of Hillary Clinton.

02:42:32 --> 02:42:34
And there was, for a long time,

02:42:33 --> 02:42:35
she was on the shortlist for Supreme Court.

02:42:34 --> 02:42:38
So one of the top, you know, jurists,

02:42:37 --> 02:42:39
lawyers in the country,

02:42:38 --> 02:42:43
but both Black was sort of legendarily successful

02:42:42 --> 02:42:46
in the academic and legal worlds and Black.

02:42:45 --> 02:42:48
And they are quoted as the authorities in this story

02:42:47 --> 02:42:50
and the story that they tell,

02:42:49 --> 02:42:53
(laughs) it's actually very, it's amazing.

02:42:52 --> 02:42:55
And by the way, it's happening today

02:42:54 --> 02:42:57
in education institutions and it's happening in companies,

02:42:56 --> 02:42:58
and you can see it all over the place,

02:42:57 --> 02:43:03
and the government, which is at least at that time,

02:43:02 --> 02:43:06
the number was half of the Black admits

02:43:05 --> 02:43:08
into a place like Harvard were not American-born Blacks.

02:43:07 --> 02:43:10
They were foreign-born Blacks,

02:43:10 --> 02:43:16
specifically, Northern African off generally Nigerian

02:43:15 --> 02:43:19
or West Indian, right?

02:43:18 --> 02:43:21
And by the way, many Nigerians

02:43:20 --> 02:43:22
and Northern Africans have come to the US

02:43:21 --> 02:43:24
and have been very successful Nigerian-Americans

02:43:23 --> 02:43:26
as a group like way outperformed,

02:43:25 --> 02:43:28
they're, you know, this a super smart cohort of people.

02:43:27 --> 02:43:29
And then West Indian Blacks in the US

02:43:28 --> 02:43:31
are incredibly successful.

02:43:30 --> 02:43:33
Most recently, by the way, Kamala Harris,

02:43:32 --> 02:43:35
as well as Colin Powell,

02:43:34 --> 02:43:37
like just two sort of examples of that.

02:43:36 --> 02:43:39
And so basically what Henry Louis Gates

02:43:38 --> 02:43:41
and Lani Guinier said in the story is Harvard

02:43:40 --> 02:43:44
is basically struggling to either, whatever it was,

02:43:43 --> 02:43:46
identify or recruit, make successful,

02:43:45 --> 02:43:49
whatever it was, American-born native Blacks,

02:43:48 --> 02:43:52
and so therefore they were using high-skill immigration,

02:43:51 --> 02:43:55
as an escape hatch to go get Blacks from other countries.

02:43:55 --> 02:43:57
And then this was 2004

02:43:56 --> 02:44:00
when you could discuss such things.

02:43:59 --> 02:44:01
Obviously that is a topic

02:44:00 --> 02:44:02
that nobody has discussed since,

02:44:01 --> 02:44:04
it has sailed on.

02:44:03 --> 02:44:05
All of the DEI programs

02:44:04 --> 02:44:09
in the last 20 years have had this exact characteristic.

02:44:08 --> 02:44:11
There's large numbers of Black people in America

02:44:10 --> 02:44:12
who are fully aware of this and are like,

02:44:11 --> 02:44:15
"It's obviously not us that are getting these slots,

02:44:15 --> 02:44:16
we're literally competing with people

02:44:15 --> 02:44:18
who are being imported."

02:44:17 --> 02:44:20
And you know, if you believe in the basis

02:44:19 --> 02:44:21
of affirmative action, you are trying to make up

02:44:20 --> 02:44:24
for historical injustice of American Black slavery.

02:44:23 --> 02:44:26
So the idea that you're import somebody from, you know,

02:44:25 --> 02:44:30
Nigeria that never experienced that, you know,

02:44:29 --> 02:44:33
is like tremendously insulting to Black Americans.

02:44:32 --> 02:44:35
Anyway, so you can see where I'm heading with this.

02:44:34 --> 02:44:38
We have been in a 60-year social engineering experiment

02:44:37 --> 02:44:40
o exclude native-born people

02:44:39 --> 02:44:42
from the educational slots and jobs

02:44:41 --> 02:44:43
that high-skill immigration

02:44:42 --> 02:44:46
has been funneling foreigners into, right?

02:44:45 --> 02:44:48
And so it turns out it's not a victim-free thing,

02:44:47 --> 02:44:50
there's like 100%, there's victims.

02:44:49 --> 02:44:50
'Cause why?

02:44:49 --> 02:44:52
There's only so many, for sure.

02:44:51 --> 02:44:53
There's only so many education slots.

02:44:52 --> 02:44:54
And then for sure, there's only so many

02:44:53 --> 02:44:56
of these jobs, right?

02:44:55 --> 02:44:58
You know, Google only hires so many, you know,

02:44:57 --> 02:45:00
whatever level seven engineers, right?

02:44:59 --> 02:45:02
And so that's the other side of it, right?

02:45:01 --> 02:45:06
And so you're a farm boy in Wisconsin, right?

02:45:05 --> 02:45:09
You know, Black American whose ancestors arrived here,

02:45:08 --> 02:45:12
you know, on a slave ship, 300 years ago, in Louisiana

02:45:11 --> 02:45:15
or a, you know, Cambodian immigrant in, you know, the Bronx

02:45:15 --> 02:45:19
and you are a kid or a Jewish immigrant or a, you know,

02:45:18 --> 02:45:22
or from a very successful Jewish family

02:45:21 --> 02:45:24
and you know, your entire, you know, for three generations,

02:45:23 --> 02:45:26
you and your parents and grandparents went to Harvard.

02:45:25 --> 02:45:29
And what all of those groups know is the system

02:45:28 --> 02:45:32
that has been created is not for them, right?

02:45:31 --> 02:45:35
It's designed specifically to exclude them.

02:45:34 --> 02:45:36
And then what happens is

02:45:35 --> 02:45:37
all of these tech people show up in public

02:45:36 --> 02:45:40
and say, "Yeah, let's bring in more foreigners," right?

02:45:39 --> 02:45:43
And so anyway, so the short version of it is,

02:45:42 --> 02:45:44
you can't anymore, I don't think,

02:45:43 --> 02:45:48
just have the, quote, "high-skill immigration,"

02:45:47 --> 02:45:50
conversation for either education

02:45:51 --> 02:45:55
or for employment without also having the DEI conversation.

02:45:55 --> 02:45:58
And then DEI is just another word for affirmative action,

02:45:57 --> 02:46:00
so it's the affirmative action conversation.

02:45:59 --> 02:46:02
And you need to actually deal with this at substance

02:46:01 --> 02:46:03
and to see what's actually happening to people.

02:46:02 --> 02:46:05
you needed to join these topics.

02:46:05 --> 02:46:07
And I think it is much harder

02:46:06 --> 02:46:10
to make the moral claim for high-skilled immigration given

02:46:10 --> 02:46:14
the extent to which DEI took over both the education process

02:46:13 --> 02:46:17
and the hiring process. - Okay, so first of all,

02:46:16 --> 02:46:20
that was brilliantly laid out, the nuance of it.

02:46:19 --> 02:46:23
So just to understand, it's not so much a criticism

02:46:22 --> 02:46:26
of H-1B, high-skilled immigration,

02:46:25 --> 02:46:29
it's that there needs to be more people saying,

02:46:28 --> 02:46:32
yay, we need more American-born hires.

02:46:31 --> 02:46:34
- So I spent the entire Christmas holiday

02:46:33 --> 02:46:36
reading every message on this and not saying anything,

02:46:35 --> 02:46:37
and what I was...

02:46:36 --> 02:46:38
(Lex laughing)

02:46:37 --> 02:46:39
Which you know me well enough to know

02:46:38 --> 02:46:41
that's a serious level of-

02:46:40 --> 02:46:42
Yeah, that was very Zen.

02:46:41 --> 02:46:43
- Yes, thank you, thank you.

02:46:42 --> 02:46:45
No, it wasn't, there was tremendous rage

02:46:44 --> 02:46:48
on the other side of it, but I suppressed it.

02:46:48 --> 02:46:53
So I was waiting for the dog that didn't bark, right?

02:46:52 --> 02:46:55
And the dog that didn't bark was, I did not,

02:46:54 --> 02:46:56
and you tell me if you saw one,

02:46:55 --> 02:46:58
I did not see a single example

02:46:57 --> 02:46:59
of somebody pounding the table

02:46:58 --> 02:47:00
for more high-skilled immigration,

02:46:59 --> 02:47:01
who was also pounding the table

02:47:00 --> 02:47:03
to go get more smart kids who are already here

02:47:03 --> 02:47:06
into these educational institutions and into these jobs.

02:47:05 --> 02:47:08
I didn't see a single one.

02:47:07 --> 02:47:09
- That's true.

02:47:08 --> 02:47:11
I think I agree with that.

02:47:10 --> 02:47:13
There really was a divide. - But it was like literally,

02:47:12 --> 02:47:15
it was like the proponents of high-skilled immigrant.

02:47:14 --> 02:47:18
And again, this was me for a very long time.

02:47:17 --> 02:47:19
I mean, I kind of took myself by surprise on this

02:47:18 --> 02:47:22
because I was on, you know, I had the much, say,

02:47:21 --> 02:47:23
simpler version of this story for a very...

02:47:22 --> 02:47:26
And like I said, I've been in Washington many times

02:47:25 --> 02:47:27
under past presidents, lobbying for this.

02:47:26 --> 02:47:28
By the way, never made any progress,

02:47:27 --> 02:47:29
which we could talk about.

02:47:28 --> 02:47:31
Like it never actually worked.

02:47:31 --> 02:47:34
But you know, I've been on the other side of this one,

02:47:33 --> 02:47:35
but I was literally sitting there being like, all right,

02:47:34 --> 02:47:38
which of these like super geniuses who, you know,

02:47:37 --> 02:47:40
many of whom by the way are very, you know,

02:47:39 --> 02:47:41
successful high-skilled immigrants

02:47:40 --> 02:47:43
or children of high-skilled immigrants, you know,

02:47:42 --> 02:47:45
which of these super geniuses are gonna like,

02:47:44 --> 02:47:48
say actually we have this like incredible talent source here

02:47:47 --> 02:47:49
in the country?

02:47:48 --> 02:47:50
Which again, to be clear,

02:47:49 --> 02:47:50
I'm not talking about white people.

02:47:49 --> 02:47:52
I'm talking about native-born Americans, whites, Asians,

02:47:51 --> 02:47:54
Jews, Blacks, for sure.

02:47:53 --> 02:47:55
For sure, for sure.

02:47:54 --> 02:47:56
Those four groups.

02:47:55 --> 02:47:57
- But also- - Yes.

02:47:56 --> 02:47:58
- White people.

02:47:57 --> 02:48:00
- Yeah, and also white people.

02:47:59 --> 02:48:04
- People that are making the case for American-born hires

02:48:04 --> 02:48:08
are usually not also supporting H-1B.

02:48:07 --> 02:48:10
It's an extreme divide,

02:48:09 --> 02:48:12
and those people, they're making that case are often

02:48:11 --> 02:48:16
ot making it in a way that's like,

02:48:16 --> 02:48:20
making it in quite a radical way.

02:48:19 --> 02:48:21
- Yeah. - Let's put it this way.

02:48:20 --> 02:48:22
- Yeah, but you have this interesting thing,

02:48:21 --> 02:48:23
you have a split between the sides that I've noticed,

02:48:22 --> 02:48:25
which is one side has all of the experts.

02:48:24 --> 02:48:26
- Right.

02:48:25 --> 02:48:29
- Right, and I'm using air quote for people listening

02:48:28 --> 02:48:31
to audio. I'm making quotes in the air with my fingers

02:48:30 --> 02:48:32
as vigorously as I can. - Yep.

02:48:31 --> 02:48:34
- One side has all the certified experts.

02:48:33 --> 02:48:36
The other side just has a bunch of people who are like,

02:48:35 --> 02:48:37
they know that something is wrong

02:48:36 --> 02:48:40
and they don't quite know how to explain it.

02:48:39 --> 02:48:42
And what was so unusual about the Harvard UNC cases,

02:48:41 --> 02:48:43
by the way, in front of Supreme Court,

02:48:42 --> 02:48:45
is they actually had sophisticated lawyers

02:48:44 --> 02:48:46
for the first time in a long time,

02:48:45 --> 02:48:47
actually put all this evidence together

02:48:46 --> 02:48:49
and actually put it in the public record,

02:48:48 --> 02:48:51
they actually had experts,

02:48:50 --> 02:48:52
which is just really rare.

02:48:51 --> 02:48:54
Generally what you get is you get,

02:48:53 --> 02:48:55
'cause if you don't have experts, what do you have?

02:48:54 --> 02:48:57
You know, something is wrong,

02:48:56 --> 02:48:59
but you have primarily an emotional response.

02:48:58 --> 02:49:01
You feel it, but can you put it, you know,

02:49:00 --> 02:49:04
can you put it in the words and tables

02:49:03 --> 02:49:06
and charts, you know, that a certified expert can?

02:49:05 --> 02:49:09
And no, you can't, like, you know, that's not who you are.

02:49:08 --> 02:49:10
That doesn't mean that you're wrong,

02:49:09 --> 02:49:11
and it also doesn't mean

02:49:10 --> 02:49:13
that you have less of a moral stance.

02:49:12 --> 02:49:15
Yeah, and so it's just like, all right, now,

02:49:14 --> 02:49:16
by the way, look,

02:49:15 --> 02:49:18
I think there are ways to square the circle,

02:49:17 --> 02:49:20
I think there's a way to have our cake and eat it too,

02:49:19 --> 02:49:22
like I think there'd be many ways to resolve this.

02:49:21 --> 02:49:24
I think, again, I think the way to do it

02:49:23 --> 02:49:26
is to look at these issues combined,

02:49:25 --> 02:49:29
look at DEI combined with high-skilled immigration.

02:49:28 --> 02:49:33
It so happens that DEI is under much more scrutiny today

02:49:32 --> 02:49:35
than it has been for probably 20 years,

02:49:34 --> 02:49:37
affirmative action is.

02:49:36 --> 02:49:39
The Supreme Court did just rule

02:49:38 --> 02:49:42
that it is not legal for universities to do that,

02:49:41 --> 02:49:45
they are still doing it, but they should stop.

02:49:46 --> 02:49:49
And then, there are more and more,

02:49:48 --> 02:49:50
you've seen more companies

02:49:49 --> 02:49:52
now also ditching their DEI programs,

02:49:52 --> 02:49:55
in part, that's happening for a bunch of reasons,

02:49:54 --> 02:49:56
but it's happening in part

02:49:55 --> 02:49:58
because a lot of corporate lawyers will tell you

02:49:57 --> 02:50:01
that the Supreme Court rulings in education

02:50:00 --> 02:50:03
either already apply to businesses,

02:50:02 --> 02:50:05
or it just as a clear foreshadowing

02:50:04 --> 02:50:07
the Supreme Court will rule on new cases

02:50:06 --> 02:50:08
that will ban in businesses.

02:50:07 --> 02:50:11
And so there is a moment here to be able

02:50:10 --> 02:50:14
to look at this on both sides.

02:50:14 --> 02:50:16
Let add one more nuance to it

02:50:15 --> 02:50:18
hat makes it even more complicated.

02:50:17 --> 02:50:19
- Yeah.

02:50:18 --> 02:50:21
- So the cliche is we're gonna brain drain the world, right?

02:50:20 --> 02:50:21
You've heard that?

02:50:20 --> 02:50:23
We're gonna take all the smart people

02:50:22 --> 02:50:24
from all over the world, we're gonna bring them here,

02:50:23 --> 02:50:25
we're gonna educate them, and then we're gonna keep them,

02:50:24 --> 02:50:27
and then they're gonna raise their families here,

02:50:26 --> 02:50:29
create businesses here, create jobs here, right?

02:50:28 --> 02:50:31
- In the cliche, that's a super positive thing.

02:50:30 --> 02:50:32
- Yeah.

02:50:31 --> 02:50:35
Okay, so what happens to the rest of the world?

02:50:35 --> 02:50:37
- They lose?

02:50:36 --> 02:50:40
- Well, how fungible are people?

02:50:41 --> 02:50:46
How many highly ambitious, highly conscientious,

02:50:45 --> 02:50:49
highly energetic, high-achieving, high-IQ,

02:50:48 --> 02:50:51
super geniuses are there in the world?

02:50:50 --> 02:50:54
And if there's a lot, that's great,

02:50:53 --> 02:50:57
but if there just aren't that many and they all come here

02:50:56 --> 02:51:00
and they all aren't where they would be otherwise,

02:50:59 --> 02:51:02
what happens to all those other places?

02:51:02 --> 02:51:05
So it's almost impossible for us here to have

02:51:04 --> 02:51:06
that conversation,

02:51:05 --> 02:51:08
in part because we become incredibly uncomfortable

02:51:07 --> 02:51:09
as a society talking about the fact

02:51:08 --> 02:51:12
hat people aren't just simply all the same,

02:51:11 --> 02:51:14
which is a whole thing we could talk about.

02:51:13 --> 02:51:17
But it also, we are purely the beneficiary

02:51:16 --> 02:51:18
of this effect, right?

02:51:17 --> 02:51:21
We are brain draining the world, not the other way around.

02:51:20 --> 02:51:22
There's only four.

02:51:21 --> 02:51:23
So if you look at the flow of high-skilled immigration

02:51:22 --> 02:51:26
over time, there's only four permanent sinks

02:51:25 --> 02:51:29
of high-skilled immigration places people go.

02:51:28 --> 02:51:31
It's the US, Canada, the UK and Australia.

02:51:31 --> 02:51:33
- Oh, Australia.

02:51:32 --> 02:51:34
- It's four of the five, five eyes.

02:51:33 --> 02:51:36
It's the major Anglosphere countries.

02:51:35 --> 02:51:38
And so for those countries,

02:51:37 --> 02:51:39
this seems like a no-lose proposition,

02:51:38 --> 02:51:42
it's all the other countries that,

02:51:41 --> 02:51:44
basically, what we four countries have been doing

02:51:43 --> 02:51:45
is draining all the smart people out.

02:51:44 --> 02:51:46
It's actually much easier

02:51:45 --> 02:51:48
for people in Europe to talk about this I've discovered

02:51:47 --> 02:51:50
because the Eurozone is, whatever, you know,

02:51:49 --> 02:51:52
28 countries, and within the Eurozone,

02:51:51 --> 02:51:54
the high-skilled people over time have been migrating

02:51:53 --> 02:51:56
to originally the UK,

02:51:55 --> 02:51:59
but also specifically I think it's the Netherlands,

02:51:58 --> 02:52:00
Germany, and France.

02:51:59 --> 02:52:02
But specifically, they've been migrating out

02:52:01 --> 02:52:04
of the peripheral Eurozone countries.

02:52:03 --> 02:52:06
And the one where this really hit the fan was

02:52:05 --> 02:52:07
in Greece, right?

02:52:06 --> 02:52:10
So, you know, Greece falls into chaos, disaster,

02:52:09 --> 02:52:11
and then, you know, you're running the government in Greece

02:52:10 --> 02:52:12
and you're trying to figure out

02:52:11 --> 02:52:14
how to put an economic development plan together.

02:52:14 --> 02:52:17
All of your smart young kids have left.

02:52:16 --> 02:52:20
Like, what are you gonna do, right?

02:52:19 --> 02:52:22
By the way, this is a potential...

02:52:21 --> 02:52:24
I know you care a lot about Ukraine,

02:52:23 --> 02:52:26
this is a potential crisis for Ukraine.

02:52:25 --> 02:52:27
Not because, in part, because of this,

02:52:26 --> 02:52:30
'cause we enthusiastically recruit Ukrainians, of course,

02:52:29 --> 02:52:33
and so we've been brain draining Ukraine for a long time.

02:52:32 --> 02:52:35
But also, of course, you know, war does tend

02:52:34 --> 02:52:36
to cause people to migrate out.

02:52:35 --> 02:52:39
And so, you know, when it comes time for Ukraine to rebuild

02:52:38 --> 02:52:42
as a peaceful country, is it gonna have the talent base

02:52:41 --> 02:52:44
ven that it had five years ago

02:52:43 --> 02:52:45
is like a very big and important question.

02:52:45 --> 02:52:48
By the way, Russia, like we have brain drained a lot

02:52:47 --> 02:52:49
of really smart people outta Russia,

02:52:48 --> 02:52:50
a lot of them are here, right?

02:52:49 --> 02:52:53
Over the last, you know, 30 years.

02:52:52 --> 02:52:55
And so there's this thing,

02:52:54 --> 02:52:56
it's actually really funny if you think about it.

02:52:55 --> 02:52:59
Like, the one thing that we know to be the height

02:52:58 --> 02:53:02
of absolute evil that the West ever did was colonization

02:53:01 --> 02:53:04
and resource extraction, right?

02:53:03 --> 02:53:06
So we know the height of absolute evil

02:53:05 --> 02:53:07
was when the Portuguese and the English and you know,

02:53:06 --> 02:53:10
everybody else went and had these colonies and then went in

02:53:09 --> 02:53:11
and we, you know, took all the oil

02:53:10 --> 02:53:13
and we took all the diamonds, or we took all the whatever,

02:53:12 --> 02:53:15
lithium or whatever it is, right?

02:53:14 --> 02:53:18
Well, for some reason we realized that's a deeply evil thing

02:53:17 --> 02:53:19
to do when it's a physical resource,

02:53:18 --> 02:53:22
when it's a non-conscious physical matter.

02:53:21 --> 02:53:24
For some reason, we think it's completely morally acceptable

02:53:23 --> 02:53:26
to do it with human capital.

02:53:25 --> 02:53:29
In fact, we think it's glorious and beautiful and wonderful

02:53:28 --> 02:53:32
and you know, the great flowering of peace

02:53:31 --> 02:53:34
and harmony and moral justice of our time to do it.

02:53:33 --> 02:53:36
And we don't think for one second what we're doing

02:53:35 --> 02:53:39
to the countries that we're pulling all these people out of.

02:53:38 --> 02:53:41
And this is one of these things like, I don't know,

02:53:40 --> 02:53:43
like maybe we're just gonna live

02:53:42 --> 02:53:44
in this delusional state forever

02:53:43 --> 02:53:45
and we'll just keep doing it

02:53:44 --> 02:53:46
and it'll keep benefiting us

02:53:45 --> 02:53:47
and we just won't care what happens,

02:53:46 --> 02:53:50
but like, I think there may come,

02:53:49 --> 02:53:51
this is like one of these submarines

02:53:50 --> 02:53:52
10 feet under the waterline.

02:53:51 --> 02:53:54
Like, I think it's just a matter of time

02:53:53 --> 02:53:56
until people suddenly realize, "Oh, my God,

02:53:55 --> 02:53:57
what are we doing?"

02:53:56 --> 02:54:00
'Cause like, we need the rest of the world

02:53:59 --> 02:54:01
to succeed too, right?

02:54:00 --> 02:54:03
Like we need these other countries to like flourish.

02:54:02 --> 02:54:05
Like we don't wanna be the only successful country

02:54:04 --> 02:54:08
in the middle of just like complete chaos and disaster,

02:54:07 --> 02:54:10
and we just extract and we extract and we extract,

02:54:09 --> 02:54:12
and we don't think twice about it.

02:54:11 --> 02:54:15
- Well, this is so deeply profound, actually.

02:54:14 --> 02:54:19
So what is the cost "winning" quote, unquote,

02:54:18 --> 02:54:22
if these countries are drained in terms of human capital,

02:54:23 --> 02:54:26
on the level of geopolitics, what does that lead to?

02:54:25 --> 02:54:28
Even if we talk about wars and conflict

02:54:27 --> 02:54:32
and all of this, we actually want them to be strong

02:54:31 --> 02:54:37
in the way we understand strong, not just in every way.

02:54:36 --> 02:54:39
So that cooperation

02:54:38 --> 02:54:41
and competition can build a better world

02:54:40 --> 02:54:42
for all of humanity.

02:54:41 --> 02:54:43
- Yep.

02:54:42 --> 02:54:44
- It's interesting,

02:54:45 --> 02:54:49
this is one of those truths where you just speak

02:54:48 --> 02:54:52
and it resonates, and I didn't even think about it.

02:54:51 --> 02:54:53
- [Marc] Yeah, exactly.

02:54:52 --> 02:54:57
- So you were sitting during the holiday season,

02:54:56 --> 02:54:58
just boiling over.

02:54:57 --> 02:55:00
So all that said- - Yeah.

02:54:59 --> 02:55:04
- There's still to use some good to the H-1B?

02:55:03 --> 02:55:05
- Okay, so then you get this other...

02:55:04 --> 02:55:06
Okay, so then there's-

02:55:05 --> 02:55:07
Come all the way around. (laughs)

02:55:06 --> 02:55:08
- There's another nuance.

02:55:07 --> 02:55:09
So there's another nuance,

02:55:08 --> 02:55:10
which is mostly the valley,

02:55:09 --> 02:55:13
we don't use H-1Bs anymore, mostly we use O-1s.

02:55:14 --> 02:55:17
So there's a separate class of these,

02:55:16 --> 02:55:19
and the O-1 is like this,

02:55:18 --> 02:55:21
it turns out the O-1 is the super genius visa.

02:55:20 --> 02:55:23
So the O-1 is the basically our founder,

02:55:22 --> 02:55:26
like when we have somebody from anywhere in the world

02:55:25 --> 02:55:28
and they've like invented a breakthrough new technology,

02:55:27 --> 02:55:30
and they wanna come to the US to start a company,

02:55:29 --> 02:55:32
they come in through an O-1 visa.

02:55:31 --> 02:55:35
And that actually is like a, it's a fairly high bar.

02:55:34 --> 02:55:36
It's a high acceptance rate,

02:55:35 --> 02:55:38
but it's like a pretty high bar. And they do a lot of work

02:55:37 --> 02:55:40
and there's like a, you have to put real work into it

02:55:39 --> 02:55:41
and really prove your case.

02:55:41 --> 02:55:45
Mostly what's happened with the H-1B visa program

02:55:44 --> 02:55:48
is that it has gone to basically two categories

02:55:47 --> 02:55:49
of employers.

02:55:48 --> 02:55:51
One is the basically a small set of big tech companies

02:55:50 --> 02:55:52
that hire in volume,

02:55:51 --> 02:55:55
which is exactly the companies that you would think.

02:55:54 --> 02:55:56
And then the other is, it goes to these,

02:55:55 --> 02:55:58
they call kind of the mills, the consulting mills, right?

02:55:57 --> 02:56:01
And so there's these set of companies with names,

02:56:00 --> 02:56:02
I don't wanna pick on companies, but you know,

02:56:01 --> 02:56:04
names like Cognizant that, you know,

02:56:03 --> 02:56:07
