hire, basically have their business model

02:56:06 --> 02:56:11
is bring in primarily Indians in large numbers,

02:56:10 --> 02:56:12
and you know, they often have, you know,

02:56:11 --> 02:56:14
offices next to company-owned housing.

02:56:13 --> 02:56:16
And they'll have, you know, organizations that are,

02:56:15 --> 02:56:17
you know, they'll have, you know, organizations

02:56:16 --> 02:56:19
that are literally thousands of Indians, you know,

02:56:18 --> 02:56:20
living and working in the US

02:56:19 --> 02:56:25
and they do basically call it mid-tier, like IT consulting.

02:56:24 --> 02:56:28
So you know, these folks, they're making good wages,

02:56:27 --> 02:56:29
but they're making 60 or 80 a year

02:56:28 --> 02:56:31
or $100,000 a year, not the, you know,

02:56:30 --> 02:56:33
300,000 that you'd make in the Valley.

02:56:34 --> 02:56:36
And so, like in practice,

02:56:35 --> 02:56:39
the startups, basic like little tech as we call it,

02:56:38 --> 02:56:41
or the startup world, mainly doesn't use H-1Bs

02:56:40 --> 02:56:43
at this point and mainly can't,

02:56:42 --> 02:56:45
'cause the system is kind of rigged

02:56:44 --> 02:56:46
in a way that we really can't.

02:56:46 --> 02:56:48
And then again,

02:56:47 --> 02:56:50
you get to the sort of underlying morality here,

02:56:49 --> 02:56:51
which is, it's like, well, you know,

02:56:50 --> 02:56:54
Amazon, like Amazon's a in like I love Amazon,

02:56:53 --> 02:56:56
but like they're a big powerful company.

02:56:55 --> 02:56:57
You know, they've got, you know, more money than God.

02:56:56 --> 02:56:59
They've got resources,

02:56:58 --> 02:57:01
they've got long-term planning horizon, they do big,

02:57:00 --> 02:57:04
you know, profound things over, you know, decades at a time.

02:57:03 --> 02:57:05
You know, they could, you know,

02:57:04 --> 02:57:06
or any of these other companies

02:57:05 --> 02:57:09
could launch massively effective programs

02:57:08 --> 02:57:10
to go recruit the best and brightest

02:57:09 --> 02:57:12
from all throughout the country.

02:57:11 --> 02:57:14
And you know, you'll notice they don't do that.

02:57:13 --> 02:57:16
You know, they bring in, you know,

02:57:15 --> 02:57:18
10,000, 20,000 H1Bs a year.

02:57:17 --> 02:57:20
And so you've got a question there.

02:57:19 --> 02:57:23
And then these mills, like, there's lots of questions

02:57:22 --> 02:57:24
around them and whether they should, you know,

02:57:23 --> 02:57:26
whether that's even a ethical way to, you know,

02:57:25 --> 02:57:28
I don't wanna say they're unethical, but there's questions

02:57:27 --> 02:57:31
around like exactly what the trade-offs are there.

02:57:32 --> 02:57:35
Yeah, and this is like a Pandora's box

02:57:34 --> 02:57:37
that really, you know, nobody really wanted to be opened,

02:57:36 --> 02:57:40
you know, to play devil's advocate on all this

02:57:39 --> 02:57:42
in terms of like national immigration issues, you know,

02:57:41 --> 02:57:43
none of this is like a top end issue just

02:57:42 --> 02:57:45
'cause the numbers are small, right?

02:57:44 --> 02:57:46
And so, you know, I don't think, you know,

02:57:45 --> 02:57:48
the administration has said like,

02:57:47 --> 02:57:51
this is not like a priority of theirs for right now.

02:57:50 --> 02:57:52
But I guess what I would say is like,

02:57:51 --> 02:57:55
there is actually a lot of complexity and nuance here.

02:57:55 --> 02:57:58
I have a lot of friends, like I said, I have a lot

02:57:57 --> 02:57:59
of friends and colleagues who are, you know,

02:57:58 --> 02:58:02
who came over on H-1Bs, or O-1s, green cards,

02:58:01 --> 02:58:03
many are now citizens

02:58:02 --> 02:58:05
and you know, every single one that one of them

02:58:04 --> 02:58:06
was not every single one,

02:58:05 --> 02:58:07
a lot of them were enthusiastic to,

02:58:06 --> 02:58:08
you know, defend the honor

02:58:07 --> 02:58:10
of immigrants throughout this whole period.

02:58:09 --> 02:58:11
And they said to me, it's like, "Well, Marc,

02:58:10 --> 02:58:13
you know, how can we more clearly express,

02:58:12 --> 02:58:15
you know, the importance of high-skilled immigration

02:58:14 --> 02:58:16
to the US?"

02:58:15 --> 02:58:17
And I was like, I think you can do it by advocating

02:58:16 --> 02:58:21
for also developing our native-born talent

02:58:20 --> 02:58:23
and be like, do you wanna inflame the issue

02:58:22 --> 02:58:25
or do you wanna diffuse the issue, right?

02:58:24 --> 02:58:28
And I think the answer is to diffuse the issue.

02:58:27 --> 02:58:32
Let me give you one more positive scenario,

02:58:31 --> 02:58:34
and then I'll also beat up on the university some more.

02:58:35 --> 02:58:38
Do you know about the National Merit Scholarship System?

02:58:37 --> 02:58:39
Have you heard about this?

02:58:40 --> 02:58:41
- Not really, can you explain?

02:58:40 --> 02:58:44
- So there's a system that was created during the Cold War

02:58:43 --> 02:58:45
called the National Merit Scholars.

02:58:44 --> 02:58:48
And it is a, basically, it was created,

02:58:47 --> 02:58:51
I forget, in the late '50s or '60s when...

02:58:50 --> 02:58:52
It was when people in government actually wanted

02:58:51 --> 02:58:54
to identify the best and the brightest,

02:58:53 --> 02:58:57
as heretical an ideas that sounds today.

02:58:56 --> 02:59:00
And so it's basically a national talent search

02:58:59 --> 02:59:01
for basically, IQ.

02:59:02 --> 02:59:05
Its goal is to identify basically the top 0.5%

02:59:04 --> 02:59:08
of the IQ in the country, by the way,

02:59:07 --> 02:59:10
completely regardless of other characteristics.

02:59:09 --> 02:59:12
So there's no race, gender, or any other aspect to it.

02:59:11 --> 02:59:14
It's just going for straight intelligence.

02:59:13 --> 02:59:16
It uses the first, the PSAT,

02:59:16 --> 02:59:20
which is the preparatory SAT that you take,

02:59:19 --> 02:59:21
and then the SAT.

02:59:20 --> 02:59:24
So it uses those scores, that is the scoring.

02:59:23 --> 02:59:26
It's a straight PSAT/SAT scoring system.

02:59:25 --> 02:59:30
So they use the SAT as a proxy for IQ, which it is.

02:59:31 --> 02:59:33
They run this every year,

02:59:32 --> 02:59:34
they identify,

02:59:35 --> 02:59:38
they get down to like 1% of the population of the kids,

02:59:37 --> 02:59:40
18 year olds any given year who scored highest on the PSAT,

02:59:39 --> 02:59:43
and then they get down to like further qualify down

02:59:42 --> 02:59:45
to the 0.5% that also replicate on the SAT.

02:59:45 --> 02:59:47
And then it's like, the scholarship amount

02:59:46 --> 02:59:49
is like $2,500, right?

02:59:48 --> 02:59:51
So it was a lot of money 50 years ago,

02:59:50 --> 02:59:52
not as much today.

02:59:51 --> 02:59:54
But it's a national system being run,

02:59:54 --> 02:59:57
literally, to find the best and the brightest.

02:59:56 --> 03:00:00
How many of our great and powerful universities use this

02:59:59 --> 03:00:01
as a scouting system?

03:00:00 --> 03:00:03
Like our universities all have sports teams,

03:00:02 --> 03:00:07
they all have national scouting, they have full-time scouts

03:00:06 --> 03:00:09
who go out and they go to every high school

03:00:08 --> 03:00:10
and they try to find all the great basketball players

03:00:09 --> 03:00:14
and bring them into the NCAA, into all these leagues.

03:00:13 --> 03:00:15
How many of our great and powerful

03:00:14 --> 03:00:17
and enlightened universities use the National Merit system

03:00:16 --> 03:00:19
to go do a talent search

03:00:18 --> 03:00:22
for the smartest kids and just bring them in?

03:00:21 --> 03:00:23
- Let me guess, very few.

03:00:22 --> 03:00:25
Zero. - Zero. (laughs)

03:00:24 --> 03:00:27
- As you say it, that's brilliant,

03:00:26 --> 03:00:29
there should be that same level of scouting

03:00:28 --> 03:00:31
for talent internally.

03:00:30 --> 03:00:33
- Go get the smartest ones.

03:00:32 --> 03:00:35
I'll give you one more kicker on this topic

03:00:34 --> 03:00:36
if I haven't beaten it to death.

03:00:35 --> 03:00:38
You know, the SAT has changed.

03:00:38 --> 03:00:42
So the SAT used to be a highly accurate proxy for IQ

03:00:43 --> 03:00:46
that caused a bunch of problems.

03:00:45 --> 03:00:49
People really don't like the whole idea of IQ.

03:00:48 --> 03:00:51
And so the SAT has been actively managed

03:00:50 --> 03:00:54
over the last 50 years by the college board that runs it.

03:00:53 --> 03:00:56
And it has been essentially like everything else,

03:00:55 --> 03:00:57
it's been dumbed down.

03:00:57 --> 03:01:01
And so the in two ways, number one,

03:01:00 --> 03:01:05
it's been dumbed down where an 800 from 40 years ago

03:01:04 --> 03:01:06
does not mean what an 800 means today.

03:01:05 --> 03:01:10
And 40 years ago, it was almost impossible to get an 800.

03:01:11 --> 03:01:13
Today, there's so many 800s

03:01:12 --> 03:01:14
that you could stock the entire Ivy League

03:01:13 --> 03:01:15
with 800s, right?

03:01:14 --> 03:01:19
And so, it's been deliberately dumbed down.

03:01:18 --> 03:01:20
And then two is they have tried

03:01:19 --> 03:01:22
to pull out a lot of what's called the g-loading.

03:01:21 --> 03:01:26
And so they've tried to detach it from being an IQ proxy

03:01:25 --> 03:01:27
'cause IQ is such an inflammatory concept.

03:01:26 --> 03:01:29
And the consequence of that is,

03:01:28 --> 03:01:30
and this is sort of perverse,

03:01:29 --> 03:01:33
they've made it more coachable, right?

03:01:32 --> 03:01:35
So the SAT 40 years ago,

03:01:34 --> 03:01:36
coaching didn't really work.

03:01:35 --> 03:01:38
And more recently, it has really started to work.

03:01:37 --> 03:01:40
And one of the things you see is the Asian spike,

03:01:39 --> 03:01:42
you see this like giant leap upward

03:01:41 --> 03:01:43
in Asian performance over the last decade.

03:01:42 --> 03:01:44
And I think looking at the data,

03:01:43 --> 03:01:47
I think a lot of that is because it's more coachable now.

03:01:46 --> 03:01:48
And the Asians do the most coaching.

03:01:49 --> 03:01:51
So there's a bunch of issues with this.

03:01:50 --> 03:01:53
And so the coaching thing is really difficult

03:01:52 --> 03:01:55
'cause the coaching thing is a subsidy

03:01:54 --> 03:01:58
then to the kids whose parents can afford coaching, right?

03:01:57 --> 03:01:59
And I don't know about you, but where I grew up,

03:01:58 --> 03:02:00
there was no SAT coaching.

03:01:59 --> 03:02:02
So there's like an issue there.

03:02:01 --> 03:02:04
I didn't even know what the SAT was until the day I took it,

03:02:03 --> 03:02:05
much less that there was coaching,

03:02:04 --> 03:02:07
much less that it could work,

03:02:06 --> 03:02:09
so much less we could afford it.

03:02:08 --> 03:02:10
So, number one, there's issues there,

03:02:09 --> 03:02:12
but the other issue there is think about what's happened

03:02:11 --> 03:02:16
by the dumbing down, 800 no longer captures

03:02:15 --> 03:02:19
all the smart, 800 is too crude of a test.

03:02:18 --> 03:02:21
It's like the AI benchmarking problem.

03:02:20 --> 03:02:23
It's the same problem they have AI benchmarking right now.

03:02:22 --> 03:02:25
800 is too low of a threshold.

03:02:25 --> 03:02:28
There are too many kids scoring 800.

03:02:27 --> 03:02:30
'Cause what you want is you want, whatever,

03:02:29 --> 03:02:32
if it's gonna be 100,000 kids, I don't know what it is,

03:02:31 --> 03:02:34
it's gonna be 50,000 kids a year scoring 800.

03:02:33 --> 03:02:36
You also then want kids to be able to score 900

03:02:35 --> 03:02:38
and 1,000, and 1,100, and 1,200,

03:02:37 --> 03:02:40
and you wanna ultimately get to, you know,

03:02:39 --> 03:02:42
you'd like to ultimately identify the top 100 kids

03:02:41 --> 03:02:45
and make sure that you get them in MIT.

03:02:44 --> 03:02:47
And the resolution of the test has been reduced,

03:02:46 --> 03:02:50
so that it actually is not useful for doing that.

03:02:49 --> 03:02:52
And again, I would say this is like part

03:02:51 --> 03:02:53
of the generalized corruption

03:02:52 --> 03:02:55
that's taken place throughout this entire system

03:02:54 --> 03:02:57
where we have been heading in the reverse direction

03:02:56 --> 03:02:59
from wanting to actually go get the best and brightest

03:02:58 --> 03:03:01
and actually put them in the places where they should be.

03:03:00 --> 03:03:03
And then just the final comment would be,

03:03:02 --> 03:03:05
the great thing about standardized testing

03:03:04 --> 03:03:07
and the National Merit System is it's comp, like I said,

03:03:06 --> 03:03:09
it's completely race blind, it's gender blind,

03:03:08 --> 03:03:11
it's blind on every other characteristic.

03:03:10 --> 03:03:13
It's only done on test scores.

03:03:12 --> 03:03:14
You know, and you can make an argument about whether

03:03:13 --> 03:03:17
that's good or bad, but it is, you know, for sure,

03:03:16 --> 03:03:18
you know, it's the closest thing

03:03:18 --> 03:03:20
that we had to get to merit.

03:03:19 --> 03:03:22
It was the thing that they did

03:03:21 --> 03:03:24
when they thought they needed merit to win the Cold War.

03:03:23 --> 03:03:27
And of course, we could choose to do that anytime we want.

03:03:26 --> 03:03:28
And I just say,

03:03:27 --> 03:03:29
I find it like incredibly striking

03:03:28 --> 03:03:32
and an enormous moral indictment of the current system

03:03:31 --> 03:03:34
that there are no universities that do this today.

03:03:33 --> 03:03:36
So back to the immigration thing, just real quick.

03:03:35 --> 03:03:37
It's like, okay,

03:03:36 --> 03:03:38
we aren't even trying to go get the smart kids

03:03:37 --> 03:03:40
out of the center of- - Yeah.

03:03:39 --> 03:03:41
- And even if they think

03:03:40 --> 03:03:43
that they can get into these places, they get turned down.

03:03:42 --> 03:03:44
And the same thing for the smart Asians

03:03:43 --> 03:03:45
and the same thing for the smart Jews,

03:03:44 --> 03:03:47
and the same thing for the smart Black people.

03:03:46 --> 03:03:52
And like, it's just like, I don't know how, like,

03:03:51 --> 03:03:53
I don't know how that's moral.

03:03:52 --> 03:03:55
Like I don't get it at all.

03:03:54 --> 03:03:57
- As you said about the 800,

03:03:56 --> 03:03:59
so I took the SAT and the ACT many times

03:03:58 --> 03:04:02
and I've always gotten perfect on math, 800.

03:04:03 --> 03:04:06
And I'm not that I'm not special like,

03:04:08 --> 03:04:11
it doesn't identify genius.

03:04:10 --> 03:04:14
I think you wanna search for genius

03:04:13 --> 03:04:18
and you wanna create measures that find genius

03:04:17 --> 03:04:21
of all different kinds, speaking of diversity.

03:04:20 --> 03:04:24
And I guess we should reiterate

03:04:23 --> 03:04:29
and say over and over and over, defend immigrants.

03:04:28 --> 03:04:30
Yes.

03:04:29 --> 03:04:33
But say we should hire more and more native-born.

03:04:32 --> 03:04:35
- Well, you asked me in the beginning like

03:04:34 --> 03:04:37
what's the most optimistic forecast, right,

03:04:36 --> 03:04:38
that we could have?

03:04:37 --> 03:04:39
And the most optimistic forecast would be, my God,

03:04:38 --> 03:04:42
what if we did both? (laughs)

03:04:43 --> 03:04:47
- So that's the reasonable, the rational,

03:04:46 --> 03:04:48
the smart thing to say here.

03:04:47 --> 03:04:51
In fact, we don't have to have a war.

03:04:50 --> 03:04:53
- Well, it would diffuse the entire issue.

03:04:52 --> 03:04:54
- [Lex] Yeah.

03:04:53 --> 03:04:55
- If everybody in the center in the South of the country

03:04:54 --> 03:04:57
and every Jewish family, Asian family,

03:04:56 --> 03:04:58
Black family knew they were getting a fair shake,

03:04:57 --> 03:05:00
like it would diffuse the issue.

03:05:00 --> 03:05:02
Like how about diffusing the issue?

03:05:01 --> 03:05:04
Like what a crazy radical...

03:05:03 --> 03:05:05
Sorry, I don't mean to really get out

03:05:04 --> 03:05:06
over my skis here, but-

03:05:06 --> 03:05:11
I think your profile on X states, "It's time to build."

03:05:10 --> 03:05:14
It feels like 2025 is a good year to build.

03:05:14 --> 03:05:19
So I wanted to ask your advice,

03:05:19 --> 03:05:25
and maybe for advice for anybody who's trying to build,

03:05:25 --> 03:05:29
who's trying to build something useful in the world

03:05:29 --> 03:05:31
or maybe launch a startup

03:05:30 --> 03:05:35
or maybe just launch apps, services, whatever,

03:05:34 --> 03:05:38
ship software products.

03:05:37 --> 03:05:41
So maybe, by way of advice,

03:05:41 --> 03:05:45
how do you actually get to shipping?

03:05:44 --> 03:05:46
- So I mean, a big part of the answer I think

03:05:45 --> 03:05:48
is we're in the middle of a legit revolution.

03:05:47 --> 03:05:50
And I know you've been talking about this on your show,

03:05:49 --> 03:05:54
but like AI coding, I mean, this is the biggest earthquake

03:05:53 --> 03:05:56
to hit software in certainly my life,

03:05:56 --> 03:05:59
maybe since the investment of software.

03:05:58 --> 03:06:00
And I'm sure you, and you know, we're involved in various

03:05:59 --> 03:06:03
of these companies, but you know, these tools,

03:06:02 --> 03:06:04
you know, from a variety of companies

03:06:03 --> 03:06:08
are just like absolutely revolutionary

03:06:08 --> 03:06:11
and they're getting better by leaps and bounds every day.

03:06:10 --> 03:06:12
And you know, all this,

03:06:11 --> 03:06:14
but like the thing with coding,

03:06:13 --> 03:06:15
like there's like open questions

03:06:14 --> 03:06:17
of whether AI can get better at like,

03:06:16 --> 03:06:18
I don't know, understanding philosophy

03:06:17 --> 03:06:19
or whatever, creative writing or whatever.

03:06:18 --> 03:06:22
But like for sure, we can make it much better

03:06:21 --> 03:06:23
at coding, right?

03:06:22 --> 03:06:25
Because you can validate the results of coding.

03:06:24 --> 03:06:27
And so, you know, there's all these methods of, you know,

03:06:26 --> 03:06:28
synthetic data and self-training

03:06:27 --> 03:06:30
and reinforcement learning that,

03:06:29 --> 03:06:31
for sure, you can do with coding.

03:06:30 --> 03:06:33
And so everybody I know

03:06:32 --> 03:06:34
ho works in the field says AI coding

03:06:33 --> 03:06:37
is gonna get to be phenomenally good.

03:06:36 --> 03:06:38
And it's already great.

03:06:37 --> 03:06:40
And you can, I mean anybody wants to see this,

03:06:39 --> 03:06:42
just go on YouTube and look at AI coding demos,

03:06:41 --> 03:06:44
you know, little kids making apps in 10 minutes,

03:06:43 --> 03:06:45
working with an AI coding system.

03:06:44 --> 03:06:47
And so I think it's the golden age.

03:06:46 --> 03:06:48
I mean I think this is an area

03:06:47 --> 03:06:49
where it's clearly the golden age,

03:06:48 --> 03:06:51
the tool set is extraordinary.

03:06:50 --> 03:06:53
You know, in a day as a coder, for sure,

03:06:52 --> 03:06:55
in a day you can retrain yourself, you know,

03:06:54 --> 03:06:57
start using these things, get a huge boost in productivity,

03:06:56 --> 03:06:58
as a non-coder,

03:06:57 --> 03:07:01
you can learn much more quickly than you could before.

03:07:00 --> 03:07:03
- That's actually a tricky one in terms of learning

03:07:03 --> 03:07:05
as a non-coder to build stuff.

03:07:04 --> 03:07:07
It's still, I feel like you still need to learn

03:07:06 --> 03:07:11
how to code, it becomes a superpower.

03:07:10 --> 03:07:13
It helps you be much more productive.

03:07:12 --> 03:07:18
Like you could legitimately be a one person company

03:07:17 --> 03:07:20
and get quite far.

03:07:19 --> 03:07:21
- I agree with that, up to a point.

03:07:20 --> 03:07:25
So I think, for sure, for quite a long time,

03:07:24 --> 03:07:26
the people who are good at coding

03:07:25 --> 03:07:29
are gonna be the best at actually having AI's code things,

03:07:28 --> 03:07:31
'cause they're gonna understand what I mean, very basic.

03:07:30 --> 03:07:33
They're gonna understand what's happening, right?

03:07:32 --> 03:07:34
And they're gonna be able be able to evaluate the work

03:07:33 --> 03:07:35
and they're gonna be able to, you know,

03:07:34 --> 03:07:38
literally like manage AIs better.

03:07:37 --> 03:07:39
Like even if they're not literally handwriting the code,

03:07:38 --> 03:07:40
they're just gonna have a much better sense

03:07:39 --> 03:07:41
of what's going on.

03:07:40 --> 03:07:43
So I definitely think like 100% my nine-year-old

03:07:42 --> 03:07:45
is like doing all kinds of coding classes,

03:07:44 --> 03:07:48
and he'll keep doing that for certainly through 18.

03:07:47 --> 03:07:49
We'll see after that.

03:07:49 --> 03:07:53
And so like for sure that's the case.

03:07:52 --> 03:07:54
But look, having said that,

03:07:53 --> 03:07:56
one of the things you can do with an AI is say,

03:07:55 --> 03:07:59
teach me how to code, right?

03:07:59 --> 03:08:02
And you know, there's a whole bunch of,

03:08:01 --> 03:08:04
you know, I'll name names, you know, Khan Academy,

03:08:03 --> 03:08:06
like there's a whole bunch of work

03:08:05 --> 03:08:07
that they're doing at Khan Academy for free.

03:08:06 --> 03:08:09
And then we, you know, we have this company, Replit,

03:08:08 --> 03:08:11
which is was originally specifically built for kids

03:08:10 --> 03:08:14
for coding, that has AI built in,

03:08:13 --> 03:08:16
that's just absolutely extraordinary now.

03:08:15 --> 03:08:17
And then, you know, there's a variety

03:08:16 --> 03:08:19
of other systems like this

03:08:19 --> 03:08:22
and yeah, that, I mean, the AI's gonna be able to teach you

03:08:21 --> 03:08:24
to code, AI, by the way, is as you know,

03:08:23 --> 03:08:28
spectacularly good at explaining code, right?

03:08:27 --> 03:08:31
And so, you know, the tools have these features now

03:08:30 --> 03:08:32
here you can talk to the code base

03:08:31 --> 03:08:33
and so you can like literally

03:08:32 --> 03:08:35
like ask the code base questions about itself.

03:08:35 --> 03:08:38
And you can also just do the simple form,

03:08:37 --> 03:08:40
which is you can copy and paste code into ChatGPT

03:08:39 --> 03:08:42
and just ask it to explain it what's going on, rewrite it,

03:08:41 --> 03:08:44
improve it, make recommendations.

03:08:43 --> 03:08:47
And so yeah, there's dozens of ways to do this.

03:08:46 --> 03:08:48
By the way, you can also,

03:08:47 --> 03:08:50
I mean even more broadly than code.

03:08:49 --> 03:08:51
Like, okay, you wanna make a video game,

03:08:51 --> 03:08:55
okay, now you can do AI, art generation, sound generation,

03:08:54 --> 03:08:57
dialogue generation, voice generation, right?

03:08:56 --> 03:09:00
And so all of a sudden, like, you don't need designers,

03:08:59 --> 03:09:01
you know, you don't need, you know,

03:09:00 --> 03:09:04
voice actors, you know, so yeah.

03:09:03 --> 03:09:05
So there's just like unlimited

03:09:04 --> 03:09:07
and then, you know, a big is, you know,

03:09:06 --> 03:09:08
a big part of coding is so-called glue,

03:09:07 --> 03:09:11
you know, it's interfacing into other systems.

03:09:10 --> 03:09:12
So it's interfacing into, you know,

03:09:11 --> 03:09:14
Stripe to take payments or something like that.

03:09:13 --> 03:09:16
And, you know, AI's fantastic at writing glue code.

03:09:17 --> 03:09:19
So, you know, really, really good at making sure

03:09:18 --> 03:09:21
that you can plug everything together,

03:09:20 --> 03:09:24
really good at helping you figure out how to deploy,

03:09:23 --> 03:09:26
you know, it'll even write a business plan for you.

03:09:26 --> 03:09:29
So it's just this, it's like everything happening

03:09:28 --> 03:09:30
with AI right now, it's just,

03:09:29 --> 03:09:31
it's like this latent superpower

03:09:30 --> 03:09:33
and there's this incredible spectrum of people

03:09:32 --> 03:09:36
who have really figured out massive performance increases,

03:09:35 --> 03:09:38
productivity increases with it already.

03:09:37 --> 03:09:40
There's other people who aren't even aware it's happening.

03:09:39 --> 03:09:44
And there's some gearing to whether you're a coder or not,

03:09:43 --> 03:09:46
but I think there are lots of non-coders

03:09:45 --> 03:09:47
that are off to the races.

03:09:46 --> 03:09:48
And I think there are lots of professional coders

03:09:47 --> 03:09:50
who are still like, eh...

03:09:50 --> 03:09:53
You know, the blacksmiths were not necessarily in favor of,

03:09:52 --> 03:09:56
you know, the car business.

03:09:55 --> 03:09:58
So yeah, there's the old William Gibson quote,

03:09:57 --> 03:10:01
"The future is here, it's just not evenly distributed yet."

03:10:00 --> 03:10:03
And this is maybe the most potent version of that

03:10:02 --> 03:10:04
I've ever seen.

03:10:04 --> 03:10:07
- Yeah, there's, you know, the old meme

03:10:09 --> 03:10:10
with the bell curve,

03:10:09 --> 03:10:13
the people on both extremes say,

03:10:12 --> 03:10:15
"AI coding is the future."

03:10:15 --> 03:10:17
- Right. - It is very common.

03:10:16 --> 03:10:18
The programmers to say, you know,

03:10:17 --> 03:10:19
if you're any good of a programmer,

03:10:18 --> 03:10:22
you're not going to be using it,

03:10:21 --> 03:10:23
that's just not true.

03:10:22 --> 03:10:25
I consider myself reasonably good programmer,

03:10:24 --> 03:10:28
and my productivity has been just skyrocketed,

03:10:27 --> 03:10:32
and the joy of programming skyrocketed,

03:10:31 --> 03:10:35
every aspect of programming is more efficient,

03:10:35 --> 03:10:39
more productive, more fun, all that kind of stuff.

03:10:38 --> 03:10:41
- I would also say code is, you know,

03:10:40 --> 03:10:43
code has of anything in like industrial society,

03:10:42 --> 03:10:46
code has the highest elasticity,

03:10:45 --> 03:10:48
which is to say the easier it is to make it,

03:10:47 --> 03:10:49
the more of it gets made.

03:10:48 --> 03:10:52
Like I think effectively there's unlimited demand for code.

03:10:51 --> 03:10:55
Like, in other words, like there's always some other idea

03:10:54 --> 03:10:57
for a thing that you can do, a feature that you can add

03:10:56 --> 03:10:59
or a thing that you can optimize.

03:11:00 --> 03:11:03
And so, like overwhelmingly, you know,

03:11:02 --> 03:11:04
the amount of code that exists in the world

03:11:03 --> 03:11:06
is a fraction of even the ideas we have today.

03:11:05 --> 03:11:08
And then we come up with new ideas all the time.

03:11:07 --> 03:11:11
And so I think that like, (laughs) you know,

03:11:11 --> 03:11:13
I was in the late '80s, early '90s,

03:11:12 --> 03:11:15
when sort of automated coding systems started

03:11:14 --> 03:11:17
to come out, expert systems, a big deal in those days,

03:11:16 --> 03:11:19
and there was a famous book called

03:11:18 --> 03:11:21
the "Decline and Fall of the American Programmer," you know,

03:11:20 --> 03:11:23
that predicted that these new coding systems

03:11:22 --> 03:11:24
were gonna mean we wouldn't have programmers in the future.

03:11:23 --> 03:11:26
And of course, the number of programming jobs exploded

03:11:25 --> 03:11:27
by like a factor of 100.

03:11:27 --> 03:11:31
Like, my guess is we'll have more coding jobs probably

03:11:30 --> 03:11:33
by like an order magnitude 10 years from now.

03:11:34 --> 03:11:36
That will be different, they'll be different jobs.

03:11:35 --> 03:11:40
They'll involve orchestrating AI, but there will be,

03:11:39 --> 03:11:42
we will be creating so much more software

03:11:41 --> 03:11:44
that the whole industry will just explode in size.

03:11:44 --> 03:11:48
- Are you seeing the size of companies decrease

03:11:47 --> 03:11:50
in terms of startups?

03:11:49 --> 03:11:52
What's the landscapes of little tech?

03:11:51 --> 03:11:53
- All we're seeing right now

03:11:52 --> 03:11:55
is the AI hiring boom of all time.

03:11:55 --> 03:11:58
- [Lex] Oh, for the big tech?

03:11:57 --> 03:11:59
- And little tech. - And little tech.

03:11:58 --> 03:12:01
- Everybody's trying to hire as many engineers

03:12:00 --> 03:12:03
as they can to build AI systems,

03:12:02 --> 03:12:04
it's 100%.

03:12:03 --> 03:12:06
I mean, there's a handful of company, you know,

03:12:06 --> 03:12:09
there's a little bit, there's customer service,

03:12:08 --> 03:12:11
you know, we have some companies and others

03:12:10 --> 03:12:12
'cause I think it's Klarna that's publicizing

03:12:11 --> 03:12:17
a lot of this in Europe where, you know,

03:12:16 --> 03:12:18
there are jobs that can be optimized

03:12:17 --> 03:12:20
and jobs that can be automated.

03:12:19 --> 03:12:22
But like for engineering jobs, like,

03:12:21 --> 03:12:24
it's just an explosion of hiring

03:12:25 --> 03:12:27
that at least, so far, there's no trace

03:12:26 --> 03:12:29
of any sort of diminishing effect.

03:12:28 --> 03:12:31
Now, having said that, I am looking forward to the day,

03:12:30 --> 03:12:34
I am waiting for the first company to walk in saying, yes,

03:12:34 --> 03:12:37
like the more radical form of it.

03:12:36 --> 03:12:38
So basically,

03:12:37 --> 03:12:39
the companies that we see are basically one of two kinds.

03:12:38 --> 03:12:40
We see the companies

03:12:39 --> 03:12:44
that are basically sometimes use weak form, strong form.

03:12:43 --> 03:12:47
So the weak form companies, I sometimes use the term,

03:12:46 --> 03:12:49
it's call it the sixth bullet point.

03:12:48 --> 03:12:52
AI is the sixth bullet point on whatever they're doing.

03:12:51 --> 03:12:53
- (laughs) Sure.

03:12:52 --> 03:12:54
- Right? And it's on the slide,

03:12:53 --> 03:12:55
right? - Yeah.

03:12:54 --> 03:12:56
- So they've got the, you know, whatever,

03:12:55 --> 03:12:57
da, da, da, da, da,

03:12:56 --> 03:12:57
and then AI is the sixth thing.

03:12:56 --> 03:12:58
And the reason AI is the sixth thing is,

03:12:57 --> 03:13:00
'cause they had already previously written the slide

03:12:59 --> 03:13:01
before the AI revolution started,

03:13:00 --> 03:13:03
and so they just added the six bullet point in the slide,

03:13:02 --> 03:13:05
which is how you're getting all these products

03:13:04 --> 03:13:07
that have like the AI button up in the corner, right?

03:13:06 --> 03:13:08
The little sparkly button.

03:13:07 --> 03:13:09
- Yep. - Right?

03:13:08 --> 03:13:10
And all of a sudden Gmail is offering

03:13:09 --> 03:13:13
to summarize your email, which I'm like, I don't need that.

03:13:12 --> 03:13:16
Like, I need you to answer my email, not summarize it.

03:13:15 --> 03:13:17
Like what the hell?

03:13:16 --> 03:13:19
Okay, so we see those and that's fine.

03:13:18 --> 03:13:20
That's like, I don't know,

03:13:19 --> 03:13:23
putting sugar on the cake or something.

03:13:22 --> 03:13:25
But then we see the strong form, which is the companies

03:13:24 --> 03:13:27
that are building from scratch for AI, right?

03:13:26 --> 03:13:28
And they're building it.

03:13:27 --> 03:13:29
I actually just met with a company

03:13:28 --> 03:13:31
that is building literally an AI email system,

03:13:30 --> 03:13:32
as an example, so just-

03:13:31 --> 03:13:34
Oh, nice.

03:13:33 --> 03:13:35
I can't wait.

03:13:34 --> 03:13:36
- Yeah, they're gonna completely.

03:13:35 --> 03:13:39
So the very obvious idea, very smart team, you know,

03:13:38 --> 03:13:40
it's gonna be great.

03:13:40 --> 03:13:43
And then, you know, Notion, just, you know,

03:13:42 --> 03:13:44
another, not one of our companies,

03:13:43 --> 03:13:46
but just came out with a product. And so now companies

03:13:45 --> 03:13:47
are gonna basically come through,

03:13:46 --> 03:13:48
sweep through,

03:13:47 --> 03:13:49
and they're gonna do basically AI-first versions

03:13:48 --> 03:13:51
of basically everything.

03:13:50 --> 03:13:52
And those are like companies built, you know,

03:13:52 --> 03:13:54
AI is the first bullet point.

03:13:53 --> 03:13:56
It's the strong form of the argument.

03:13:55 --> 03:13:57
- Yeah, Cursor is an example of that.

03:13:56 --> 03:14:01
They basically said, okay, we're gonna rebuild the thing

03:14:00 --> 03:14:02
with AI as the first citizen.

03:14:01 --> 03:14:05
- What if we knew from scratch that we could build on this?

03:14:04 --> 03:14:06
And again, this is like, this is part

03:14:05 --> 03:14:09
of the Full Employment Act for startups and VCs is,

03:14:08 --> 03:14:12
it just like if a technology transformation

03:14:11 --> 03:14:13
is sufficiently powerful,

03:14:12 --> 03:14:14
then you actually need

03:14:13 --> 03:14:17
to start the product development process over from scratch,

03:14:16 --> 03:14:18
'cause you need to reconceptualize the product,

03:14:17 --> 03:14:21
and then usually what that means is you need a new company

03:14:20 --> 03:14:23
'cause most incumbents just won't do that.

03:14:23 --> 03:14:25
And so, yeah.

03:14:24 --> 03:14:26
So that's underway across many categories.

03:14:25 --> 03:14:29
What I'm waiting for is the company where it's like,

03:14:28 --> 03:14:32
no, our org chart is redesigned as a result of AI, right?

03:14:31 --> 03:14:34
So I'm looking at, I'm waiting for the company

03:14:33 --> 03:14:37
where it's like, no, we're gonna have, like, you know,

03:14:36 --> 03:14:39
and the cliche, here's a thought experiment, right?

03:14:38 --> 03:14:40
The cliche would be we're gonna have

03:14:39 --> 03:14:42
like the human executive team,

03:14:41 --> 03:14:43
and then we're gonna have the theis be the workers, right?

03:14:42 --> 03:14:46
So we'll have a VP of engineering supervising 100 instances

03:14:47 --> 03:14:49
of coding agents, right?

03:14:48 --> 03:14:51
Okay, maybe, right by the way,

03:14:50 --> 03:14:54
or maybe the VP of engineering should be the AI,

03:14:54 --> 03:14:57
maybe supervising human coders

03:14:56 --> 03:14:58
who are supervising AIs, right?

03:14:57 --> 03:15:01
'Cause one of the things that AI should be pretty good at

03:15:00 --> 03:15:03
is managing 'cause it's like not, you know,

03:15:02 --> 03:15:05
it's like a process-driven, it's the kind of thing

03:15:04 --> 03:15:07
that AI's actually pretty good at, right?

03:15:06 --> 03:15:09
Performance evaluation, coaching.

03:15:09 --> 03:15:12
And so should it be an AI executive team?

03:15:12 --> 03:15:14
You know, and then, of course, the ultimate question,

03:15:13 --> 03:15:17
which is AI CEO, right?

03:15:18 --> 03:15:21
You know, and then maybe the most futuristic version

03:15:20 --> 03:15:23
of it would be an actual AI agent

03:15:22 --> 03:15:24
hat actually goes fully autonomous.

03:15:23 --> 03:15:26
Yeah, what if you really set one of these things loose

03:15:25 --> 03:15:28
and let it basically build itself a business?

03:15:28 --> 03:15:32
And so I will say like we're not yet seeing those,

03:15:31 --> 03:15:34
and I think there's a little bit of,

03:15:33 --> 03:15:36
the systems aren't quite ready for that yet.

03:15:35 --> 03:15:38
And then I think it's a little bit of,

03:15:37 --> 03:15:40
you really do need, at that point,

03:15:39 --> 03:15:41
like a founder who's really willing

03:15:40 --> 03:15:45
to break all the rules and really willing to take the swing.

03:15:44 --> 03:15:47
And those people exist, and so I'm sure we'll see that.

03:15:46 --> 03:15:50
- And some of it is, as you know, with all the startups,

03:15:49 --> 03:15:51
this is the execution.

03:15:50 --> 03:15:55
The idea that you have a AI-first email client,

03:15:54 --> 03:15:56
seems like an obvious idea,

03:15:55 --> 03:15:59
but actually creating one, executing it,

03:15:58 --> 03:16:02
and then taking on Gmail is really difficult.

03:16:01 --> 03:16:03
I mean, Gmail,

03:16:02 --> 03:16:08
it's fascinating to see Google can't do it, because why?

03:16:08 --> 03:16:10
Because of momentum,

03:16:09 --> 03:16:13
because it's hard to re-engineer the entirety of the system.

03:16:12 --> 03:16:16
'Cause feels like Google's perfectly positioned to do it.

03:16:15 --> 03:16:20
Same with like, you have Perplexity, which I love,

03:16:19 --> 03:16:23
like Google could technically take on Perplexity

03:16:22 --> 03:16:26
and do it much better, but they haven't, not yet.

03:16:25 --> 03:16:30
So it's fascinating why that is for large companies.

03:16:29 --> 03:16:32
I mean that is an advantage for little tech,

03:16:31 --> 03:16:34
they could be agile.

03:16:33 --> 03:16:35
- Yeah, that's right. - They can move fast.

03:16:34 --> 03:16:36
- Yeah.

03:16:35 --> 03:16:37
Little companies can break glass

03:16:36 --> 03:16:39
in a way big companies can't- - Right.

03:16:38 --> 03:16:40
- This is sort of the big breakthrough

03:16:39 --> 03:16:42
that Clay Christensen had in "The Innovator's Dilemma,"

03:16:41 --> 03:16:44
which is sometimes when big companies don't do things,

03:16:43 --> 03:16:45
it's because they're screwing up.

03:16:44 --> 03:16:47
And that certainly happens.

03:16:46 --> 03:16:48
But a lot of times they don't do things

03:16:47 --> 03:16:50
because it would break too much glass.

03:16:49 --> 03:16:51
It would specifically,

03:16:51 --> 03:16:53
it would interfere with their existing customers

03:16:52 --> 03:16:55
and their existing businesses.

03:16:54 --> 03:16:56
And they just simply won't do that.

03:16:55 --> 03:17:00
And by the way, responsibly, they shouldn't do that, right?

03:16:59 --> 03:17:02
And so they just get,

03:17:01 --> 03:17:03
this is Clay Christensen's big thing

03:17:02 --> 03:17:05
is they often don't adapt

03:17:04 --> 03:17:08
because they're well-run, not because they're poorly run.

03:17:07 --> 03:17:10
But they're optimizing machines.

03:17:09 --> 03:17:13
They're optimizing against the existing business.

03:17:12 --> 03:17:14
And as you kind of just said,

03:17:13 --> 03:17:16
this is like a permanent state of affairs

03:17:15 --> 03:17:17
for large organizations.

03:17:16 --> 03:17:18
Like every once in a while,

03:17:18 --> 03:17:20
one breaks the pattern and actually does it.

03:17:19 --> 03:17:21
But for the most part,

03:17:20 --> 03:17:23
like this is a very predictable form of human behavior,

03:17:22 --> 03:17:25
and this fundamentally is why startups exist.

03:17:26 --> 03:17:29
- It feels like 2025 is when the race for dominance

03:17:28 --> 03:17:32
in AI will see some winners.

03:17:31 --> 03:17:33
Like, it's a big year.

03:17:32 --> 03:17:36
So who do you think wins the race?

03:17:35 --> 03:17:38
OpenAI, Meta, Google, xAI.

03:17:37 --> 03:17:40
Who do you think wins the AI race?

03:17:39 --> 03:17:41
- I would say, I'm not gonna predict,

03:17:40 --> 03:17:44
I'm gonna say there's questions all over the place.

03:17:43 --> 03:17:45
And then we have this category question we call

03:17:44 --> 03:17:47
the trillion-dollar question, which is like,

03:17:46 --> 03:17:49
literally depending on how it's answered,

03:17:48 --> 03:17:50
people make or lose a trillion dollars,

03:17:49 --> 03:17:52
and I think there's like, I don't know,

03:17:51 --> 03:17:54
5 or $6 trillion questions right now

03:17:53 --> 03:17:55
that are hanging out there,

03:17:54 --> 03:17:57
which is an unusually large number.

03:17:56 --> 03:17:58
- [Lex] Yeah.

03:17:57 --> 03:17:58
- And I'll just hit a few of them

03:17:57 --> 03:17:59
and we can talk about them.

03:17:58 --> 03:18:02
So one is big models versus small models.

03:18:01 --> 03:18:04
Another is open models versus closed models.

03:18:03 --> 03:18:07
Another is whether you can use synthetic data or not.

03:18:06 --> 03:18:09
Another is chain of thought.

03:18:08 --> 03:18:10
How far can you push that?

03:18:09 --> 03:18:12
And reinforcement learning.

03:18:11 --> 03:18:15
And then another one is political trillion-dollar questions.

03:18:14 --> 03:18:17
You know, policy questions, which, you know,

03:18:16 --> 03:18:20
the US and the EU have both been flunking dramatically

03:18:19 --> 03:18:23
and the US hopefully is about to really succeed at.

03:18:23 --> 03:18:25
Yeah, and then there's probably another, you know,

03:18:24 --> 03:18:27
half dozen big important questions after that.

03:18:26 --> 03:18:31
And so these are all just like, say, this is an industry

03:18:30 --> 03:18:34
that's in flux in a way that I even more dramatic,

03:18:33 --> 03:18:36
I think, than the ones I've seen before.

03:18:35 --> 03:18:38
And look, the most obvious example of the flux

03:18:37 --> 03:18:42
is you know, sitting here less than three years ago,

03:18:41 --> 03:18:44
sitting here in December of '22,

03:18:43 --> 03:18:44
we would've said that OpenAI

03:18:44 --> 03:18:47
is just running away with everything.

03:18:46 --> 03:18:49
And sitting here today, it's like, you know,

03:18:48 --> 03:18:50
there's at least six, you know,

03:18:49 --> 03:18:52
world-class God model companies

03:18:51 --> 03:18:54
and teams that are, by the way,

03:18:54 --> 03:18:57
generating remarkably similar results.

03:18:56 --> 03:18:58
That's actually been one of the most shocking things to me,

03:18:57 --> 03:19:01
is like, it turns out that once you know that it's possible

03:19:00 --> 03:19:04
to build one incredibly smart Turning-test-passing

03:19:03 --> 03:19:06
large language model, which was a complete shock

03:19:05 --> 03:19:09
and surprise to the world, it turns out within, you know,

03:19:08 --> 03:19:11
a year, you can have five more.

03:19:10 --> 03:19:13
There's also a money component thing to it,

03:19:12 --> 03:19:16
which is to get the money to scale one of these things

03:19:15 --> 03:19:17
into the billions of dollars.

03:19:16 --> 03:19:19
There's basically right now only two sources of money

03:19:18 --> 03:19:20
that will do that for you.

03:19:19 --> 03:19:23
One is the hyperscalers giving you the money,

03:19:22 --> 03:19:25
which you turn around and roundtrip back to them.

03:19:24 --> 03:19:27
Or, you know, foreign sovereigns, other, you know,

03:19:26 --> 03:19:30
country sovereign wealth funds, which can be,

03:19:29 --> 03:19:33
you know, difficult in some cases, for companies to access.

03:19:33 --> 03:19:37
So there's maybe another trillion-dollar question

03:19:36 --> 03:19:39
is the financing question.

03:19:38 --> 03:19:39
Here's one.

03:19:38 --> 03:19:41
So Sam Altman has been public about the fact

03:19:40 --> 03:19:42
hat he wants to transition OpenAI

03:19:41 --> 03:19:44
from being a nonprofit, being a for-profit.

03:19:44 --> 03:19:47
The way that is legally done

03:19:46 --> 03:19:49
is that, and there is a way to do it.

03:19:48 --> 03:19:51
There is a way in US law to do it.

03:19:50 --> 03:19:53
The IRS and and other legal entities,

03:19:52 --> 03:19:55
government entities, scrutinizes very carefully,

03:19:54 --> 03:19:58
'cause the US takes foundation nonprofit law very seriously

03:19:57 --> 03:19:59
because of the tax exemption.

03:20:00 --> 03:20:02
And so the way that historically,

03:20:01 --> 03:20:04
the way that you do it is you start a for-profit

03:20:03 --> 03:20:06
and then you raise money with the for-profit

03:20:05 --> 03:20:09
o buy the assets of the nonprofit at fair market value.

03:20:09 --> 03:20:14
And, you know, the last financing round at OpenAI was,

03:20:13 --> 03:20:15
you know, 150 some billion dollars.

03:20:14 --> 03:20:19
And so logically, if the flip is going to happen,

03:20:18 --> 03:20:21
the for-profit has to go raise $150 billion

03:20:20 --> 03:20:24
out of the chute to buy the assets.

03:20:23 --> 03:20:26
You know, raising 150 billion is a challenge.

03:20:27 --> 03:20:29
So, you know, is that even possible?

03:20:28 --> 03:20:32
If that is possible, then OpenAI maybe is off to the races

03:20:31 --> 03:20:33
as a for-profit company.

03:20:32 --> 03:20:35
If not, you know, I don't know.

03:20:34 --> 03:20:38
And then, you know, obviously the Elon lawsuit.

03:20:37 --> 03:20:39
So just because they're the market leader today, you know,

03:20:38 --> 03:20:42
there's big important questions there.

03:20:41 --> 03:20:44
You know, Microsoft has this kind of love-hate relationship

03:20:43 --> 03:20:44
with them.

03:20:43 --> 03:20:45
Where does that go?

03:20:44 --> 03:20:47
Apple's, you know, lagging badly behind,

03:20:46 --> 03:20:49
but you know, they're very good at catching up.

03:20:48 --> 03:20:51
Amazon, you know, is primarily hyperscaler,

03:20:50 --> 03:20:53
but they now have their own models.

03:20:52 --> 03:20:54
- And then there's the other questions,

03:20:53 --> 03:20:55
like you laid out brilliantly,

03:20:54 --> 03:20:59
briefly and brilliantly, open versus closed,

03:20:58 --> 03:21:01
big versus little models, synthetic data.

03:21:00 --> 03:21:03
That's a huge, huge question.

03:21:02 --> 03:21:06
And then test on compute with chain of thought.

03:21:06 --> 03:21:07
They're all of that.

03:21:06 --> 03:21:09
And it's just fascinating.

03:21:08 --> 03:21:10
And these are,

03:21:09 --> 03:21:12
I think it's fair to say, trillion-dollar questions.

03:21:11 --> 03:21:14
- Yeah, these are big, like look, you know, it's like,

03:21:13 --> 03:21:15
okay, here's a trillion-dollar question,

03:21:14 --> 03:21:16
which is kind of embedded in that,

03:21:15 --> 03:21:17
which is just hallucinations, right?

03:21:16 --> 03:21:21
Like, so if you are trying to use these tools creatively,

03:21:20 --> 03:21:23
you're thrilled because they can draw new images

03:21:22 --> 03:21:24
and they can make new music

03:21:23 --> 03:21:27
and they can do all this incredible stuff, right?

03:21:26 --> 03:21:28
They're creative.

03:21:27 --> 03:21:30
The flip side of that is if you need them to be correct,

03:21:29 --> 03:21:31
they can't be creative.

03:21:30 --> 03:21:33
And that's, you know, the term hallucination.

03:21:32 --> 03:21:35
And these things do hallucinate.

03:21:34 --> 03:21:39
And you know, there have been, you know, court cases already

03:21:38 --> 03:21:40
where lawyers have submitted legal briefs

03:21:39 --> 03:21:43
that contain made-up court citations, case citations.

03:21:42 --> 03:21:45
The judge is like, "Wait a minute, this doesn't exist."

03:21:44 --> 03:21:48
And the very next is, "Did you write this yourself?"

03:21:47 --> 03:21:50
And the lawyer goes, "Er-" (laughs)

03:21:49 --> 03:21:52
- I mean that's why with Elon,

03:21:51 --> 03:21:53
with Grok- - Yes.

03:21:52 --> 03:21:54
- Looking for truth.

03:21:53 --> 03:21:57
I mean, that's an open technical question.

03:21:56 --> 03:21:59
How close can you get to truth with LLMs?

03:21:58 --> 03:22:01
- Yeah, that's right.

03:22:00 --> 03:22:04
And my sense is this very contentious topic at the industry,

03:22:03 --> 03:22:07
my sense is if to the extent

03:22:06 --> 03:22:09
hat there is a domain in which there is a definitive

03:22:08 --> 03:22:11
and checkable provable answer,

03:22:10 --> 03:22:12
and you might say, math satisfies that,

03:22:11 --> 03:22:13
coding satisfies that,

03:22:12 --> 03:22:15
and maybe some other fields,

03:22:14 --> 03:22:18
then you should be able to generate synthetic data.

03:22:17 --> 03:22:19
You should be able to do chain of thought reasoning.

03:22:18 --> 03:22:20
You should be able to do reinforcement learning,

03:22:19 --> 03:22:22
and you should be able to ultimately, you know,

03:22:21 --> 03:22:23
eliminate hallucinations.

03:22:24 --> 03:22:26
But by the way,

03:22:25 --> 03:22:27
that's a trillion-dollar question right there

03:22:26 --> 03:22:29
as to whether that's true.

03:22:28 --> 03:22:30
But then there's question of like, okay,

03:22:29 --> 03:22:32
is that gonna work in the more general domain?

03:22:31 --> 03:22:35
Like, so for example, one possibility is these things

03:22:34 --> 03:22:37
are gonna get truly superhuman like math and coding.

03:22:36 --> 03:22:41
But at like discussing philosophy, they're gonna just,

03:22:40 --> 03:22:43
they're basically as smart as they're ever gonna be.

03:22:42 --> 03:22:45
And they're gonna be kind of, you know,

03:22:44 --> 03:22:47
say mid-wit grad student level.

03:22:46 --> 03:22:48
And the theory there would just be

03:22:47 --> 03:22:49
they're already outta training data.

03:22:48 --> 03:22:52
Like they literally, if, you know,

03:22:51 --> 03:22:53
you talk to these people, like literally the big models,

03:22:52 --> 03:22:55
the big models are like within a factor of 2X

03:22:54 --> 03:22:57
of consuming all the human-generated training data

03:22:57 --> 03:22:58
to the point that some

03:22:57 --> 03:23:00
of these big companies are literally hiring people

03:22:59 --> 03:23:01
like doctors and lawyers to sit

03:23:00 --> 03:23:03
and write new training data by hand.

03:23:02 --> 03:23:05
And so does this mean that like you have to,

03:23:04 --> 03:23:06
if you want your model to better philosophy,

03:23:05 --> 03:23:08
you have to go hire like a thousand philosophers

03:23:07 --> 03:23:09
and have them write new content,

03:23:08 --> 03:23:11
and is anybody gonna do that?

03:23:10 --> 03:23:12
And so, you know,

03:23:11 --> 03:23:14
maybe these things are topping out in certain ways

03:23:13 --> 03:23:16
and they're gonna leap way ahead in other ways.

03:23:15 --> 03:23:20
And so anyway, so we just don't, you know, this is...

03:23:19 --> 03:23:22
Actually, maybe my main main conclusion is,

03:23:21 --> 03:23:23
anybody telling, you know,

03:23:22 --> 03:23:26
anybody telling you these big sweeping conclusions,

03:23:25 --> 03:23:27
you know, this whole super, you know,

03:23:26 --> 03:23:29
all of these abstract generalized super intelligence

03:23:28 --> 03:23:30
AGI stuff like,

03:23:29 --> 03:23:32
you know, maybe it's the engineer in me,

03:23:31 --> 03:23:33
but like, no, like,

03:23:34 --> 03:23:38
that's to abstract like, it's gotta actually work.

03:23:39 --> 03:23:41
And then by the way,

03:23:40 --> 03:23:42
it has to actually have to be able to pay for it.

03:23:42 --> 03:23:45
I mean, this is a problem right now with the, you know,

03:23:44 --> 03:23:47
the big models that are like really good at coding and math,

03:23:46 --> 03:23:50
they're like actually very expensive to run, you know,

03:23:49 --> 03:23:50
they're quite slow.

03:23:51 --> 03:23:54
Another trillion-dollar question, future chips,

03:23:53 --> 03:23:56
which I know you've talked a lot about.

03:23:55 --> 03:23:58
Another trillion-dollar question,

03:23:57 --> 03:24:00
yeah, I mean, all the global issue.

03:23:59 --> 03:24:04
Oh, another trillion-dollar question censorship, right?

03:24:03 --> 03:24:08
Like, and all the, as they say,

03:24:07 --> 03:24:10
all the human feedback training process.

03:24:10 --> 03:24:12
Exactly, what are you training these things to do?

03:24:11 --> 03:24:14
What are they allowed to talk about?

03:24:14 --> 03:24:17
How long did they give you these...

03:24:16 --> 03:24:18
How often do they give you

03:24:17 --> 03:24:20
these incredibly preaching moral lectures?

03:24:21 --> 03:24:23
Here's a trillion-dollar question.

03:24:22 --> 03:24:26
How many other countries want their country

03:24:25 --> 03:24:29
to run its education system, healthcare system, new system,

03:24:28 --> 03:24:31
political system, on the basis of an AI

03:24:30 --> 03:24:32
that's been trained according

03:24:31 --> 03:24:37
to the most extreme left-wing California politics, right?

03:24:36 --> 03:24:39
'Cause that's kind of what they have on offer right now.

03:24:38 --> 03:24:41
And I think the answer to that is not very many.

03:24:40 --> 03:24:45
So there's like massive open questions there about

03:24:44 --> 03:24:47
like what, you know, and by the way, like what morality

03:24:46 --> 03:24:49
of these things are gonna get trained on as a-

03:24:48 --> 03:24:52
And that one we're cracking wide open

03:24:51 --> 03:24:56
with what's been happening over the past few months.

03:24:55 --> 03:24:59
Censorship on every level of these companies,

03:24:58 --> 03:25:02
and just the very idea what truth means

03:25:01 --> 03:25:05
and what it means to be expand the Overton window of LLMs

03:25:04 --> 03:25:09
or the Overton window of human discourse.

03:25:08 --> 03:25:10
- So what I experienced, you know, going back

03:25:09 --> 03:25:12
to how we started, what I experienced was, all right,

03:25:11 --> 03:25:14
social media censorship regime from hell,

03:25:14 --> 03:25:18
debanking at like large scale,

03:25:17 --> 03:25:21
and then the war on the crypto industry, trying to kill it.

03:25:20 --> 03:25:23
And then basically declared intent to do the same thing

03:25:22 --> 03:25:27
to AI and to put AI under the same kind of censorship

03:25:26 --> 03:25:30
and control regime as social media and the banks.

03:25:29 --> 03:25:33
And I think this election tipped, in America,

03:25:32 --> 03:25:35
I think this election tipped us from a timeline

03:25:34 --> 03:25:38
in which things were going to get really bad on that front

03:25:37 --> 03:25:39
o a timeline in which I think things

03:25:38 --> 03:25:41
are gonna be quite good.

03:25:40 --> 03:25:43
But look, those same questions also apply outside the US

03:25:42 --> 03:25:46
and you know, the EU is doing their thing,

03:25:45 --> 03:25:47
they're being extremely draconian

03:25:46 --> 03:25:49
d they're trying to lock in a political censorship regime

03:25:48 --> 03:25:51
on AI right now that's so harsh

03:25:50 --> 03:25:53
that even American AI companies are not even willing

03:25:52 --> 03:25:56
to launch new products in the EU right now.

03:25:55 --> 03:25:57
Like, that's not gonna last,

03:25:56 --> 03:25:59
but like, what happens there, right?

03:25:58 --> 03:26:00
And what are the trade-offs?

03:25:59 --> 03:26:02
You know, what levels of censorship

03:26:01 --> 03:26:03
are American companies gonna have to sign up for

03:26:03 --> 03:26:05
if they wanna operate in the EU?

03:26:04 --> 03:26:06
Or is the EU still capable

03:26:05 --> 03:26:09
of generating its own AI companies

03:26:08 --> 03:26:13
or have we brain drained them, (laughs) so that they can't?

03:26:12 --> 03:26:15
So big questions.

03:26:15 --> 03:26:17
- Quick questions.

03:26:16 --> 03:26:19
So you're very active on X.

03:26:18 --> 03:26:21
A very unique character,

03:26:22 --> 03:26:26
flamboyant, exciting, bold.

03:26:26 --> 03:26:29
You post a lot.

03:26:28 --> 03:26:31
I think there's a meme, I don't remember it exactly,

03:26:30 --> 03:26:35
but that Elon posted something like inside Elon,

03:26:34 --> 03:26:36
there are two wolves.

03:26:35 --> 03:26:39
One is please be kind or more positive.

03:26:38 --> 03:26:43
And the other one is, I think, you know, doing the,

03:26:43 --> 03:26:47
I take a big step back and fuck yourself in the face guy.

03:26:47 --> 03:26:52
How many wolves are inside your mind when you're tweeting?

03:26:51 --> 03:26:53
- To be clear,

03:26:52 --> 03:26:55
a reference from the comedy classic "Tropic Thunder."

03:26:54 --> 03:26:56
- "Tropic Thunder," yeah.

03:26:55 --> 03:26:57
Legendary movie.

- Yes. (Lex laughing)

Any Zoomers listening to this who haven't seen

that movie, go watch it immediately.

03:27:04 --> 03:27:07

03:27:10 --> 03:27:14
(Lex laughing)

So- - Yeah.

03:27:15 --> 03:27:18
- Yeah, no, look, I should start by saying like,

03:27:17 --> 03:27:20
I'm not supposed to be tweeting at all, so-

03:27:19 --> 03:27:21
Yeah. - Yes, yes, yes.

03:27:20 --> 03:27:23
So, but, you know.

03:27:22 --> 03:27:25
- So how do you approach that?

03:27:24 --> 03:27:26
Like how do you approach what to tweet?

03:27:25 --> 03:27:31
- I mean, I don't.

03:27:30 --> 03:27:32
I don't well enough.

03:27:31 --> 03:27:35
It's mostly an exercise in frustration.

03:27:34 --> 03:27:36
Look, there's a glory to it

03:27:35 --> 03:27:38
and there's an issue with it, and the glory of it is like,

03:27:37 --> 03:27:41
you know, instantaneous global communication, you know,

03:27:40 --> 03:27:43
X in particular is, you know,

03:27:42 --> 03:27:45
the town square on all these, you know, social issues,

03:27:44 --> 03:27:47
political issues, everything else, current events.

03:27:46 --> 03:27:49
But I mean, look, there's no question of the format.

03:27:48 --> 03:27:51
The format of at least the original tweet is,

03:27:50 --> 03:27:53
you know, prone to be inflammatory.

03:27:52 --> 03:27:55
You know, I'm the guy who at one point,

03:27:54 --> 03:27:58
the entire nation of India hated me

03:27:57 --> 03:27:59
because I was tweeted something.

03:27:58 --> 03:28:01
It turned out that it's still politically sensitive

03:28:00 --> 03:28:02
in the entire continent.

03:28:01 --> 03:28:04
I stayed up all night that night

03:28:03 --> 03:28:06
as I became front page headline

03:28:05 --> 03:28:09
and leading television news in each time zone in India

03:28:08 --> 03:28:10
for a single tweet.

03:28:09 --> 03:28:12
So like, the single tweet outta context

03:28:11 --> 03:28:13
is a very dangerous thing.

03:28:13 --> 03:28:18
Obviously, X now has the middle ground where they,

03:28:17 --> 03:28:21
you know, they now have the longer form essays.

03:28:20 --> 03:28:22
And so, you know, probably the most productive thing

03:28:21 --> 03:28:26
I can do is longer form things.

03:28:26 --> 03:28:28
- You're not gonna do it though, are you?

03:28:27 --> 03:28:30
- [Marc] I do, I do, from time-to-time. I do.

03:28:29 --> 03:28:31
- Sometimes. - I should do more of them.

03:28:30 --> 03:28:33
And then, yeah, I mean, look, but, and yeah,

03:28:32 --> 03:28:35
and obviously, X is doing great.

03:28:34 --> 03:28:36
And then, like I said, like Substack, you know,

03:28:35 --> 03:28:39
has become the center for a lot, you know, a lot of the,

03:28:38 --> 03:28:41
I think the best kind of, you know, deeply thought

03:28:40 --> 03:28:45
hrough, you know, certainly intellectual content, you know,

03:28:44 --> 03:28:47
tons of current events stuff there as well.

03:28:46 --> 03:28:49
And then, yeah, so,

03:28:48 --> 03:28:50
and then there's a bunch of other, you know,

03:28:49 --> 03:28:51
a bunch of new systems that are very exciting.

03:28:50 --> 03:28:53
So I think one of the things we can look forward

03:28:52 --> 03:28:54
to in the next four years is number one,

03:28:53 --> 03:28:57
just like a massive reinvigoration of social media

03:28:56 --> 03:28:58
s a consequence of the changes

03:28:57 --> 03:28:59
that are happening right now.

03:28:58 --> 03:29:01
I'm very excited

03:29:00 --> 03:29:03
to see what's gonna happen with that.

03:29:02 --> 03:29:04
And it's happened on X,

03:29:03 --> 03:29:06
but it's now gonna happen on other platforms.

03:29:05 --> 03:29:08
And then the other

03:29:07 --> 03:29:11
is crypto's gonna come right back to life.

03:29:10 --> 03:29:13
And actually that's very exciting, actually,

03:29:12 --> 03:29:14
that's worth noting

03:29:13 --> 03:29:16
is that's another trillion-dollar question on AI,

03:29:15 --> 03:29:19
which is in a world of pervasive AI,

03:29:18 --> 03:29:21
and especially in a world of AI agents,

03:29:20 --> 03:29:22
and imagine a world of billions

03:29:21 --> 03:29:24
or trillions of AI agents running around,

03:29:23 --> 03:29:25
they need an economy.

03:29:25 --> 03:29:28
And crypto, in our view,

03:29:27 --> 03:29:30
happens to be the ideal economic system for that, right?

03:29:29 --> 03:29:32
'Cause it's a programmable money,

03:29:31 --> 03:29:34
it's a very easy way to plug in and do that.

03:29:33 --> 03:29:36
And there's this transaction processing system

03:29:35 --> 03:29:36
that can do that.

03:29:35 --> 03:29:39
And so I think the crypto AI intersection, you know,

03:29:38 --> 03:29:41
is potentially a very, very big deal.

03:29:41 --> 03:29:46
And so that was gonna be impossible under the prior regime,

03:29:45 --> 03:29:47
and I think under the new regime,

03:29:46 --> 03:29:49
hopefully, it'll be something we can do.

03:29:48 --> 03:29:50
- Almost for fun.
